Hyundai’s electric SUV finds some footing, with IONIQ 5 sales topping 33,000 in 2026

📅 Nov 03, 2026  |  Category: Business  |  Read Source →

The Hyundai IONIQ 5 remains one of America’s most popular EVs outside Tesla, with October sales up 21%.

While the Tesla Model Y and Model 3 still dominate the US electric vehicle market, accounting for about 50% of all sales through the third quarter, it was the Hyundai IONIQ 5 that ranked third.

The market has slowed since the $7,500 federal tax credit was removed last September, with several automakers reporting fewer EV sales than in 2025 when buyers rushed to claim the credit.

Hyundai responded by cutting prices on the 2026 IONIQ 5 by up to nearly $10,000 on some trims, with a new starting MSRP of around $35,000.

As the market shows signs of recovery, IONIQ 5 sales bounced back in October, rising 21% to 1,981 units.

Through the first 10 months of 2026, Hyundai sold 33,093 IONIQ 5s in the US, still down 23% from the more than 42,700 it sold during the same period last year, when credit was still available.

Hyundai said the IONIQ 5 helped drive growth as the company posted its best October in the US ever with 73,452 vehicles sold, a 5% increase YOY.

While Hyundai sold only 169 IONIQ 9s last month (-47%), sales are still up 49% through October, with nearly 6,700 units sold.

Hyundai’s hybrids set a new October sales record, rising 20% from last year and accounting for 29% of total sales in October. Through October, Hyundai’s total US sales are up 3% YOY at 770,916 units.

Since not all automakers report monthly sales figures, it’s hard to determine where the IONIQ 5 stands through October.

Earlier today, Hyundai’s sister brand Kia reported that sales of its three-row electric SUV, the EV9, were up 11% year over year in October. EV9 sales are now up 5% year-to-date, with 13,835 units sold through October.

The cheapest 2027 Hyundai IONIQ 5 SE Standard Range starts at $35,250 and offers up to 245 miles of range.

If you want more range, upgrading to the extended-range SE RWD model costs an extra $2,500, but you get up to 318 miles of range on a single charge.

Ready to take a closer look? You can use the links below to find available Hyundai IONIQ 5 and IONIQ 9 models near you, along with any current offers.

Dan James has surely just saved his Leeds career - verdict

📅 Nov 03, 2026  |  Category: Business  |  Read Source →

Senior Correspondent AUTHORITY Senior football journalist specialising in refereeing and officiating; former contributor to The Football League Paper, Late Tackle, and the Premier League; University of Derby graduate. FOCUS Refereeing controversies, football finance and governance, PGMOL decisions, and officiating analysis across the Premier League, EFL, and SPFL. THE INSIGHT Harri utilises a network of officiating and industry contacts to deliver verified, expert analysis of refereeing decisions and PGMOL policy. He provides the regulatory clarity behind the controversy to ensure fans get the full picture.

Dan James' future at Elland Road has been a huge talking point amongst Leeds United fans so far this season.

Of course, Daniel Farke's contract situation has been the most-discussed top, and there has yet to be any movement on that situation.

Despite his injury problems, James remains one of the manager's favourite players, even with continuous links to a move away.

Football Insider revealed last month that the Welshman could push for an exit in the January window, but Farke is unlikely to want to that to happen.

The manager showed his faith in his player for the Whites' away clash against Bournemouth on Saturday, 31 October.

Sadly, it ended in defeat, with goals from Evanilson and Amine Adli, meaning that Leeds are now without a win in five straight matches.

If James can reproduce his performances in upcoming games, though, Leeds might be able to turn that form around after the international break.

He may have only lasted 65 minutes before being replaced, but it was a great cameo for the 28-year-old at the Vitality Stadium.

James reached the joint-highest speed in the game, completed 100 per cent of his dribbles, the most of anyone, and made six line-breaking passes [FotMob].

Defensively, he was just as important, making four recoveries and two clearances, as well as diving into seven duels and committing two fouls.

It was exactly the kind of performance that Leeds fans will have wanted to see from the rarely-seen winger, especially with Noah Okafor struggling.

The Whites will be desperate to get back to winning ways against Tottenham Hotspur on Saturday, 7 November, especially at home.

From the Bournemouth game, there are several players who could be forced to sit out based on their performances.

James Trafford made mistakes, but there's no doubt that he'll bounce back. The same can't be said for Ao Tanaka, Gabriel Gudmundsson or Justin Bogle.

Brenden Aaronson, Lukas Nmecha and Sean Longstaff all offered very little off the bench, so maybe Farke doesn't have many changes that he can make.

Fannie Mae, Freddie Mac rally as Ackman presses for conservatorship exit

📅 2026-11-03  |  Category: Business  |  Read Source →

The midterm elections could be a turning point for the release of Fannie Mae and Freddie Mac from government control, according to billionaire investor Bill Ackman.

Investors may agree with him, if the rally in both stocks is any sign.

The Federal National Mortgage Association, known as Fannie Mae, and Federal Home Loan Mortgage Corporation, or Freddie Mac, have each jumped more than 60% from Oct. 14 to Monday's close. The stocks were both up roughly 3% on Tuesday.

Ackman, the founder of Pershing Square Capital Management, has long pushed for the two entities to be released from federal conservatorship.

"The reason why it's not being touched right now is, you know, midterms," he said in an interview with CNBC's "Squawk Box."

Ackman, who owns both names through Pershing Square, believes Freddie and Fannie have the potential to increase "10-fold from current share prices," he said in his quarterly letter to shareholders, issued on Monday.

Fannie and Freddie buy mortgages from banks and other lenders, and then package them into mortgage-backed securities, known as MBS. Their operations are critical to the housing market, providing liquidity to banks and creating a secondary market for MBS.

The entities were placed into conservatorship in September 2008, during the financial crisis. The stocks were delisted from the New York Stock Exchange in 2010 and have traded over the counter since then.

Ackman wants the conservatorship to end, arguing the entities would be worth multiples of their current valuation if they were freed from government control.

President Donald Trump has supported ending the conservatorship of Fannie and Freddie. But it remains politically and economically contentious.

The entities operate under strict capital threshold requirements and would need to be recapitalized, which could happen through issuing common equity. However, the Treasury Department would need to figure out an exit plan for its roughly $388 billion ownership stake in the entities through preferred securities and warrants.

Congress may also need to play a role, passing legislation to restructure the government's role in mortgage financing and home-loan guarantees.

Nonetheless, Treasury Secretary Scott Bessent recently said it could happen before Trump's term ends, telling 2Way's "The Morning Meeting" podcast that the administration will tackle the ownership structure "next year, the year after." He said affordability will be guiding the decision.

One point of controversy is whether ending the conservatorship of Fannie and Freddie would increase the spreads on MBS over Treasuries, effectively making mortgages costlier. Bessent has sought to reassure the market that wouldn't happen.

"Whatever we do is going to be guided by making sure that the spread on [agency] mortgages … over the Treasury does not increase," Bessent said.

Ackman argues that privatizing the GSEs would help insulate them from political meddling. "Fannie and Freddie are these massive, enormously profitable institutions where whichever political party controls them…if they're still in conservatorship, can influence the outcome of elections," he told CNBC.

In one scenario, he explained, Democrats win the White House, conservatorship is still in place, and Democrats push Fannie and Freddie to lower credit standards in order to expand home ownership and affordability.

"If they're going to move more aggressively into borrowers with lower credit quality, for example, to achieve a political objective, that's going to have an impact on trillions of dollars outstanding mortgage securities," Ackman said. "It's going to have an impact on overall credit spreads."

MBS investors want certainty about how the entities will be run, he noted.

"That only gets resolved in a world in which they exit conservatorship, they become publicly traded companies that are owned by institutional and retail investors," Ackman said. "We set the rules. In conservatorship, the president can do anything."

Chad Lowe Lists $3.2 Million Los Angeles Mansion—1 Month After Losing His 13-Year-Old Daughter to Suicide

📅 Nov 03, 2026  |  Category: Business  |  Read Source →

Actor Chad Lowe and his producer wife, Kim Painter, have put their sprawling Los Angeles home on the market for $3.2 million—just over a month after their 13-year-old daughter, Fiona, died.

Lowe, 58, and Painter, 52, listed the four-bedroom dwelling on Oct. 16, just weeks after announcing the devastating news of their daughter's passing, as first reported by the TMZ.

Now, not only have they put the pad on the selling block, they have also accepted an offer of $3.24 million.

However, the offer is contingent on certain conditions being met.

The home comes complete with three bathrooms and sprawls across 3,239 square feet.

Lowe and Painter purchased the Spanish-inspired abode in March 2025 for $3.15 million.

Fiona's death was announced via a statement from the family shared to People on Sept. 29 that read, “We are deeply heartbroken by the loss of our beloved Fiona. She was adored by her sisters and she was deeply loved by her parents,” the family said, adding, “In fact, she was the light of our lives.”

“Her passing is devastating for our entire family and we ask for prayers and privacy at this time. If you or someone you know is struggling with mental health, please reach out to the 988 Suicide & Crisis Lifeline by calling or texting 988.”

Fiona was the second of their three daughters. They also share daughters Mabel Painter, 17, and Nixie Barbara, 10.

Mabel honored her sister in a heartbreaking social media post following her death, penning, “I love you so much, Fiona. I wish your short time here was easier and I hope your mind is at ease now. I will honor you forever and keep your memory alive. I love you, I hope you knew just how much.”

On Oct. 2, Fiona's cause of death was determined to be a suicide.

After her death, a family friend told the Daily Mail that Fiona had been devastated by the loss of their California home due to the Palisades fire last year.

Mykesha Robinson told the outlet, "Something happened when the fires happened," adding, that the family “lost everything.”

JetBlue raises bag fees for third time in a year as fuel costs bite

📅 2026-11-03  |  Category: Business  |  Read Source →

JetBlue Airways has raised the price of checking a bag for many customers for the third time in less than a year as high fuel costs continue to eat into the industry's profits.

Checking a first bag for travelers booked in economy will now be $50, up from $45, if purchased more than 24 hours before departure during a non-peak travel period. If it's paid within 24 hours of departure, the fee will be $60. During a peak period, such as during the holidays or the summer, it will be $55, up from $49, and $70 for within 24 hours of departure.

There are exceptions to the checked bag fees, such as for customers who hold a co-branded JetBlue credit card or hold elite frequent flyer status.

"We're adjusting checked bag fees to help offset rising fuel and operating costs," JetBlue said in a statement. "This helps us keep our base fares competitive while giving customers the choice to pay for a checked bag only if they need one."

The price of jet fuel, airlines' biggest cost after labor, has roughly doubled this year since the start of the Iran war in February, costing airlines billions and driving up the price of tickets. Jet fuel was going for $4.44 a gallon on Tuesday for the U.S. Gulf of Mexico, according to the U.S. Energy Information Administration.

Other airlines often follow when one carrier raises fees. Major U.S. airlines did so earlier this year after JetBlue raised its bag fees.

The changes were reported earlier by the Thrifty Traveler blog.

San Diego Accountant Tricked Investors Into Sinking $8.2 Million Into Phony ADU Venture

📅 Nov 03, 2026  |  Category: Business  |  Read Source →

A California man convinced dozens of investors to pour millions of dollars into a real estate venture he claimed would deliver big returns through the construction of backyard homes.

But the 30-year-old accountant lost more than $8.2 million of their money in risky stock market trades and not a single accessory dwelling unit (ADU) was built, prosecutors said.

Ethan Joseph Sanchez, of San Diego, was sentenced to 63 months in federal prison after admitting to orchestrating the investment fraud scheme, the U.S. Attorney's Office for the Southern District of California announced Friday.

“Ethan Sanchez exploited the trust of hardworking people and stole their savings through lies and fabricated investments,” U.S. Attorney Adam Gordon said in a statement. “This sentence reflects the profound harm he caused.”

Sanchez, who was a certified public accountant at the time and operated through his business, BSC Equity Ventures LLC., repeatedly lied to his victims as to how their investment funds would be used, according to prosecutors.

The scheme began back in 2022 when Sanchez allegedly began soliciting loans from investors, falsely claiming the money would fund real estate ventures, including the construction and renovation of ADUs.

He promised the investors they would receive their principal back as soon as possible, with an above-average return of interest, in some cases as much as 25%.

Sanchez also allegedly used a fictitious identity, Richard “Rick” Aguilar, to make the investment opportunities appear more legitimate.

But prosecutors say Sanchez used the investors’ funds for day trading and in most cases lost the money. He did not fund the real estate transactions he said the investors were financing nor did he build any of the ADUs he said would be financed with the investors’ money.

The FBI reported in February 2025 that BSC Equity Ventures had received over $6 million in investments from its investors, while Sanchez returned less than $1.8 million to them.

At the sentencing hearing on Oct. 30, U.S. District Judge Robert S. Huie ordered Sanchez to forfeit over $8.2 million and to pay restitution to victims.

He described the scheme as “aggravated and reprehensible,” highlighting how Sanchez had used existing victims to recruit additional investors, sometimes including members of their own families.

The judge described the harm caused by the scheme as “almost unimaginable,” adding that the consequences went far beyond the financial losses.

In a January 2026 interview with ABC 10News, one investor, identified only as Brooke, said the loss he suffered forced him out of retirement and back to work to rebuild his savings.

Brooke told the outlet that Sanchez told him he needed money to refinance his portfolio of investment properties and would pay interest on the loans. Sanchez initially borrowed $40,000 from Brooke in December 2023, but then continued to borrow more money, including a $150,000 loan.

Sanchez made small payments, Brooke said, but never repaid the full amount of his investment. Brooke also said that he had borrowed against his home to make the investment with Sanchez and was paying interest on that money.

“It was always lie after lie,” he said. “And then it became so obvious that this was a Ponzi scheme.”

Range Rover Replacing Evoque and Velar Models with Upcoming GT EV

📅 Nov 03, 2026  |  Category: Business  |  Read Source →

It’s unclear if either SUV will have a 2027 model year run in the U.S. market.

Prepare to say goodbye to Range Rover’s two most affordable models, as the Evoque and Velar celebrate their final model year in production. The company has confirmed to British outlet Autocar that the volume sellers will cease production after 2027 and be replaced by the new Range Rover GT.

“Both Range Rover Velar and Range Rover Evoque will continue to be sold in selected global markets where demand remains strong, throughout 2027,” a JLR spokesperson told Car and Driver. “Production of both models will end in mid-2027 in line with their natural product lifecycles, as JLR’s manufacturing facilities transition to build its next generation of vehicles.”

See all results for Land Rover Range Rover Evoque for sale near 55433

The Evoque and Velar’s sales have accounted for just 6000 of Land Rover’s U.S. sales through this year. Paltry numbers, representing only 10 percent of the brand’s 2026 sales volume in the U.S. so far. It would not surprise us if North America is not one of the global markets the company considers a high-demand market for these two models. We asked for clarification from JLR but have not heard back.

The two SUVs are attractive not only for their styling but also for their relatively low prices compared with the six-figure ask for the flagship Range Rover SUV. The Evoque starts at around $52,000 and the Velar at around $64,000. Both ride on the same platform as the Land Rover Discovery Sport SUV, which was discontinued after the 2026 model year.

The move comes amid a broader strategy shift at JLR, Jaguar and Land Rover’s parent company. In the past year, both brands have focused on launching electric-powered flagships, the most buzzworthy of which is the Jaguar Type 01 sedan.

Range Rover has launched the Range Rover Electric and is preparing a Range Rover Sport Electric, effectively electrifying its core lineup. A new entry-level Range Rover GT is also on the way and will effectively replace the Evoque and Velar once it hits production as a 2028 model.

Like the Jaguar Type 01, the Range Rover GT takes the shape of a long and low sedan-like vehicle with a minimalist interior and an all-wheel-drive electric powertrain. The Range Rover GT will launch first as an EV, but the company says the platform is capable of hosting gas and hybrid powertrains as well. A starting price of around $90,000 is expected, significantly increasing the entry price to the Range Rover brand.

See all results for Land Rover Range Rover Evoque for sale near 55433

Land Rover’s other two brands—Defender and Discovery—are expected to pick up the slack and serve customers in the sub-$90,000 price range once the Evoque and Velar cease production. A Defender Sport model is reportedly in the works as a junior entry to the popular Defender off-roader. It stands to reason that a Discovery Sport model could be revived in the future, possibly as an EV, to serve as an entry point to the Discovery lineup.

Eaton Sales Up 28%; GAAP EPS Rises Just 7%: Rapid Earnings Reaction

📅 Nov 03, 2026  |  Category: Business  |  Read Source →

Eaton's third quarter paired record demand with a widening gap between adjusted and GAAP profit. Here's what's eating into earnings and what to watch next.

Eaton (ETN -1.05%) reported third-quarter sales of $8.9 billion, up 28%, and shares rose about 1.8% in Tuesday trading. Organic sales grew 19%, orders and backlog hit records, and management raised its organic growth guidance. But GAAP earnings per share rose only 7%, to $2.77. Acquisition debt and amortization absorbed most of the extra operating profit.

Debt is the main drag. Net interest expense nearly tripled, to $198 million, as borrowings roughly doubled alongside the Boyd Thermal and Ultra PCS purchases.

Amortization is the second drag. Acquired intangible assets are expensed over time, and that charge more than doubled. Adjusted EPS excludes both charges, so it grew far faster than GAAP. Full-year GAAP EPS guidance is roughly flat with 2025, while adjusted guidance implies double-digit growth.

Orders are the real signal. Twelve-month rolling orders hit record dollars in all three segments, and Electrical backlog grew 46%.

Watch whether fourth-quarter organic growth lands in the guided 13% to 15% range, down from 19% this quarter. Also track net interest expense, which sets how much operating profit reaches GAAP earnings.

Randy Coon has no position in any of the stocks mentioned. The Motley Fool has positions in and recommends Eaton Plc. The Motley Fool has a disclosure policy.

Why Amazon Stock Can Keep Rising Through 2035

📅 Nov 03, 2026  |  Category: Business  |  Read Source →

Amazon's cloud business gets the spotlight, but improving e-commerce margins is an overlooked opportunity.

Most of the attention on Amazon (AMZN +2.20%) goes to its cloud business, Amazon Web Services (AWS). But the company's online stores are quietly becoming more profitable, thanks to automation and robotics. That's a big reason the stock can keep rising through 2035.

On the third-quarter earnings call, CEO Andy Jassy said, "There has never been a business with a trillion dollars of annual revenue. Amazon is on a path to have two of them in AWS and stores." He added that some folks are "sleeping a bit" on how much progress the retail business has made.

That progress shows up in profit margins. Jassy said Amazon's North America and International segments have each expanded operating margins by nearly 10 percentage points over the past few years. The company is also on track to lower its cost to serve each unit, or deliver each item, for a fourth straight year.

Robotics is a big driver. Amazon has more than 1 million robots in its fulfillment network, and its newest facilities, including same-day delivery sites, are "very heavy on robotics." In the third quarter, shipping costs, excluding fuel and long-haul inflation, grew about four percentage points slower than units shipped. Jassy said the economic impact on its business so far is "small compared to what it is going to be in the next five years."

Lower costs on a sales base this large, with Amazon's online store generating $286 billion in annual revenue, can free up a lot more cash. Over the past 12 months, operating cash flow, the cash the business brings in before investments, grew 29% year over year to $168 billion. It has compounded about 25% a year over the past five years, and with Amazon's automation push still early, that cash flow has room to keep growing for years. And over time, Amazon's stock price tends to follow cash flow.

Yet the stock trades at about 16 times operating cash flow, based on the Oct. 29 close. That's near the lowest multiple Amazon stock has traded since the 2008 bear market -- a discount for a business whose cash flow is still growing rapidly and has room to expand.

The risk is heavy spending. Amazon expects capital expenditures of about $220 billion in 2026, mostly for Amazon Web Services, and management expects to spend "meaningfully more" in 2027. High capital spending has pushed free cash flow (operating cash flow minus capital expenditures) negative over the past year.

If those investments don't earn strong returns, free cash flow could stay low and weigh on the stock. Still, with retail becoming a second profit engine and the stock trading at a discount, Amazon shares could deliver excellent returns over the next 10 years.

John Ballard has positions in Amazon. The Motley Fool has positions in and recommends Amazon. The Motley Fool has a disclosure policy.

Rising temperatures may have a dangerous effect on mental health

📅 Nov 03, 2026  |  Category: Business  |  Read Source →

New research reveals how extreme heat may increase suicide risk by disrupting sleep and emotional regulation, highlighting a hidden mental health threat.

Extreme heat can do more than exhaust the body. It may also disrupt sleep, weaken emotional control, and increase vulnerability to serious psychological distress.

A new study has identified temperatures at which extreme heat is associated with increased psychological distress, revealing how disrupted sleep and emotional regulation may contribute to suicide risk.

The research was led by scientists from Georgetown University and the World Bank. It draws on a nationally representative panel study of nearly 7,500 adults in Bangladesh.

The researchers found that extreme heat was associated with an increased risk of suicide-related thoughts, with disrupted sleep playing a surprisingly important role.

“Rising global temperatures threaten human mental health on a massive scale,” said lead author Syed Shabab Wahid, an assistant professor of global health at Georgetown.

“In climate-vulnerable nations such as Bangladesh, this heat stress can translate into severe psychological strain and elevated suicide risk.”

The study tracked two interconnected pathways between heat and suicidal cognition: disrupted sleep and emotional dysregulation.

The association between temperature and suicide-related thoughts became statistically significant at 28°C and was strongest at 35°C.

Temperatures this high are common in Bangladesh during the summer months.

At that upper threshold, nearly 30 percent of the total association between heat and suicide risk ran through poor sleep quality alone. Emotional dysregulation accounted for a smaller but still significant 1.8 percent.

Together, poor sleep and difficulties regulating emotions statistically accounted for roughly a third of the observed relationship between heat and suicide-related thoughts.

The study design is worth understanding, because it shaped what the researchers could and couldn’t conclude.

Participants were interviewed twice – once in winter (January–February 2024) and once at the height of summer (May–June 2024).

Suicide risk was measured using the Brief Suicide Cognitions Scale, a validated tool that captures the sense of unbearability, unlovability, and unsolvability that precedes suicidal behavior.

Heat exposure was operationalized as the number of days with maximum temperatures at or above 35°C in the two weeks prior to each interview.

The panel structure meant the same individuals answered both rounds, which allowed the team to compare the same person’s mental state across seasons.

Summer scores were meaningfully worse: average suicide cognition scores rose from 10.99 in winter to 12.96 in summer.

Sleep quality declined. Emotional regulation worsened. Both effects were consistent across age groups, though older adults and women showed the steepest deterioration.

“Imagine a smartphone with a drained battery, overheating processor, and critical apps crashing all at once. Sustained ambient heat acts on the human brain and body in the same way,” Wahid explained.

“The body cannot cool down at night, leading to broken rest and depleting mental reserves. When the emotional processor overheats without restorative recovery, coping mechanisms collapse under stress.”

The study found that risk was notably higher during summer among less educated and poorer populations.

These differences may reflect unequal access to cooling, greater exposure to outdoor heat, and housing conditions that make it harder to escape high temperatures.

Individuals with disabilities showed consistently elevated risk across seasons. Unemployed people reported higher suicide cognition scores than those in most other employment categories.

Urban residents were more susceptible than rural ones. This may reflect the urban heat island effect. Concrete and asphalt absorb and radiate heat in ways that push nighttime temperatures higher.

There’s a global dimension to these findings that goes beyond Bangladesh.

Previous meta-analyses have found that a 1°C increase in monthly mean temperature is associated with a 1.5 to 1.7 percent increase in suicidal outcomes.

Bangladesh sits in a region the IPCC projects will experience some of the worst heatwaves of the coming decades. But the mechanisms the study identified are not unique to South Asia.

“Rather than treating climate-related mental health crises as unpreventable events, public health systems can now focus on intermediate, modifiable targets,” Wahid said. Sleep is one of them.

“Health agencies can monitor heat waves alongside sleep quality disruption indicators to mobilize preventative interventions. Interventions centered on sleep preservation, household cooling access, and community-based emotional regulation training should serve as effective suicide prevention tools during heatwaves.”

The paper points toward green roofing, passive architecture, and low-cost cooling technologies as structural interventions with documented effects on indoor temperatures in Bangladesh.

A pilot study found that buildings with vegetation-covered green roofing maintained temperatures 3.5°C lower than bare rooftops.

Such reductions could help make indoor environments more comfortable during extreme heat, potentially protecting sleep and reducing heat-related stress.

The study has some important limitations.

First, it relies on ambient outdoor temperature rather than indoor measurements. That’s a known proxy problem in Bangladesh, where air conditioning is rare and indoor heat tracks outdoor heat closely.

Humidity data wasn’t available, meaning the full thermal stress experienced by respondents – particularly relevant in a country where wet-bulb temperatures can reach dangerous levels – is likely underestimated.

What the study offers is new evidence that poor sleep and difficulties regulating emotions may help explain the relationship between extreme heat and suicide-related thoughts.

These findings point toward practical ways to reduce the psychological toll of rising temperatures.

Protecting sleep, improving access to cooling, and supporting mental health could become increasingly important as extreme heat becomes more common.

The study is published in the journal Environmental Research.

Paris Jackson Wants Dad Michael's Estate to Fund Trust With $600 Million

📅 Nov 03, 2026  |  Category: Business  |  Read Source →

Paris Jackson says her dad's estate still hasn't fully funded the family trust ... and she wants a judge to order them to put her late father's money into the fund and distribute it as his will demands. The estate claims it can't fund the trust until issues with the IRS are resolved.

Michael Jackson's only daughter filed legal documents last week claiming the estate hasn't fully funded the trust ... even though MJ died back in 2009.

She says her father intended for his beneficiaries -- namely her, Prince and Bigi -- to begin receiving income from the trust when they turned 21 and principal distributions starting at age 30. Paris says there is at least $600 million in distributable cash available for the trust.

Prince is 29, Paris is 28 and Bigi is 24 ... but she says they still haven't been getting what they're entitled to receive.

Paris claims John Branca -- the executor of the estate -- is wielding his power over distributions from the estate to "deter them from checking his abuses" ... and just doling out "lesser amounts" to them.

She says the estate's officials told the court back in 2011 that they “are contemplating and do intend to file a separate Petition for Preliminary Distribution" ... but she says that never happened.

Paris says she's been fighting Branca and the estate for a year now ... and it's resulted in them allegedly retaliating against her -- including severely limiting the little money she has received.

She's asking the judge to force the estate to finally put the money in the trust and distribute it.

A rep for Michael's estate tells TMZ, "Ms. Jackson’s counsel is well aware that the Estate is prevented from fully funding the philanthropic giving and individual trusts for the beneficiaries until several legal hurdles have been removed, including, in particular, the ongoing IRS litigation where they sought $700 million in taxes and penalties from the Estate."

"The Estate was able to secure a ruling to significantly reduce that amount, but the matter has not been settled and could still change," the rep added. "Further, the claim that the executor has withheld or ‘cut' payments is a complete fabrication. In fact, more than $200 million has already been distributed for the benefit of Paris and the other beneficiaries. Michael Jackson’s will is clear."

The rep explained none of the kids will receive a distribution until they are in their 30s.

You may remember ... Paris previously ripped MJ's estate for the handling of his biopic -- placing the blame squarely on Branca when the estate had to fund costly reshoots because of confusion caused by a previous settlement agreement.

A lawyer for the estate, attorney Jonathan Steinsapir, responded, "Ms. Jackson and her attorneys are once again abusing the courts and the legal system by making a series of false allegations as part of a media campaign to distract from their legal setbacks and the inherent weakness of their case."

We've reached out to Branca ... so far, no word back.

Time to admit Newcastle United have made a huge mistake, no not that one

📅 Nov 03, 2026  |  Category: Business  |  Read Source →

The independent voice of Newcastle United since 1988

Get your daily update and weekly newsletter by signing up today!

Get your daily update and weekly newsletter by signing up today!

Is it time to admit that Newcastle United have made a huge mistake?

“Any man can make mistakes, but only an idiot persists in his error” – Marcus Tullius Cicero

After another shocker of a Newcastle United performance against Everton last night, there is a growing anger and frustration in the fanbase with the direction (or lack of) of the team on field.

Consistent underperformers, no clear strategy, and players proven not to be good enough are selected regardless, though to be fair, managing to be toothless up top as well as all over at the back is impressive.

The knives are sharpening for Matthias Jaissle and he’s looking to be all mouth and no trousers. But for me, appointing Jaissle, isn’t the biggest mistake we’ve made in the last year or so, that dubious honour goes to the appointment of Ross Wilson (pictured at the top with David Hopkinson).

You might think and say well Wilson actually had a pretty decent CV, on paper it looks good, he was involved at Southampton when they brough Van Dijk in, was at Rangers when they broke Celtic’s grip on the SPL and was at Forest when they stayed up and then qualified for Europe.

Delve a bit deeper though and Rangers fans tend to give credit to his predecessor and are still trying to get rid of some of his flops and elsewhere while he’s had successes his overall recruitment strategy is scattergun at best. Theres no clear player type of strategy picked up, very opportunistic and he’s got good players at good prices (particularly getting Anderson from us) but it seems the strategy is slinging a lot at the wall and seeing what sticks.

If you look back since the takeover we’ve had two highly thought of Directors of football come in and go not long afterwards due to ‘issues’ with others at the club, so despite having a reasonable CV he didn’t come in with as big a reputation and to this observer his primary attribute was he was affable to Eddie and his nephew, which at the time made some sense as Eddie had put together an all time season in 24/25 and looked like he’d be our manager for years to come even after that summer of 25.

So Ross Wilson came in after that summer, which now with the benefit of hindsight has been nothing short of disastrous, with Elanga, Wissa and Ramsey being total overpays (as well as two of them debatably being our biggest wastes of money ever) and Woltemade being a glass slipper when we needed a steel toe capped boot.

Of course, we can’t put any of that on Wilson as he didn’t join till afterwards and it would be a challenge for even the best directors of football to deal with the fallout of it all.

The first warning signs for me came in the January 26 transfer window, when the team was on the ropes, Eddie was struggling and we didn’t bring in any first team recruits to assist a struggling squad. Now I know some of you will bring up the likes of PSR, FFP, SKY 6, Ashley, the Illuminati as reasons why we didn’t spend, but even one signing could have reinvigorated the squad and at least limited the late season slide we went on.

The risk is we’d have to sell at the end of the season to cover ourselves but we had to sell in any case because we plummeted down the league and didn’t even get any European football in one of the weakest Premier Leagues I can remember. It showed very little foresight for Ross Wilson to sit on his hands when the team was visibly on the ropes.

We then come to the end of the season where we have plummeted, put together some Bruce level performances and lost another derby which allowed our dear rivals to experience Europe for the first time in a lifetime. The performances were so poor and we looked totally bereft of ideas. Eddie Howe looked a shell of the man who’d once had us putting it up anyone we came up against.

The decision was there to be made, all logic pointed to a departure of a club legend but as Eddie Howe didn’t quit, Ross Wilson didn’t make the decision to let him go. There’s always the loyalty argument but at that stage it was just ignoring the evidence your eyes presented to you to keep him.

This eventually backfired as by mid pre-season Eddie took the decision out of his hands and left for some well-earned rest and relaxation. This put us further on the back foot as a lot of managers had already moved at the end of the previous season and we’d already started our season’s preparations expecting Eddie Howe to be our manager.

The next managerial move was to bring in Matthias Jaissle, who talked one hell of a game, got to give him and the club credit as the way he was presented looked amazing and had most of us very excited for the potential but then the football started. The ‘vertical football’ promised turned out to be an Allardyce like hoofball without the defensive solidity and if they’d played Stelios as their CF rather than Davies.

As a toon fan Ive had plenty of opportunity to make judgement on God awful managers and all I can see with Jaissle is another one.

Could Wilson have been duped like the rest of us? Of course he could of, but even if he was his actions with 50m+ in the bank to go and sign a left winger who thinks they are a forward when we needed a proper physical CF, a 10, pacy ball playing CBs and an inverted RW was just negligent. Don’t get me wrong, Matias Fernandez-Pardo could turn out great, but it was such a bad use of resources.

In a similar note the signing of Ewen Jaouen is looking like excess which wasn’t needed, again he could be great but spending eight figures on a back-up goalkeeper is just wasteful at best. Signing a bunch of youngsters for a manager who won’t give them minutes in general is foolish.

Overall, regardless of the many flaws Matthias Jaissle has shown, Ross Wilson hasn’t built a squad that’s fit for purpose for the system that Wilson hired the new Head Coach for.

Looking at what we did on outgoings we can also see issues with the way Wilson worked, our pet media liked to say we are trying to use the Brighton model, ignoring that the Brighton model is to bring in younger players who have time to settle in before selling your bigger names. This year we sold all three of Bruno, Gordon, and Tonali before bringing in replacements, putting us straight on the back foot.

We have different opinions on the value we got for those three players (personally I think we sold too early) but having a replacement ready for none of them just shows Ross Wilson hasn’t prepared properly.

Looking at other decisions made over the summer, giving Harvey Barnes an extension rather than cashing in seems similar to previous decisions not to cash in on the likes of Miggy/Wilson after they’d had hot streaks. Barnes puts up great goals and assists stats, and you’ll get the argument ‘where would we be without his goals,’ but I disagree and think a better all-round player will benefit the team more eventually.

The Palace game is the prime example of what Barnes is, great finish for his goal but the number of attacks of ours that broke down because of his unwillingness/inability to beat a man, poor passing and poor decisions was in double figures. I think he’s an effective sub but the time to cash in was this summer gone.

The worst decisions though were to come and that was the decisions not to take the cash for both Murphy and Willock at the end of the window, the decision just tells everyone in the squad that they are the standard.

Murphy’s hot spell is long in the past, he can put in the occasional good cross (maybe one in three or four) but his all-round games makes Barnes look like the complete winger, he’s often terrified in possession and will put teammates under pressure or just play it to nobody so he doesn’t have it. You can also add his age, so to turn down the claimed £15m transfer fee is ridiculous. You may say ‘but he’s a good presence’ turning down £15m (as well as keeping Premier League wages) for someone who smiles and claps is a bit much. As for NUFC needing options on the right – Elanga, Pardo, Osula, Barnes and Wissa have all played on the right at some point and Joelinton and Ramsey have played on the left.

Joe Willock was an afterthought for Eddie Howe, but to his credit this season he’s popped up with some key goals and his chaotic style seems to suit our headless gameplan a little bit at least, though to turn down whatever transfer fee could have been banked in the summer is a failure in long-term squad building.

Overall, we may not quite be a sinking ship just yet due to riding our luck so far, but we can’t continue to go on like this. To fire Matthias Jaissle without also firing Ross Wilson would be like getting rid of the monkey without the organ grinder, so we need to remove Wilson from his position too, to try and get back on the right track.

I’m sure some will say you need to give directors of football time, but sometimes when something is wrong you just need to bite the bullet and move on, rather than persisting with the mistake.

EXCLUSIVE: Jim Carrey Lands a Buyer for His $35 Million Hawaii Home—One Month After Putting it on the Market

📅 Nov 03, 2026  |  Category: Business  |  Read Source →

Jim Carrey has found a buyer for his sprawling Hawaii waterfront retreat, less than one month after listing the tropical property for $35 million and after tying the knot with his longtime partner, Min Ah.

The 64-year-old "Ace Ventura" star married Ah in an intimate ceremony in Los Angeles, with a representative for the actor confirming the nuptials to People.

Fresh off their wedding, Carrey made another major life change by putting his lavish Maui estate on the market.

Now, Realtor.com® can exclusively reveal that the home is in the "pending" stage, meaning Carrey has found a buyer.

The actor purchased the waterfront property for $16.5 million in 2016, according to records. Its $35 million asking price means Carrey is hoping to more than double his investment after a decade of ownership.

Built in 2002, the three-bedroom, 4.5-bathroom residence spans 4,717 square feet and sits on nearly an acre along Keawakapu Beach.

Listed by Blake Hill of Hawaii Life, the property is being offered fully furnished and is described in the listing as a "bohemian luxury unicorn."

"Close to an acre on Keawakapu Beach, where South Kihei Road ends and Wailea begins. Roughly 170 feet along the water lets the homes stand far back behind a wide green lawn and a grove of tall coconut palms," the listing states.

Surrounded by lush tropical greenery, the estate was designed to embrace seamless indoor-outdoor living while taking full advantage of its oceanfront setting.

Inside, soaring ceilings and an expansive floor plan create an airy atmosphere, while coastal-inspired furnishings complement the home's enviable location.

"The great room rises into the trusses and opens fully to a covered stone lanai," the listing adds

The chef's kitchen features two large islands, white cabinetry, and high-end appliances, while the primary suite takes the home's relaxed aesthetic one step further with a swinging bed and floor-to-ceiling windows.

Its accompanying bathroom is equally luxurious, complete with a statement soaking tub and dual vanities.

The dining area continues the breezy beach aesthetic, with a large wooden table and multiple seating areas providing plenty of space for entertaining.

Outside, the amenities become even more resortlike.

"Between the homes, a freeform pool curves through natural rock, hiding a grotto and a slide, with a shallow ledge for lounging and an elevated spa," according to the listing.

The property was designed by architecture firm Ike Kligerman Barkley, with the listing emphasizing the rarity of its expansive oceanfront setting.

"This bohemian luxury property is truly a unicorn. Oceanfront land like this is uncommon in Maui. Designed by Ike Kligerman Barkley. Offered fully furnished and in excellent condition," the description states.

Carrey's decision to sell comes just after he and Ah marked a major milestone in their famously private relationship.

Their marriage followed a rare public appearance together at the César Awards in Paris in February, when Carrey accepted an honorary award and offered a sweet tribute to his partner from the stage.

While accepting the honor, the comedian thanked his daughter, Jane, and grandson, Jackson, before referring to Ah as his "sublime companion" and declaring his love for her.

The couple has otherwise kept their romance largely out of the spotlight, despite reportedly having been together for several years.

That desire for privacy appears to align with the quieter lifestyle Carrey has embraced away from Hollywood in recent years.

The "Truman Show" star has spent significant time in Hawaii, where his waterfront Maui residence provided a dramatically different backdrop from the Los Angeles lifestyle he maintained for decades.

Reports have painted a picture of a decidedly low-key existence for the actor on the island, with Carrey spending his time reading, writing, walking, painting, and sketching.

That makes his decision to part with the property particularly notable. It remains unclear whether Carrey plans to maintain a home in Hawaii after the sale or whether he will return to his California roots.

In 2022, Carrey revealed that he was "fairly serious" about stepping away from acting, explaining that he had become content with the life he had created outside the relentless pace of Hollywood.

"I really like my quiet life," he said at the time while discussing his passion for painting and his spiritual life.

Carrey had spoken about his affinity for solitude even earlier. In 2018, he described his Maui lifestyle as "an isolated life" while explaining that he enjoyed spending time alone.

His move toward a more private existence also coincided with the end of another major chapter in his real estate portfolio: his decadeslong ownership of an impressive Brentwood estate.

Carrey purchased the Los Angeles property for $3.8 million in 1994—the same year his career skyrocketed with the release of "Ace Ventura: Pet Detective," "The Mask," and "Dumb and Dumber."

Over nearly three decades of ownership, the 2-acre property became an amenity-packed private retreat.

Built in 1951, the ranch-style residence spans more than 10,000 square feet and features five bedrooms, six fireplaces, an Art Deco-style movie theater, and a circular sunroom.

After owning the property for nearly 30 years, Carrey decided it was time to move on. He initially listed the Brentwood estate for $28.9 million in February 2023, revealing at the time that he was no longer spending much time there.

Finding a buyer proved to be a lengthy process. The asking price was subsequently reduced to $26.5 million and then $23.95 million. In May 2024, Carrey lowered the price again to $21.9 million before trimming it to $19.75 million that September. The home ultimately sold for $17 million.

She previously wrote for Daily Mail, True Urban Culture, and JMedia Corporation, where she specialized in covering celebrities, relationships, psychology, and gender issues. She has a bachelor's degree in journalism from Pace University, where she received the James Rose Award for excellence in journalism. Mortgage Rates Today, Tuesday, November 3: Still Moving Lower

TL;DR: Mortgage rates eased again today, giving buyers modest relief from rates above 7%.

Mortgage rates are continuing to cool off as we head into November.

The average interest rate on a 30-year, fixed-rate mortgage dropped to 6.93% APR, according to rates provided to NerdWallet by Zillow. This is 14 basis points lower than yesterday and 17 basis points lower than a week ago. (See our chart below for more specifics.) A basis point is one one-hundredth of a percentage point.

A significant drop is eye-catching, but bear in mind that you should consider mortgage interest rates' overall direction, not just what's going on today. Since September, mortgage rates have been hovering around 7%. That’s a full percentage point higher than they were at the beginning of 2026.

This week’s employment data could potentially push mortgage rates one way or the other — more on that below the graph.

» Take the next step: Compare mortgage rates from NerdWallet’s top lenders

Mortgage rates are constantly changing, since a major part of how rates are set depends on reactions to new inflation reports, job numbers, Fed meetings, global news ... you name it. For example, even tiny changes in the bond market can shift mortgage pricing.

This week, the Nerds will be watching the October jobs report, which comes out on Friday, Nov. 6. If it shows that employment remained steady (or even improved), that will build a stronger case for the Federal Reserve to raise overnight borrowing rates again at their next meeting on Dec. 8-9.

While the Fed doesn’t directly set mortgage rates, it does set the federal funds rate, which is the fee that banks must pay to borrow from one another (which is how they fund mortgages). Lenders raise mortgage rates any time they think that the federal funds rate is about to go up.

Central bankers hiked the federal funds rate in September, so a relatively steady jobs report would suggest the labor market could withstand higher rates.

The Fed has a dual mandate to control inflation while supporting maximum employment, and raising the federal funds rate is their main tool for bringing inflation down. If they can work to lower inflation without hurting employment too badly, central bankers may feel more confident to act.

The September Personal Consumption Expenditures price index (PCE), the Fed’s preferred inflation gauge, was released last Thursday. The data shows inflation was 3.6% year-over-year in September, up from 3.4% in August.

September’s federal funds rate hike came in the middle of the month, so October’s inflation data will really capture its effect on inflation. The October PCE report will come out on Nov. 25.

» Learn more: How the Fed affects mortgage rates

Refinancing might make sense if today’s rates are at least 0.5 to 0.75 of a percentage point lower than your current rate (and if you plan to stay in your home long enough to break even on closing costs).

With rates where they are right now, you may want to start considering a refi if your current rate is around 7.43% or higher — but there aren't too many people for whom that's true.

Also consider your goals: Are you trying to lower your monthly payment, shorten your loan term or turn home equity into cash? For example, you might be more comfortable with paying a higher rate for a cash-out refinance than you would for a rate-and-term refinance, so long as the overall costs are lower than if you kept your original mortgage and added a HELOC or home equity loan.

If you're looking for a lower rate, use NerdWallet's refinance calculator to estimate savings and understand how long it would take to break even on the costs of refinancing.

» Time to refi? Compare refinance rates from NerdWallet’s top lenders

There is no universal “right” time to start shopping — what matters is whether you can comfortably afford a mortgage now at today’s rates.

If the answer is yes, don’t get too hung up on whether you could be missing out on lower rates later; you can refinance down the road. Focus on getting preapproved, comparing lender offers, and understanding what monthly payment works for your budget.

NerdWallet’s affordability calculator can help you estimate your potential monthly payment. If a new home isn’t in the cards right now, there are still things you can do to strengthen your buyer profile. Take this time to pay down existing debts and build your down payment savings. Not only will this free up more cash flow for a future mortgage payment, it can also get you a better interest rate when you’re ready to buy.

» Is now a good time to buy? See NerdWallet’s analysis

If you already have a quote you’re happy with, you should consider locking your mortgage rate, especially if your lender offers a float-down option. A float-down lets you take advantage of a better rate if the market drops during your lock period.

Rate locks protect you from increases while your loan is processed, and with the market forever bouncing around, that peace of mind can be worth it.

No Fan Service? FSU Rules Out Deion Sanders

📅 Nov 03, 2026  |  Category: Business  |  Read Source →

Florida State fans might scream for a homecoming, but the people running the show have shut the door on Deion Sanders. The university wants a coach with deep roots, yet it is actively avoiding its most famous alum. A source close to the coaching search told Yahoo Sports that while there is an appetite for an 'FSU guy,' the top brass does not want the baggage Sanders brings. They need stability right now, and his style does not fit that bill.

The administration knows the fans love the idea of Prime returning to Tallahassee. But this is a business decision, not a nostalgia trip. The program is in the middle of a rebuild and needs someone who can handle the pressure without turning every day into a media event. Sanders has the name, but his recent track record makes him a risky bet for a team trying to climb out of the ACC mess.

Let's look at the actual wins and losses before we get carried away by the star power. Sanders is 5-14 since his strong 9-4 season in 2024, according to CBS Sports. That puts his overall record at Colorado at 18-25 in his fourth year. Florida State just fired Mike Norvell because he could not win enough games in conference play. Hiring a coach with a 9-21 conference record feels like a gamble the school cannot afford right now.

The numbers show a program that struggles to close out games. Colorado led many contests this season but found new ways to lose. Fans are disappearing in droves, and the investment has not led to consistent results on the field. A rebuild needs a steady hand, not a revolving door of headlines. The administration sees the cold, hard facts and decides they cannot take that chance.

There is also the issue of history. Sanders has not exactly been waving the garnet-and-gold flag lately. He told the media a few years back, "I'm not a Nole, I'm an HBCU grad," pointing out his degree came from Talladega College in 2020. That comment annoyed old-school boosters and left a sour taste in the mouths of university officials. After he secured top recruit Travis Hunter for Jackson State instead of the Seminoles, fans burned his jersey.

When asked about taking that recruit, Sanders said on The Rich Eisen Show, "He never belonged to them; I don't think I 'took' him." That kind of friction is the last thing a program needs as it tries to heal. The search is for someone who brings authentic program roots and institutional stability. Sanders checks the first box but violates the second with his high-profile friction and personal branding. The top-down message is clear: they want a rebuild, not a circus.

The border wall dispute isn’t fit for the emergency docket

📅 Nov 03, 2026  |  Category: Business  |  Read Source →

Type to search articles, cases, and authors.

In its latest request to the Supreme Court over President Donald Trump’s approach to immigration, the Justice Department turned to the court’s emergency docket to ask for fast-track permission to construct a border barrier system along a 517-mile stretch in remote west Texas. Stymied by a federal district court’s preliminary injunction issued in October, the Justice Department argues that Supreme Court intervention is needed to prevent irreparable harm to the country. But details of the government’s legal argument reveal that the Trump administration hopes to use the emergency docket as a fast track to victory regardless of the actual urgency of the legal dispute.

Early this year, the Department of Homeland Security began taking important preliminary steps to build physical barriers through Big Bend National Park, Big Bend Ranch State Park, and nearby property along the U.S.-Mexico border. Part of the Border Patrol’s Big Bend Sector, covering approximately one-quarter of the entire border, no artificial barriers exist there. Despite its massive geographic reach, the region is sparsely populated and at immense distance from large urban centers. Unlike other parts of the U.S.-Mexico border, few people or goods cross from one country to the other in the Big Bend Sector. Indeed, according to U.S. District Judge Kathleen Cardone, who issued a preliminary injunction blocking DHS from carrying out its plans to erect a border barrier in the area, this is the border region with “the lowest aggregate number of apprehensions [of migrants] and the lowest number of apprehensions per mile of border.”

In Mullin v. Friends of the Ruidosa Church, a group of individuals with personal or business ties to the region, along with two advocacy groups, argued that DHS had failed to comply with required environmental reviews. Pointing to a 1996 law signed by President Bill Clinton, the Justice Department responded that Secretary of Homeland Security Markwayne Mullin and his predecessor, Kristi Noem, waived application of those environmental laws. The government also argued that the district court lacked the power to decide the lawsuit in the first place.

Cardone disagreed with the government on both counts. Congress narrowed courts’ jurisdiction in border-barrier disputes, but left room for courts to consider constitutional challenges to DHS’s conduct, she concluded. The challenge to Mullin’s waiver of environmental laws raises a constitutional question, Cardone added.

Having concluded that she could consider the challengers’ lawsuit, Cardone then turned to the criteria that Congress imposed on the secretary of Homeland Security to waive environmental review. The 1996 law directs the immigration agency “to waive all legal requirements” that could slow or impede construction of border barriers and roads. But the same law also instructs DHS to build barriers and roads “in areas of high illegal entry into the United States.” Considering the government’s data about apprehensions of people and seizures of drugs in the Big Bend Sector, as compared to other parts of the U.S.-Mexico border, Cardone concluded that the region cannot be considered to have high levels of illegal entry. “[I]f the Big Bend Sector is an area of high illegal entry, so is every other sector,” Cardone announced.

Seven days after Cardone released her opinion, the U.S. solicitor general asked the Supreme Court to intervene. Much of the government’s argument turns on its disagreement with Cardone about what constitutes an area of “high illegal entry.” Mullin determined that the Big Bend Sector constitutes an area of high illegal entry because Customs and Border Protection, the DHS agency that enforces immigration laws at and near the border, apprehended 89,000 people and “seized enormous amounts” of illicit drugs from fiscal years 2021 to 2025, the government explains. In addition, failure to build a border barrier in this region “would act as a dangerous magnet” for additional illegal entries, the solicitor general claims. Despite Mullin’s assessment that the facts on the ground met the “high illegal entry” threshold required by Congress, “the district court nonetheless issued an unprecedented injunction blocking those waivers and halting associated border-wall construction,” the solicitor general wrote to the court.

Unsurprisingly, the challengers disagree. Opposing the government’s request for relief from the justices, they describe Mullin and Noem’s waivers as “unprecedented” and claim they “carry undisputed and extraordinary political and economic significance.”

The solicitor general’s claim is fundamentally a dispute about the proper interpretation of the text of the 1996 law. The government argues that courts cannot second-guess Mullin’s decision to waive environmental laws, and that Mullin’s view of what constitutes “high illegal entry” is all that matters. Cardone disagrees and contends that courts have a role to play here.

By turning to the emergency docket, the Justice Department has dispensed with the ordinary appellate process in favor of a fast-track path to, it hopes, final victory. Wanting an answer sooner rather than later is understandable, but a party’s interest in “line-jumping,” as Justice Ketanji Brown Jackson has described the emergency docket, isn’t reason enough to circumvent the slower course of the ordinary appellate process. Jackson would prefer to limit the emergency docket to situations in which an actual emergency exists. It’s hard to see what exigency exists in west Texas, however. The solicitor general argues that drugs and people will continue to enter through the Big Bend Sector. That is undoubtedly true, but, as that stretch of land has been unfortified since it became a border on March 18, 1848, nothing is likely to occur in the near future that is markedly different from past decades.

The government’s request for fast-track intervention by the Supreme Court also does not meet the standards set by Jackson’s ideological opposites. Explaining his view of the court’s emergency docket in Trump v. CASA, the court’s fast-track decision announcing that district courts are not authorized to issue universal injunctions, Justice Brett Kavanaugh wrote that its alternative pathway is appropriate when “Congress passes a major new statute, or the Executive Branch issues a major new rule or executive order.” Neither applies to the border-wall dispute. Congress enacted the contested statute 30 years ago and the waivers issued by Mullin and Noem are neither an agency rule nor an executive order.

Nor is the border-wall legal battle the type of dispute that only the Supreme Court can resolve. In a 2021 speech, Justice Samuel Alito compared the court’s emergency docket to a hospital’s emergency room, suggesting that, in his view at least, the fast-track process is proper only when no suitable alternative exists. But an alternative does exist to decide if Cardone overstepped. The government could have appealed Cardone’s decision to the U.S. Court of Appeals for the 5th Circuit. Like other circuit courts, the 5th Circuit regularly reviews preliminary injunctions granted by district courts. As Kavanaugh explained in CASA, “The courts of appeals and this Court can (and regularly do) expeditiously review district court decisions awarding or denying preliminary injunctive relief.” Instead of asking the 5th Circuit to correct the mistake it claims Cardone committed, the solicitor general skipped the 5th Circuit and went directly to the Supreme Court.

The reason it did this is not difficult to figure out: The administration is hopeful that it will receive a friendly audience at the Supreme Court. But this is a fast-track procedure meant for exceptional circumstances. And there is nothing exceptional about the legal fight over the administration’s attempt to build new segments of border barrier in rural west Texas. If the court grants the government’s request to pause Cardone’s preliminary injunction, allowing construction to move forward where no barrier currently exists, it should, at the very least, explain how this case meets the criteria that the justices have previously provided and if the term “emergency docket” is a misnomer.

César Cuauhtémoc García Hernández is the author of three books about immigration law and policy.

Titan1Studios Adapting Stephen R. Bown’s ‘The Company: The Rise and Fall Of The Hudson’s Bay Empire’ As Historical Drama Series

📅 Nov 03, 2026  |  Category: Business  |  Read Source →

EXCLUSIVE: This one’s being billed as “The Crown meets Peaky Blinders” – albeit set on the other side of the Atlantic Ocean.

Calgary’s Titan1Studios has secured media and adaptation rights to Stephen R. Bown‘s historical book The Company: The Rise and Fall of the Hudson’s Bay Empire, and is developing a TV drama series titled House of Hudson.

The series will be centered on “the cutthroat, 19th-century corporate battles that forged a continent,” with Titan1Studios, which has exclusive media and adaptation rights, underway on development. A search for a showrunner is underway. Related Stories News European Production Association CEPI Welcomes Canadian Bodies As First International Members News Hudson Williams, Zach Galifianakis & Jay Baruchel Set For 'The Assembly' Season 2 At Canada's CBC

Here’s the synopsis: “Set against the dramatic backdrop of the 1820s, House of Hudson operates as The Crown meets Peaky Blinders – exploring the bloody war of attrition between the Hudson’s Bay Company and its hyper-violent rival, the North West Company, culminating in their forced corporate merger and the ruthless rise of Sir George Simpson, the cold-blooded governor known as ‘The Little Emperor.'” Watch on Deadline

The plan is for the series to shift between “the opulent, scheming boardrooms of London and Montreal and the unforgiving wilderness,” looking at at British imperial machinery in the UK, the struggle for the Pacific Northwest frontier in the U.S., and the corporate origins of modern Canada. “At its core, it is a universal, character-driven boardroom thriller about the global cost of early capitalism,” said Titan1Studios.

Bown’s book, The Company: The Rise and Fall of the Hudson’s Bay Empire, was published in 2020 and is known for taking a fresh perspective on foundational Canadian stories, with a broader look at events that highlight characters not previously part of dominant narratives. Since release, the book has won the 2021 National Business Book Award and the J.W. Dafoe Book Prize.

“Stephen R. Bown managed to take a foundational piece of North American history and frame it with the relentless pacing, psychological depth, and tension of a modern corporate drama,” said Rathan Moorthy, CEO and executive producer of Titan1Studios.

“The Hudson’s Bay Company was effectively the Amazon of the 19th century – a multi-national corporate entity with its own currency, private military, and total control over millions of square miles. We are thrilled to bring Stephen’s masterwork to the screen for global television audiences.”

“When I wrote The Company, my goal was to bring the human drama, complex motivations, and fierce corporate rivalries of the fur trade to life,” added. Bown. “Titan1Studios shares a compelling, clear vision for how to translate this high-stakes narrative into a visually stunning, character-focused television event. I’m excited to see this epic story brought to a whole new audience.”

Titan1Studios’ Moorthy and VP of Content Wes Ambrecht are leading on the project, with the rights negotiations handled in collaboration with Jake Babad and Caroline Vassallo at Westwood Creative Art.

Bown has authored several books on history, exploration and biography, including The Company, Island of the Blue Foxes and Dominion, and his books have been translated into multiple languages, earning acclaim for their cinematic storytelling and rigorous historical research.

For Titan1Studios, the option adds to a slate already including heist drama Sideshow (working title), an Events at Unity Farm TV adaptation and a franchise project based around Steve Lerner’s Cat Assassin, with Dave Bautista’s Dogbone Entertainment also attached.

How Syrup Became a Coffee-Shop Culture War

📅 Nov 03, 2026  |  Category: Business  |  Read Source →

For some coffee shop customers, premade syrups are now a major red flag. It presents a new opportunity for small business owners.

In a TikTok video with over 82,000 likes, the person behind the camera zooms into the shelves at a Pennsylvania coffee shop, three rows stacked with an array of Torani syrups in flavors like blueberry, brown-sugar cinnamon, English toffee, and salted caramel. “I just know my latte isn’t going to be good,” the caption reads. Across social media, similar sentiments appear. “If I see these at a cafe, I’m walking out immediately,” says one tweet, co-signed by many commenters. In a multipart video series, another creator documents her journey of replacing all of her Torani syrups with ones from small businesses instead. Last year, the niche food newsletter Unsnackable published what was described as a “Torani tirade.”

It’s not just a Torani problem, though the brand — by virtue of its market dominance — tends to get the most flack on social media. While some people seem to prefer competitor Monin, it, too, draws backlash over allegations of tasting “like chemicals.” The problem, for some drinkers of dessert-like specialty beverages, is premade syrups across the board.

For a certain sect of consumers, they’ve become a coffee shop red flag, a sign that a so-called “specialty” establishment asking them to fork over $8 plus tip for a latte is unfairly cutting corners when it comes to quality or craft. For these consumers, mass-market syrups now symbolize exactly that: They’re synonymous with coffee chains and corporations, the kind of impersonal institutions that many people are trying to get away from when they patronize a small business. The flip side: For independently owned coffee shops across the country, house-made syrup now represents a major opportunity.

When Carrie and Dennis Medina first envisioned their coffee shop, Room Service, in Ventura, California, they thought they would take an approach similar to the one at Sightglass Coffee, where they’d both gotten their start. “Very purist, with no syrups, no specialty drinks,” Dennis says. They imagined eight kinds of pour-over coffee and an emphasis on single-origin beans, since the couple also runs a roastery. “We said we’re never gonna do cream tops; we’re never gonna do these crazy drinks,” Carrie says. But in the first week alone, every customer was asking for them.

Now, Room Service offers 10 to 12 different syrups at any given time (and six different cream tops), all of them made in-house. It offers drinks such as a cinnamon roll latte topped with a cinnamon roll, and a honey cornbread latte with an ube cream top. “I hear that question all the time,” Dennis says. “‘Hey, do you guys make your syrups in-house?’” To him, a shop that isn’t trying to do at least the basics — like vanilla — in-house is “falling behind what the times are pushing for.”

The demand for syrups and other embellishments is so intense that the Medinas have brought on a full-time prep person who works exclusively on “all these things that we do in-house that make us special,” Carrie says. Room Service’s PSL, for example, starts with kabocha squash. It’s roasted, blended with spices such as cloves and cinnamon, and cooked into syrup, a process that takes two days. Echoing a sentiment that seems common among coffee shop owners right now, Carrie describes their current approach as “not necessarily the coffee shop we wanted, but it’s more like the coffee shop we have to be.” And if the market demands syrups and cream tops, then the Medinas wanted to ensure that the intention and emphasis on quality that they applied to their coffee beans also carried over.

In 2025, Michael Smith opened Trust Coffee Co. in Bay Village, Ohio, taking over, remodeling, and rebranding an older coffee shop. He, like the Medinas, imagined a more “pour-over, third-wave” style establishment. “But I’ve been a business owner for a long time,” says Smith, who also runs a restaurant and a catering business. “I’ve realized over the years that your customers tell you more about who you are than who you actually think you are.” His customers, it turned out, wanted seasonal syrups in niche flavors like maple rosemary, caramelized banana, and strawberry rhubarb.

The purist is no longer coffee’s north star; the maximalist is.

Syrups like Monin, which the shop’s previous iteration used, “serve a purpose in the industry,” Smith says, but he wanted more control over the menu. The two full-time pastry chefs from Smith’s other businesses oversee the shop’s syrups. “It’s become such a huge thing,” he says: Every time Trust’s social media manager posts a new syrup, the line is out the door for a week. “It sets us apart,” he explains.

When Sabrina Kaylor opened the first location of Bizarre Coffee in Canton, Georgia in 2020, the shop used bottled syrups. She hadn’t worked in coffee prior, and she said that approach felt like the norm at the time. “It allowed us to hone our craft and learn what we were doing,” she says. Over time, Kaylor — who’s quite active on the company’s official social media platforms — noticed that the tenor of the conversation was changing: In the immediate post-COVID years, people were making more things at home and there was more pride to be gained in “scratch-made.” With her business becoming known for lattes in flavors such as blueberry, banana, and guava, Kaylor decided to shift toward more house-made syrups. Previously, her customers didn’t ask about their sourcing, but now, they ask all the time.

As much as there is a sense of valor in the entirely from-scratch approach, Kaylor retains some skepticism about the current torrent of Torani and Monin backlash. Today, 90 percent of Bizarre’s drinks use house-made syrups, though one of its best-selling drinks still relies on the bottled stuff; Bizarre’s menu is extensive and making everything in-house would be a significant investment of labor. On the part of coffee shops, “I think a lot of [the house-made syrup conversation] is marketing, quite honestly,” Kaylor says. As for the bandwagoning that is all the anti-Torani memes, “I think it’s part of consumer behavior,” she says. And that, she acknowledges, can be really “discouraging” for small business owners.

In a sense, that’s what the premade-versus-house-made-syrup conversation boils down to: the stand between small businesses and big ones. For more consumers, the economic and social conditions of the current moment have inspired increased skepticism toward the influence of corporations and private equity in the food and beverage world.

The Minnesota coffee shop Little Joy has become famous across social media for posting the recipes of its signature drinks in detailed DIY videos. It has since launched a membership program through which other coffee shop owners can purchase its recipes, scaled up for commercial usage; gain access to training videos; and sell its drinks without even needing to attribute them to Little Joy, though the ones that would like to can pin their use of Little Joy’s recipes to a Drink Finder map.

Yes, these might just be recipes for syrups and foams, but in an interview with Eater’s industry newsletter Pre Shift earlier this year, Little Joy owner Cody Larson described the system as a way to build solidarity among independent businesses. “The big coffee chains, they’re never gonna do house-made syrups, so I think that’s where independent shops can get a leg up,” he said. “Ultimately, the chains are our competition... If independent shops can, in any way possible, raise the bar across the board, that’s good for all independent shops, including us.”

It’s hard to look at the drinks coming out of specialty coffee shops today — fluffy with foam, flavored to emulate nuanced desserts like princess cake and tiramisu— and not think of the Starbucks Frappuccino. The combination of app-ordering and social media “hack” culture have mainstreamed super-elaborate beverage requests, allowing Starbucks and coffee chains like it to become purveyors of highly complicated customizable beverages, mainstreaming the mindset of the aforementioned “hyperflavor.”

When it comes to why consumers have moved away from the single-origin pour-over approach at shops like his, “my guess is that these are a lot of people who used to be Starbucks loyalists and they’re looking for something that’s more local,” Smith says. “It’s a trend across everything where people want local, and we’re able to provide what Starbucks did, but in a more high-quality, more local way.”

Intentionality is what people are gravitating towards, especially as prices rise. If consumers are spending so much on a drink that’s undeniably a luxury, then they want to know that every part of that drink has been made carefully. And if they’re going to make the trip to a new, independent shop, they want to know that the offerings there aren’t the ones they can get anywhere, made with easily attainable, big-brand flavorings. For small coffee shops, the key to success could be as simple as syrup.

The freshest news from the food world every day
