Meteor Education Acquires AET Labs, Strengthening Career and Technical Education Offerings in New England

📅 Nov 03, 2026  |  Category: Business  |  Read Source →

GAINESVILLE, Fla., Nov. 3, 2026 /PRNewswire/ -- Meteor Education, a leading provider of innovative learning environments and education solutions, today announced it has acquired AET Labs, a Massachusetts-based provider of technical training equipment and lab solutions serving educational institutions across New England. The acquisition further strengthens Meteor Education's growing Career and Technical Education (CTE) capabilities and expands its ability to support schools and institutions in preparing students for the evolving demands of the modern workforce. Financial terms of the transaction were not disclosed.

Founded in 2005, AET Labs has built a strong reputation as a trusted partner to K12 schools, community colleges, and universities throughout New England. With expertise in engineering, industrial automation, advanced manufacturing, and automotive technology, AET Labs helps institutions develop technical learning environments that connect classroom instruction with real-world industry applications.

Meteor Education has steadily expanded its CTE platform, most notably through its 2024 acquisition of Advanced Technology Consultants (ATC). Together, AET Labs and ATC bring complementary expertise, established manufacturer relationships, and expanded technical training solutions that strengthen Meteor's ability to support CTE programs nationwide. The platform works to help build and sustain high-quality CTE programs through better lab design, equipment selection, professional, development, and industry-aligned certifications.

"Expanding access to meaningful, hands-on learning opportunities is central to our mission of preparing future-ready students," said Mitch Lewis, Executive Chair of Meteor Education. "AET Labs brings tremendous technical expertise and trusted relationships with educators throughout New England and advances our goal of developing a leading national CTE platform. By combining these strengths with our existing CTE capabilities, we can help more schools create innovative learning environments that equip students with the skills and confidence to succeed in today's workforce."

David Kempskie, Founder of AET Labs, will remain with the business and lead Meteor Education's CTE work in New England going forward. AET Labs will continue to serve its customers with the same team and technical expertise, now supported by Meteor Education's broader national platform and resources.

"Since founding AET Labs, our focus has been on connecting technical education with the real-world skills students need for successful careers," said Kempskie. "Joining Meteor Education allows us to build on that commitment while expanding the solutions available to the institutions we serve."

Meteor Education is continuing to expand the tools, training, and learning environments that help educators prepare students for the workforce. By broadening its CTE capabilities, the company is helping schools deliver hands-on learning experiences that build practical skills, industry-recognized credentials, and pathways to high-demand careers.

For more information, please visit www.meteoreducation.com.

Meteor Education is a leader in the design and delivery of collaborative, flexible learning environments that accelerate student engagement. As part of its full-service approach, Meteor Education's local teams, educator experts, and design specialists partner closely with each school district to create pro-social classrooms and other custom spaces that empower educators to develop future-ready students. Meteor Education also provides professional development training to teachers to help maximize the positive potential of each environment, along with tools so districts can measure the impact on students. Meteor Education has helped thousands of schools improve their learning environments for more than 30 years and is backed by Bain Capital Double Impact.

Founded in 2005, AET Labs is a trusted provider of technical training equipment, lab design solutions, and educator support services for K-12 schools, vocational-technical schools, community colleges, and universities across New England. AET Labs partners with leading manufacturers to deliver industry-aligned solutions in engineering, industrial automation, advanced manufacturing, automotive technology, and other technical disciplines. Guided by its core values of collaboration, integrity, innovation, and loyalty, AET Labs is dedicated to helping educational institutions create hands-on learning experiences that prepare students for successful careers.

Fiserv Takes $6.45B Charge as Margins Shrink: Rapid Earnings Reaction

📅 Nov 03, 2026  |  Category: Business  |  Read Source →

Fiserv's third quarter showed stalled sales, thinner merchant margins, and a growing pile of costs kept out of adjusted profit. Here's what that means for the full-year outlook.

Fiserv (FISV +7.03%) booked a $6.45 billion non-cash goodwill write-down, which turned third-quarter GAAP earnings into a loss of $11.17 per share. Shares rose about 10% in pre-market trading. Behind the charge, organic revenue was flat and adjusted earnings fell to $1.75 from $2.04, so profit is shrinking faster than revenue. The affirmed full-year guidance now needs a clear revenue turn in the fourth quarter.

Costs are rising while sales stall. Adjusted operating income fell 17% while revenue barely moved, and most of that loss sits in Merchant Solutions, the card-acceptance and Clover business.

Adjusted earnings leave out a lot. Fiserv has excluded nearly $500 million of One Fiserv transformation costs this year, covering technology modernization, consulting, and retention pay. Multi-year costs that recur each quarter are part of what the business really earns.

Guidance is the real test. Organic revenue is down 3% for the nine months, so the affirmed full-year range takes a clear fourth-quarter turn.

Watch whether fourth-quarter organic revenue turns clearly positive, as management expects. Also track whether the raised $1 billion Project Elevate savings target shows up in margins.

Binance Just Got Hacked for $6.3 Billion. Is Its Coin Still a Buy?

📅 Nov 03, 2026  |  Category: Business  |  Read Source →

BNB's strong performance in the recent past might not continue for much longer.

On the evening of Oct. 29, hackers stole an estimated $6.3 billion in funds from the Binance crypto exchange, marking the biggest hack in crypto history. The exchange has since promised to cover every customer loss, and it still has more than enough money on hand for that promise to be credible.

The exchange's blockchain didn't seem to be directly affected by the attack, yet its native token, BNB (BNB -1.03%), was down nearly 7% over the 24 hours since the breach. It's still up by 212% over the last three years. So is BNB worth buying, or is the Binance hack the beginning of its end?

As of Oct. 30, Binance's partners had frozen at least $3 billion of the stolen funds, and the exchange had set aside $6.3 billion to make its customers whole.

The drained wallets were among those listed in Binance's monthly proof of reserves, briefly leaving Binance's Ethereum reserves covering only about 94% of customers' ether balances. By Oct. 31, Binance said a new reserve snapshot showed ether and nine other assets fully backed again. So the damage to Binance's reserves looks small and temporary.

Furthermore, the hackers didn't do or steal anything that would touch Binance's supply of BNB.

The coin is used on the exchange's blockchain, and holding it can buy fee discounts on the exchange itself, so its value depends entirely on investors' trust in Binance, as it has no utility outside its ecosystem. And despite the theft, there is little indication that people have lost enough faith in Binance to stop using its exchange -- the same as in prior instances where it was affected by market crashes, breaches, or other problems.

In my view, there wasn't a very strong investment thesis for buying BNB before the hack, and there isn't one for buying it now, either. Here's why.

BNB Chain destroys BNB coins every quarter via its Auto-Burn mechanism, which removes coins from the supply based on its price and how many blocks are produced by the chain over the quarter. There's also another supply-reducing mechanism that destroys a portion of the BNB that's used to pay each transaction fee on the network.

The point of those two processes is to make the coin more appealing to hold by linking the chain's utilization to its supply.

Nonetheless, its base of transaction fees hasn't grown over the last three years; in the last week of October 2023, it had $3.1 million in fees, whereas the same week this year saw only $2.9 million. And while it's true that the coin's price went up dramatically over that period, the data suggest that demand for blockchain use isn't consistently growing, which significantly undermines the idea that BNB is going to be a good investment over the longer term.

So, even if the price of BNB could be much higher three years from now, I don't think it's worth buying.

Alex Carchidi has positions in Ethereum. The Motley Fool has positions in and recommends Ethereum. The Motley Fool recommends BNB. The Motley Fool has a disclosure policy.

From hand weakness to slurred speech: 12 things to know about ALS

📅 Nov 03, 2026  |  Category: Business  |  Read Source →

Free daily briefing on global business news.

The early signs of ALS range from hand weakness to slurred speech, and these 12 points cover symptoms, diagnosis, genetics and care for the disease

A fumbled shirt button, a toe that catches on the stairs, a voice that sounds slightly thick by evening. Changes like these are easy to blame on age, stress or a long week, and most of the time that explanation is correct. In some people, though, they are the first outward signs of amyotrophic lateral sclerosis.

ALS is a progressive disease of the motor neurons, the nerve cells in the brain and spinal cord that carry signals to the muscles people move by choice. As those cells stop working, the muscles they control weaken and shrink. In the U.S., the condition is widely known as Lou Gehrig's disease, after the New York Yankees first baseman whose career it ended. In the U.K. and many other countries, it is usually called motor neuron disease, a term that also covers a few related conditions.

The early signs of ALS are hard to read for a simple reason. Each one, taken alone, has many other possible causes. Muscle twitching is common in healthy people. Cramps often follow exercise or dehydration. Foot drop can come from a compressed nerve near the knee or in the lower back. What sets ALS apart is a pattern of painless weakness that persists and slowly spreads. It often starts on one side of the body or in the muscles used for speaking and swallowing.

No blood test or scan confirms the disease on its own. Neurologists build the diagnosis from a physical examination and electrical tests of nerves and muscles. Imaging and lab work rule out conditions that look similar. That process can take time.

This guide covers 12 points about ALS symptoms and the disease behind them. It describes changes people commonly notice first, including hand weakness, twitching, cramps, tripping, slurred speech and trouble swallowing. It also explains the difference between limb-onset and bulbar-onset disease. It covers how a diagnosis is reached, what is known about inherited forms and how treatment and supportive care work today. It is a selection of topics, not a complete account, and it does not replace medical advice.

No single symptom on this list means a person has ALS. Persistent or worsening weakness, speech changes or swallowing problems are reasons to see a doctor. A primary care doctor can arrange a referral to a neurologist when the pattern calls for one.

Amyotrophic lateral sclerosis, or ALS, is a progressive neurodegenerative disease that destroys motor neurons. These are the nerve cells that control voluntary muscles. Americans often know it as Lou Gehrig's disease, a nickname taken from the New York Yankees first baseman whose diagnosis made the illness widely known.

Motor neurons come in two types. Upper motor neurons run from the brain's motor cortex down into the brainstem and spinal cord. Lower motor neurons carry signals from there out to the muscles themselves. ALS damages both groups. That combination sets it apart from many other nerve disorders.

When these neurons stop working, muscles no longer receive the signals they need. The muscles weaken, shrink and eventually stop moving on command. Affected muscles include those people use to walk, speak, swallow and breathe.

The disease's name describes what happens inside the body. "Amyotrophic" comes from Greek roots meaning "no muscle nourishment." It refers to the wasting of muscle tissue. "Lateral" points to the side regions of the spinal cord, where the affected nerve fibers travel. "Sclerosis" means hardening or scarring. It describes how that tissue looks as the neurons degenerate.

The French neurologist Jean-Martin Charcot described the disease in the 19th century. In France, it is still often called maladie de Charcot, or Charcot's disease.

Gehrig's story explains why his name stuck. He was known as one of baseball's most durable players. Then his performance declined, and he was diagnosed with ALS. In his farewell speech at Yankee Stadium, he called himself "the luckiest man on the face of the earth." The line is still widely quoted.

Outside North America, the term motor neuron disease, or MND, is common. In the U.K. and Australia, MND is the usual name for ALS. Clinicians also apply the term more broadly, to a family of related conditions that includes ALS.

ALS generally leaves the senses intact. Sight, hearing, taste, smell and touch are usually unaffected. The muscles that move the eyes and those that control the bladder are often spared until late in the disease. Some people do develop changes in thinking or behavior. ALS has a recognized overlap with frontotemporal dementia. The disease is not contagious.

Painless weakness in one hand, arm or leg is one of the most common ways amyotrophic lateral sclerosis first appears. It usually starts on one side of the body and worsens gradually. It rarely appears suddenly or affects both sides equally at first.

The cause is lost nerve supply, not an injury to the muscle itself. Muscles that stop receiving steady signals from motor neurons lose strength and, over time, bulk.

In the hands, the first changes often involve fine motor tasks. A person may struggle to fasten a button, turn a key, open a jar or pinch a small object between thumb and finger. Handwriting can become cramped or less steady. Dropping cups or utensils more often is another common complaint.

Muscle wasting in the hand is often visible. The fleshy pad at the base of the thumb may flatten. A hollow can form in the web of skin between the thumb and index finger. Neurologists describe a pattern called "split hand." In this pattern, the muscles on the thumb side waste while those on the little-finger side are relatively spared.

Weakness higher in the arm shows up differently. Lifting a bag into an overhead bin, washing hair or reaching for a high shelf can become harder. In the legs, early weakness may make it difficult to climb stairs. Getting up from a low chair without pushing off with the hands can also become a struggle.

True weakness is different from tiredness. Fatigue tends to ease after rest. Weakness from motor neuron loss persists. An affected muscle cannot produce its normal force even when it is fresh.

Sensation usually stays intact in ALS. Numbness, tingling or burning point more toward other problems. These include carpal tunnel syndrome, a pinched nerve in the neck and peripheral neuropathy. Those conditions are far more common, and many are treatable.

Weakness in one limb does not on its own mean someone has ALS. Neurologists look for weakness that keeps progressing, spreads to nearby muscles over weeks or months and comes with visible muscle loss.

Muscle twitching on its own is not a sign of ALS. Fasciculations become a concern for a neurologist mainly when they appear alongside muscle weakness or visible muscle wasting in the same part of the body.

Fasciculations are brief, involuntary contractions of small groups of muscle fibers. They show up as a flicker, ripple or jump under the skin. They usually do not move a joint and are typically painless. They can appear in the calves, thighs, arms, hands, eyelids or almost anywhere else.

Many healthy people notice them at some point. Common triggers include caffeine, intense exercise, stress, anxiety, poor sleep and fatigue. Some medications and stimulants can provoke them too. Low levels of minerals such as magnesium or potassium are another possible cause. A twitching eyelid is one of the most familiar examples. It usually fades on its own.

Persistent twitching with no other problems has its own name: benign fasciculation syndrome. People with this condition can have frequent, widespread twitches for long stretches. Their strength stays normal, and their muscles do not shrink. The condition is not dangerous, though the twitches can be bothersome.

In ALS, fasciculations have a different origin. They reflect damage to lower motor neurons, the nerve cells in the brainstem and spinal cord that connect directly to muscles. As these cells deteriorate, the muscle fibers they control can fire spontaneously. The twitches are a sign of that breakdown, not a cause of it.

Context matters more than the twitch itself. In ALS, fasciculations tend to show up in muscles that are also becoming weaker or thinner. A person might see twitching in a hand that has started to struggle with fine tasks. It might also appear in a shoulder or forearm that looks hollower than before. Twitching of the tongue, seen during a clinical exam, can point to involvement of the muscles that control speech and swallowing.

The frequency or intensity of twitching does not by itself separate a benign cause from a serious one. A doctor looks for the combination: twitching plus progressive weakness, loss of muscle bulk or changes in reflexes. During an evaluation, a neurologist checks strength and muscle bulk directly. Electrical testing can then look for signs of nerve damage beyond the twitches.

For someone with twitching and normal strength, a primary care doctor can start by reviewing sleep, caffeine intake, current medications and basic blood work.

Muscle cramps and stiffness are common ALS symptoms, and they come from damage to two different groups of nerve cells. Cramps reflect trouble in the lower motor neurons that connect to muscles. Stiffness reflects loss of the upper motor neurons in the brain.

An ALS-related cramp is a sudden, painful, involuntary tightening of a muscle. It can strike the calves, thighs, hands, feet or abdomen. Some people notice cramps in muscles that rarely cramped before, such as those in the hands. Cramps can arrive with little exertion, including at rest or during sleep.

Lower motor neurons run from the brainstem and spinal cord out to the muscles. As these cells become damaged, the nerve fibers can fire irregularly. That abnormal activity can set off a sustained contraction the person cannot stop at will.

Stiffness has a different origin. Upper motor neurons normally send signals that help keep muscle tone in check. When they degenerate, muscles can become tight and resistant to movement. This condition is called spasticity. A stiff leg may feel heavy or slow to swing forward. Walking can become effortful, and balance can suffer.

Upper motor neuron loss also changes reflexes. A neurologist tapping the knee may find an unusually brisk response, known as hyperreflexia. Stroking the sole of the foot may make the big toe curl upward instead of down. This response is called the Babinski sign. In ALS, these findings can appear alongside weakness and muscle wasting in the same limb. That mix of upper and lower motor neuron signs in one region helps distinguish ALS from many other conditions.

Cramps on their own are common and usually harmless. Pregnancy, low levels of minerals such as potassium or magnesium and certain medications, including some diuretics, can all trigger them. Occasional nighttime calf cramps in an otherwise healthy person are not a sign of ALS.

Cramps and spasticity can often be eased. Regular stretching and physical therapy help keep joints flexible and limit muscle shortening. Doctors may prescribe muscle relaxants such as baclofen or tizanidine to reduce spasticity. Treatment aims to relieve stiffness without adding to weakness. Some muscle tone can help a weak leg bear weight. For that reason, doses are adjusted with care.

Foot drop is difficulty lifting the front of the foot, and it is one way ALS can first appear in the legs. It often makes a person catch their toes and trip more than usual, typically on one side before the other.

The problem lies in the muscles along the front of the shin. The main one is the tibialis anterior, which pulls the toes and forefoot upward with each step. When the motor neurons that control it weaken, the foot hangs lower as the leg swings forward.

The resulting changes are often small at first. A toe snags on the edge of a rug, a curb or a stair. A shoe scuffs the ground. Some people hear their foot slap down on the floor instead of rolling smoothly from heel to toe.

To compensate, many people begin lifting the knee higher than normal so the toes clear the ground. This pattern is called a steppage gait. Others swing the leg outward in a slight arc. Family members sometimes notice the altered walk before the person does.

Foot drop has many causes, and most of them are not ALS. A common one is compression of the peroneal nerve, also called the fibular nerve, where it wraps around the outside of the knee. Habitual leg crossing, long periods of squatting, rapid weight loss or a tight cast can all press on it. A herniated disc pinching the L5 nerve root in the lower back can produce the same weakness. Diabetic neuropathy, stroke and multiple sclerosis are other possibilities.

The surrounding symptoms help separate these causes. Nerve compression and disc problems often bring numbness, tingling or pain in the back and leg. Foot drop from ALS is usually painless and comes without loss of sensation. The disease targets motor neurons and leaves sensory nerves largely intact.

In ALS, foot drop also tends to progress rather than hold steady. Weakness may later appear in the other leg or spread to the thigh and hip muscles.

A lightweight brace called an ankle-foot orthosis can hold the foot at a working angle. It can help reduce tripping and falls whatever the underlying cause.

Slurred, slow or effortful speech, known medically as dysarthria, can be the first noticeable sign of ALS in some people. It happens when the motor neurons that control the lips, tongue, soft palate, jaw and vocal cords begin to fail.

Dysarthria is a problem with the mechanics of speech, not with language. A person with dysarthria still knows what they want to say and can find the right words. The difficulty lies in physically forming the sounds clearly.

The changes often start subtly. Consonants that need precise tongue movements, such as "t," "d," "l" and "k," may become blurred. Speech can slow down. Some people find that talking takes more effort. Others notice that their voice tires after a long conversation or phone call.

A nasal quality is another common feature. When the soft palate weakens, it no longer seals off the nasal passage properly during speech. Air escapes through the nose and gives words a muffled or nasal sound. The voice itself may also become hoarse, strained or quieter.

ALS can damage both upper and lower motor neurons, and speech reflects that. Lower motor neuron damage tends to leave muscles weak and floppy. The result is breathy, nasal speech, a pattern clinicians call flaccid dysarthria. Upper motor neuron damage makes muscles stiff. That produces a strained, slow and harsh voice, known as spastic dysarthria. In ALS, features of both types often appear together, and clinicians describe the result as mixed dysarthria.

Family members, friends or colleagues sometimes notice the change before the person does. A frequent early clue is being asked to repeat things. Another is hearing that you sound tired, or as though you have been drinking.

Slurred speech has many causes besides ALS. Alcohol, certain medications, dental problems, stroke, multiple sclerosis, Parkinson's disease and myasthenia gravis can all affect speech. The timing matters. ALS-related dysarthria develops gradually and keeps progressing. Speech that becomes slurred suddenly, especially alongside facial drooping or arm weakness, is a stroke warning sign and needs emergency care.

When gradual speech changes raise concern, a neurologist typically examines the tongue, palate and facial muscles for weakness, wasting or stiffness. A speech-language pathologist can assess which parts of speech production are affected and track how they change over time.

Dysphagia, the medical term for difficulty swallowing, occurs in ALS when the motor neurons controlling the tongue, lips and throat weaken. It can appear early, especially when the disease starts in the bulbar muscles. It can also develop later as the disease spreads.

Swallowing is a tightly timed sequence. The lips seal, the tongue pushes food backward, and the throat muscles squeeze it toward the esophagus. At the same moment, the larynx lifts and the airway closes. Weakness at any point in that chain can let food or liquid slip where it should not go.

Early changes are often small. A person may cough or clear their throat during meals, or feel food sticking partway down. Thin liquids such as water are frequently harder to manage than thicker foods. They move quickly and give weakened muscles less time to react. Meals may take longer, and some people start avoiding certain textures.

Drooling can also develop. In ALS, it usually reflects less frequent swallowing of saliva and weaker lip closure, not extra saliva production. A wet or gurgly voice after drinking can signal that liquid is pooling near the vocal cords.

The main medical concern is aspiration, which happens when food, liquid or saliva enters the airway and lungs. Aspiration can lead to a lung infection called aspiration pneumonia. Swallowing trouble can also make it hard to take in enough calories and fluids. That raises the risk of weight loss and dehydration.

A speech-language pathologist typically evaluates swallowing. One common tool is a videofluoroscopic swallow study, also called a modified barium swallow. It records X $TWTR-ray video as a person swallows foods and liquids of different textures. Another option is a fiberoptic endoscopic evaluation of swallowing, known as FEES. It uses a thin, flexible camera passed through the nose to view the throat.

Early adjustments often focus on technique and texture. These can include sitting upright during meals, taking smaller bites, tucking the chin while swallowing and thickening liquids.

Dysphagia on its own does not point to ALS. Stroke, acid reflux, narrowing of the esophagus and many other conditions can cause it. Swallowing difficulty that persists or worsens calls for a medical evaluation to find the cause.

Neurologists often describe ALS by where its first symptoms appear. Limb-onset ALS starts in the arms or legs, while bulbar-onset ALS starts in the muscles that control speech, chewing and swallowing.

The word "bulbar" comes from an older anatomical name for the medulla oblongata, the lower part of the brainstem. Motor neurons there control the tongue, lips, jaw, throat and voice box. When those cells are affected first, the earliest changes show up in the face and throat rather than in the hands or feet.

Limb-onset ALS is the more common of the two patterns. It typically begins in a single arm or leg. The weakness is often uneven between the two sides of the body.

The labels describe a starting point, not a fixed category. ALS is progressive, and symptoms generally spread to other regions of the body over time. Someone whose disease begins in a leg may later develop bulbar symptoms. Someone with bulbar onset may later develop weakness in the limbs.

A smaller group of people first show weakness in the breathing muscles, a pattern sometimes called respiratory-onset ALS. In those cases, trouble breathing while lying flat can be one of the first clues.

The onset pattern shapes where people first go for help. Limb symptoms often lead to a visit with a primary care doctor or an orthopedic specialist. At that stage, the problem may be mistaken for carpal tunnel syndrome, arthritis or a spine problem. Bulbar symptoms may lead people first to an ear, nose and throat specialist, a dentist or a speech-language pathologist.

The pattern also guides early care planning. People with limb onset often begin with physical and occupational therapy. Braces, splints and mobility aids may follow as strength changes. People with bulbar onset tend to need earlier input from speech-language pathologists and dietitians. That input can include planning for communication devices before they are needed.

Both patterns reflect the same underlying disease. Each involves the loss of motor neurons. The same diagnostic process applies to both.

No single blood test or scan can confirm amyotrophic lateral sclerosis. Neurologists diagnose it by combining a detailed physical exam with electrical tests of nerves and muscles. They also rule out other conditions that can look similar.

The exam centers on two kinds of motor neurons. Upper motor neurons carry signals from the brain to the spinal cord. Damage to them tends to produce stiffness, overactive reflexes and a Babinski sign. In that sign, the big toe extends upward when the sole of the foot is stroked.

Lower motor neurons carry signals from the spinal cord to the muscles. Damage there causes weakness, muscle wasting and visible twitching. Finding signs of both types in the same body region, and seeing them spread to other regions, is central to an ALS diagnosis.

Electromyography, usually shortened to EMG, measures electrical activity inside muscles. A thin needle electrode is placed into several muscles. The neurologist then reads the signals while each muscle is at rest and while it contracts. In ALS, the recording can show denervation, meaning muscle fibers have lost their nerve supply. It can also show reinnervation, a sign that surviving nerves are sprouting new branches to take over orphaned muscle fibers. EMG can detect lower motor neuron damage in muscles that do not yet feel weak.

Nerve conduction studies are often done during the same visit. Small electrical pulses are applied to the skin, and sensors record how quickly and strongly signals travel along a nerve. In ALS, sensory nerves usually test normal. Sensory abnormalities, or a block in motor nerve conduction, can point toward other diagnoses, such as peripheral neuropathy or multifocal motor neuropathy.

MRI of the brain and spine helps exclude conditions that mimic ALS. These include compression of the spinal cord in the neck, tumors and multiple sclerosis. Blood and urine tests can check for thyroid problems, vitamin B12 deficiency and other treatable causes of weakness. Some patients also have a lumbar puncture or genetic testing.

Early findings can be subtle. For that reason, a diagnosis may require repeat exams over several months to show that symptoms are progressing. Neurologists often refer to published frameworks, such as the revised El Escorial criteria and the Gold Coast criteria, to standardize the process. Some patients seek a second opinion at a specialized ALS clinic before treatment planning begins.

Most people with ALS have the sporadic form, which appears with no known family history. A smaller group has familial ALS, which is inherited. Changes in the C9orf72 gene are among the most common known genetic causes of amyotrophic lateral sclerosis.

Sporadic ALS has no single identified cause. Its origins are not fully understood. Genetic susceptibility and environmental exposures may both play a part, but no one exposure has been confirmed as a direct cause.

Familial ALS usually follows an autosomal dominant pattern. In that pattern, one altered copy of a gene inherited from one parent can be enough to raise a person's risk. Each child of a parent who carries the change can inherit it. Inheriting it does not guarantee the disease will develop. Geneticists call this incomplete penetrance.

The C9orf72 change is a repeat expansion. A short, six-letter stretch of DNA, written as GGGGCC, is repeated far more times than usual. The same expansion is linked to frontotemporal dementia, a condition that affects behavior, language and decision-making. Some affected families include members with ALS, others with frontotemporal dementia and some with features of both.

Other genes tied to inherited motor neuron disease include SOD1, TARDBP and FUS. SOD1 was the first gene linked to ALS. TARDBP carries the instructions for a protein called TDP-43. That protein forms abnormal clumps inside the motor neurons of most people with ALS, including many who have no inherited mutation.

The line between the two forms is not always sharp. Genetic testing sometimes finds a C9orf72 expansion or another known mutation in a person with no family history of the disease. That can happen when relatives died young of other causes, were misdiagnosed or carried the change without developing symptoms.

In the clinic, familial and sporadic ALS usually look alike. A neurologist generally cannot tell them apart from symptoms or examination findings alone.

Genetic counselors help patients and relatives decide whether to be tested. They also explain what a result would mean and how to share it with family members. In the U.S., the Genetic Information Nondiscrimination Act bars genetic discrimination in health insurance and most employment. It does not cover life, disability or long-term care insurance.

There is no cure for amyotrophic lateral sclerosis, and no treatment reverses the loss of motor neurons. Care combines disease-modifying drugs such as riluzole and edaravone with coordinated support for breathing, nutrition, mobility and communication.

Riluzole is a pill taken by mouth, usually twice a day. It is thought to work by reducing the effects of glutamate, a chemical messenger that can overstimulate and damage nerve cells. The drug aims to slow the disease rather than restore strength that has already been lost. It can raise liver enzymes. Doctors typically order regular blood tests while a patient takes it. Liquid and film forms exist for people who have trouble swallowing tablets.

Edaravone is an antioxidant. It is designed to limit oxidative stress, a type of cellular damage linked to the breakdown of motor neurons. It can be given as an intravenous infusion on a repeating schedule of treatment days and drug-free periods. An oral suspension is also available. Access to each drug differs between countries. A patient in one place may be offered one medicine but not the other.

Medicine is only part of the plan. Many people with ALS are seen at specialized multidisciplinary clinics, where several specialists assess them during a single visit. A typical team can include a neurologist, a respiratory therapist, a dietitian, a speech-language pathologist, physical and occupational therapists and a social worker.

Breathing support becomes central as the diaphragm and chest muscles weaken. Noninvasive ventilation, delivered through a mask, can ease shortness of breath and improve sleep. Devices that assist coughing help clear secretions from the airways.

Nutrition is another focus. When eating becomes slow or unsafe, a feeding tube placed directly into the stomach, called a gastrostomy, can supply calories, fluids and medication.

Braces, walkers, wheelchairs and home modifications help with mobility and daily tasks. For communication, speech-language pathologists can introduce text-to-speech apps and eye-tracking devices. Some patients use voice banking. They record their own voice while speech is still clear. A computer can later use those recordings to speak for them.

Doctors also prescribe drugs aimed at specific symptoms, including muscle relaxants for spasticity and medicines that reduce excess saliva. These treatments do not change the course of the disease. They target the problems that shape comfort from day to day.

Weakness, speech changes or swallowing trouble that persists for weeks and slowly gets worse is a reason to book a medical appointment. A primary care doctor can examine the problem first and refer the patient to a neurologist, the specialist who evaluates diseases of the brain, spinal cord and nerves.

How symptoms behave over time matters more than any single episode. A muscle that feels weak after a hard workout, or a voice that sounds hoarse during a cold, usually recovers within days. Symptoms that do not ease with rest, or that spread to a new area of the body, call for a closer look.

Most people with these complaints do not have ALS. Pinched nerves in the neck or lower back, thyroid disease, vitamin B12 deficiency and myasthenia gravis can all produce weakness or speech changes. Several of these conditions are treatable. An early evaluation gives a doctor the chance to identify them.

In the U.K. and many other countries with public health systems, a GP referral is the standard route to a neurologist. In the U.S., some insurance plans also require a referral before a patient can see a specialist.

Written notes prepared before the appointment help the doctor. Useful details include when the problem started, which body part was affected first and whether it has stayed the same or changed. A record of daily tasks that have become harder gives the clinician concrete examples to work from.

Family medical history is also relevant. Patients should mention any relatives diagnosed with ALS, motor neuron disease or frontotemporal dementia. These conditions can share genetic links. A current list of medications and supplements completes the picture.

A relative or friend can come along to the appointment. A second person can take notes, ask questions and remember details that are easy to miss in a short consultation.

If a neurologist suspects ALS, the evaluation may take several visits and more than one round of testing. Some patients ask for a second opinion at a specialized ALS clinic or neuromuscular center. A neuromuscular specialist can confirm or revisit an earlier diagnosis.

Eurorivali: il Ferencvaros non perde allo scorso 27 luglio

📅 Nov 03, 2026  |  Category: Business  |  Read Source →

Il Ferencvaros è il prossimo avversario del Milan in Europa League, giovedì alle 18.45 a San Siro in occasione della quarta giornata della League Phase della competizione. Se sulla carta la bilancia pende nettamente dalla parte della formazione rossonera, sul campo la formazione di Ruben Amorim non dovrà nè potrà abbassare la guardia al cospetto di una squadra che sta avendo un inizio di stagione davvero impressionante: basti pensare che gli ungheresi hanno già giocato 24 gare stagionali e ne hanno persa solamente una, in OTP Bank Liga contro il Paks lo scorso 27 luglio.

In Europa League il Ferencvaros si presenta come prima della classe: tre vittorie su tre partite, contro Celtic, Viktoria Plzen e Torreense. Al contrario il Milan non può concedersi errori dopo le due sconfitte nelle prime tre uscite, se non vuole complicarsi ulteriormente la vita. Domani, giornata di vigilia della sfida, il Ferencvaros si allenerà alle ore 11 in Ungheria, con i primi 15 minuti che saranno trasmessi in diretta sul canale YouTube del club. Poi la partenza per Milano dove, nel tardo pomeriggio, si terrà la conferenza stampa a San Siro.

The countries that don't have a military — and who protects them

📅 Nov 03, 2026  |  Category: Business  |  Read Source →

Free daily briefing on global business news.

From Costa Rica to Vanuatu, 20 countries on a CIA list of states with no regular military, and how some lean on allies for defense.

Most governments treat an army as a fixture of statehood, as routine as a flag or a currency. Budgets assume it, neighbors watch it, and politicians argue over how large it should grow. A small set of countries looked at that bargain and turned it down. Their choice raises a question that sounds simple and proves stubborn. What keeps a country safe once it stops paying for soldiers, or never starts? The answer reaches beyond the capitals involved. Taxpayers in every armed country underwrite a defense establishment, and the existence of states that function without one invites a hard look at what all that money buys.

The conventional path carries a steep bill. The CIA reported that military spending absorbed 2.42% of global GDP in 2012, about $2 trillion. Against an outlay of that size, a country without troops looks frugal. The "no military" label hides a messier reality. With few exceptions, countries the CIA lists as lacking a military run some kind of security service, and those services differ in how much they confine themselves to threats at home. Some of these countries lean on larger partners to guard against outside danger. The boundary between a police force and a fighting force can blur, and where an observer draws it decides which countries qualify as unarmed.

Defense One published Kathy Gilsinan's survey of these countries on Nov. 12, 2014. The piece draws on the CIA World Factbook's roster of independent countries that keep no regular military forces. The Factbook's tally lands at 22 or 23, depending on how a reader classifies Vatican City's mostly ceremonial Swiss Guard, and the piece prints 23 names. Autonomous territories such as Puerto Rico and the Cayman Islands fall outside the roster, and counting them would add many entries. The piece offers no scoring system or ranking. It explains why some countries gave up the armies they once had and quotes Stearns, who said there is "no perfect measure" of lacking a military. This article covers 20 of the 23 countries the piece names.

José Figueres Ferrer, Costa Rica's president at the time, pushed in 1948 to abolish the country's military. His campaign put Costa Rica among the countries on this list that once kept armed forces and later shed them. The decision originated at home. Grenada and Panama, by contrast, lost their armies to U.S. invasions. Costa Rica's government chose to disarm itself, with no foreign power forcing its hand.

The Council on Hemispheric Affairs points out that Figueres took power by armed revolt. The same group suggests he may have dissolved the forces "in order to avoid a future potential military coup against him." Taken together, the two observations cast the abolition as a guard against the kind of uprising that had elevated Figueres. A leader who had seized office by force had cause to close that route to future rivals. On this reading, the move served domestic politics as much as foreign policy. Soldiers posed a risk to the man in charge, and disbanding them erased the danger.

Shedding its troops did not lift Costa Rica out of a hostile neighborhood. The country lies along a major drug-smuggling route in a dangerous part of the world. It has also feuded for years with Nicaragua, its neighbor to the north, over the line between them. Smuggling and a frontier dispute both test a state's ability to control its own territory. Neither problem vanished with the soldiers.

Costa Rica's exposure illustrates why people argue at length over what counts as a military. Traffickers moving through its territory and a contested northern frontier keep that question alive. Any agency that confronts those pressures takes on work that soldiers might handle elsewhere. The country gave up its army without escaping the threats an army confronts. Figueres shielded his government from an internal coup, but the external hazards outlasted the force he dissolved.

Grenada's army came to an end after a U.S. invasion in 1983. The country belongs to a short roster of places that once maintained armed forces and later abandoned them. Its path to that status ran through an outside power's intervention. That route sets Grenada apart from former U.S.-administered countries such as the Marshall Islands and Palau, which never organized troops at all after independence.

Grenada's loss of its army came six years before Panama followed a similar course after the 1989 U.S. invasion. Gilsinan's piece cites the two countries together as cases where an army disappeared in the wake of American military action. Their shared history shows that demilitarization on this list did not always begin as a domestic choice.

Grenada kept no regular military forces in 2014, 31 years after the invasion. Three decades without a rebuilt army suggest the change outlasted the invasion that preceded it.

Panama's army disappeared after the U.S. invasion of 1989, which puts the country among the listed states that fielded soldiers before going without them. Outside force produced the change. No domestic reform or vote figures in the record.

Costa Rica reached the same condition 41 years earlier by the opposite route. A president at home drove its 1948 abolition. A foreign power's intervention brought about Panama's.

Grenada supplies the closer parallel. Its army also fell after an American invasion, six years before Panama's. Both countries lost their national forces in the wake of U.S. military action, and the two stand as a distinct pair among the cases that carry a known date.

Panama's history also sets it apart from former U.S.-administered territories such as the Marshall Islands and Palau. Those island countries never built militaries after independence and handed their defense to the U.S. Panama had an army, and it vanished once American troops arrived. Two different relationships with Washington ended in the same unarmed status.

Stearns called a commitment to avoid external violence "an interesting step." Panama's account turns on an invasion, and no pledge of restraint enters it. Its demilitarization reflects a foreign power's action.

Panama's 1989 date falls last among the three dated cases. The most recent recorded loss of an army on this roster followed a foreign invasion.

This slide runs about 225 words, short of the 300-word minimum. The source gives Panama a single fact (its army ended after the 1989 U.S. invasion), and the approved web details contain nothing on Panama, so no genuine fact remains to extend it without padding.

The Marshall Islands never raised a military of its own after independence and left its defense to the U.S., the country that once administered it. Palau followed the same path. The two belong to a group of former U.S.-administered territories that skipped building armed forces once they won sovereignty.

The islands arrived at their unarmed status by a different road than Costa Rica, Grenada, and Panama. Each of those three dismantled an existing army, through a president's push in one case and in the aftermath of a U.S. invasion in the other two. The Marshall Islands had no troops to disband. Its reliance on the U.S. carried over from the administrative era into self-rule. The country never faced a moment of choosing to disarm.

The source names an outside protector for few of the countries it lists, and the Marshall Islands is among them. Its security rests with Washington. Anyone asking who would answer a foreign threat to the islands gets a direct reply.

Handing that job to a partner frees the islands from the cost of a standing force. Defense One described going without an army as "potentially a cost-effective step." The Marshall Islands tests that idea in its plainest form. It never spent money to build troops, so it never had to weigh the savings of cutting them.

Stearns said a country that commits to avoiding external violence has "taken an interesting step." The Marshall Islands fits that description only in part. Gilsinan's account describes a transfer of responsibility to the U.S. and says nothing about the islands renouncing arms as a principle. The country's lack of soldiers reflects a choice about who fights on its behalf. The islands' safety depends less on institutions at home than on the durability of their bond with the country that once governed them.

Palau left U.S. administration and became an independent country without ever raising an army of its own. It handed responsibility for its defense to the U.S., the power that had governed it.

Palau never had soldiers to send home. Costa Rica scrapped its military through a president's push, and Grenada and Panama lost theirs after U.S. invasions. Palau skipped the building stage, so its unarmed status reaches back to its first day as a sovereign state.

Palau supplies one of the clearer answers to the question of who guards a country without troops. Washington carries the duty. The Marshall Islands, another territory the U.S. once administered, took the same path, which points to a pattern among islands that left American oversight with no forces of their own.

Palau's sovereignty and its reliance on the U.S. began at the same moment, so the country has never stood guard over itself.

Iceland belongs to NATO and joins international peacekeeping missions through what the CIA calls "a civilian-led Crisis Response Unit." Its seat in the alliance gives one of the few direct answers to the question of who stands behind a country with no regular armed forces.

Iceland holds that seat without fielding troops. The team it sends to overseas operations answers to civilian leaders. The country takes part in collective missions through that unit instead of through a standing force, and it keeps its place on the CIA World Factbook's roster of countries without a military.

Iceland's case splits two jobs that an army tends to bundle, guarding the homeland and serving abroad. NATO membership ties the country's security to its allies. The crisis unit covers the outward-facing work and points Iceland toward peacekeeping missions far from its shores.

The arrangement undercuts the assumption that a state without soldiers must stay out of security work beyond its borders. Iceland answers a question an army normally settles by itself, and it answers with an alliance for protection and a civilian team for service overseas.

A country can skip the cost of an army and keep a hand in international peacekeeping.

Vatican City entrusts its security to a mostly ceremonial Swiss Guard, and how an observer classifies that corps decides whether the Vatican belongs among the unarmed at all. Accounts have Joseph Stalin deriding the Catholic Church's lack of physical might with one question: "The pope! How many divisions has he got?" The jab equated power with troops. By that yardstick, the pope came up empty, with no divisions for anyone to tally.

Stalin's taunt exposes a gap in his own reasoning. He bothered to belittle a figure who led no troops, which suggests the pope held a kind of standing that a census of soldiers could not register. The Vatican's inclusion makes the same point. A state can matter enough to mock without fielding a single division.

The Swiss Guard turns the Vatican into the most contested entry of all. The corps' work leans toward pageantry, yet a reader who treats it as a fighting force arrives at a different headcount from one who sees it as ornament. Of all the security units these countries maintain, the survey singles out this one as able to swing the total by a full country. The Vatican marks the spot where ceremony and defense become hard to separate, and the line anyone draws there settles whether the roster holds one name more or one fewer.

Every other country in the survey would sit beside the pope with nothing to show by Stalin's standard. Each of them remains independent. Their endurance suggests troop counts miss whatever keeps these states intact.

Slide 7 (Vatican City) runs about 256 words, short of the 300-word minimum. The source supports only the Stalin quip, the Vatican's membership in the group without armed forces, and the mostly ceremonial Swiss Guard's effect on the 22-versus-23 count, which the introduction already states. No approved web details cover Vatican City, and the source notes conflict on which way the Guard's classification moves the tally, so that detail stays out.

Tuvalu maintains no regular military forces, according to the CIA World Factbook.

Gilsinan's survey supplies no backstory for Tuvalu's unarmed status. The piece recounts how Costa Rica, Grenada, and Panama dismantled armies they once fielded. It describes the Marshall Islands and Palau as former U.S.-administered territories that never raised forces after independence. Tuvalu falls into neither group in her telling, and the survey never says whether the country once had soldiers to give up.

Tuvalu lacks a named protector in the piece. Gilsinan identifies the U.S. as the defender of the Marshall Islands and Palau and notes Iceland's NATO membership, yet she assigns Tuvalu no outside guarantor. The country's security arrangements, whatever form they take, sit beyond what the survey reports.

Tuvalu's case shows how far the CIA's tally stretches past the handful of stories that explain it. A place on the roster confirms that no regular army exists and reveals nothing about why, or about who fills the gap.

Nauru keeps no regular military forces and holds a place on the CIA World Factbook's roster of independent countries without an army.

The listing records the absence and goes no further. The survey gives no year when Nauru's forces ended, or whether the country ever had any. It ties the gap to no invasion, coup fear, or political decision. Several other entries carry histories of that kind. Nauru's bare listing leaves its route to an unarmed state unexplained.

Nauru has no named guardian in the record. The survey credits the U.S. with defending the Marshall Islands and Palau and links Iceland to NATO. No comparable partner appears beside Nauru. The evidence leaves open who would answer an outside threat to the country.

Stearns said a country that commits to avoid external violence has "taken an interesting step." Nothing in the record shows whether Nauru made such a commitment or never built the means to fight beyond its borders. Its listing confirms the absence of regular forces and leaves the motive behind it unrecorded.

San Marino keeps no regular military forces, and the CIA World Factbook includes it among the independent countries that go without them.

Andorra keeps no regular military forces, and the CIA World Factbook puts it among the independent countries that go without them. CNBC reports that the Factbook credits Spain and France with providing for Andorra's defense.

Two outside powers stand behind Andorra, an arrangement that sets it apart from the Marshall Islands and Palau. Each of those former U.S.-administered countries left its defense to one protector, the U.S. Andorra's security rests on a pair of governments. Repelling a foreign threat falls to them, not to any force Andorra fields itself.

Andorra skips the expense of a standing army and can turn to two named states for protection. Most entries in this selection come with no identified guardian on record, which makes Andorra's case one of the more direct answers to the question of who keeps an unarmed country safe.

Dominica turns up on the CIA World Factbook's tally of independent countries with no regular military forces. The listing comes without a backstory. No abolition date, invasion, or founding decision explains how the island joined the group, and no ally appears as its guarantor.

Stearns judged that a country committing to avoid external violence has "taken an interesting step." His remark treats the absence of an army as a stance toward the outside world. Dominica's entry records the absence itself but does not show whether the island made such a pledge or never built a force in the first place.

Gilsinan describes going without an army as "potentially a cost-effective step." The word "potentially" matters here. Any savings depend on what a country spends in place of troops, and Dominica's listing offers no figure for either side of that ledger.

Dominica's entry establishes that the island has no army and nothing more, leaving both its savings and its protector unrecorded.

This slide runs about 140 words, short of the 300-word minimum. The source supports only Dominica's list membership, and no approved web details cover Dominica.

Haiti stands among the independent countries that the CIA World Factbook records as having no regular military forces.

Slide 13 (Haiti) falls short of the 300-word minimum at 18 words. The source names Haiti only as a member of its list. It gives no detail on how Haiti came to lack a military, what security service it runs, or who handles its outside defense. No approved web details cover Haiti. Expanding the slide would require padding or facts from outside the evidence.

Kiribati goes without regular military forces, and the CIA World Factbook includes it on its list of independent countries that have no standing army.

Police officers carry out Kiribati's law enforcement at sea. The Kiribati Police Service operates a Maritime Unit for work on the water, and the unit's base sits in Tarawa, the country's capital.

Kiribati's sea patrols answer to a police command. Its offshore enforcement shows one concrete shape a security service can take in a country with no troops.

This slide runs about 70 words, short of the 300-word minimum. The primary source gives Kiribati nothing beyond its place on the CIA list. The Kiritimati Island detail was cut because it does not explain the slide's finding without an invented link to the Maritime Unit's reach. No further supported fact was available, so the slide was not padded.

Liechtenstein keeps no regular military forces, and the CIA World Factbook includes it among the independent countries that go without one.

Mauritius goes without regular military forces, a status that earns it a place on the CIA World Factbook's roster of independent countries with no army.

The UN's office in Mauritius describes training built around maritime security operations. The sessions covered detecting vessels, boarding ships within the bounds of the law, keeping maritime assets in working order, and prosecuting offenses committed at sea.

Each subject on that syllabus concerns policing the water, and none involves combat. The skill set belongs to law enforcement, which matches the profile of a country that keeps no troops.

Micronesia keeps no regular armed forces and appears on the CIA World Factbook's roster of independent countries without a standing army. Two other countries on that roster, Palau and the Marshall Islands, share more with it than the absence of troops.

The Centers for Disease Control and Prevention groups the Federated States of Micronesia with Palau and the Marshall Islands as the three freely associated states its Office of Island Affairs works with. Each is a sovereign country bound to the U.S. by a formal pact called a Compact of Free Association.

The compact governs diplomatic, economic, and military relations between the U.S. and each freely associated state. Micronesia's agreement reaches into defense matters even though the country fields no army of its own. Its security ties to the U.S. sit in the same document as its diplomatic and economic dealings with Washington.

Micronesia's two compact partners on the list left their defense to the U.S. after independence. It holds the same status under the same kind of pact. Every freely associated state among the countries without an army pairs that absence with a written agreement covering its military ties to the U.S.

Monaco keeps no regular military forces, and the CIA World Factbook includes it among the independent countries that go without them.

Monaco functions as a constitutional monarchy on the Mediterranean coast. Its land frontier meets France. The country covers a tiny territory.

Monaco shares the Factbook roster with four other European states: Andorra, Iceland, Liechtenstein, and San Marino. Those five give Europe a sizable cluster among the unarmed countries on the list, alongside the island states of the Pacific and the Caribbean.

Samoa sits on the CIA World Factbook's list of independent countries with no regular military forces. The survey prints the name and nothing more, offering no account of when Samoa went without an army or why.

Samoa's path to its present status ran through trusteeship. Western Samoa had held the standing of a trust territory before it adopted the name Samoa. New Zealand helped the country manage its international relations.

New Zealand's documented role covers diplomacy, a separate job from guarding borders. Neither the survey nor the trusteeship record identifies any country as Samoa's protector. The Marshall Islands and Palau, by contrast, handed their security to the U.S. after independence. Their arrangement supplies a clear answer where Samoa's leaves a blank.

Samoa's case exposes the limits of a bare entry on a roster. Without a named guardian, the evidence cannot show whether Samoa relies on a partner, a domestic force, or neither. Its lack of soldiers stands as an established fact, and its safeguard remains unexplained.

Shortfall: this slide runs about 165 words, well under the 300-word minimum. The primary source supplies only Samoa's name in its list, and the single approved web page supplies only the trusteeship name change and New Zealand's support with international relations. No further genuine fact is available, so the slide stops where the substance ends.

Vanuatu keeps no regular military forces, which puts it on the CIA World Factbook's list of independent countries that go without a standing army. Its listing arrives with no account of an abolished army, a U.S. invasion, or a guard kept for ceremony, the stories behind Costa Rica, Grenada, and Vatican City. The country's protection comes into focus through the countries it works alongside.

Vanuatu's law enforcement network reaches across several governments. ABC News reports that the country's police minister said Vanuatu had memorandums of understanding or cooperation agreements with Australia, New Zealand, France, the United Kingdom, and PNG. China stood at the time as the lone country with which Vanuatu needed to formalize one. The five signatories spread Vanuatu's outside backing across a group of governments instead of resting it on a single guardian.

Australia holds a standing above the rest. ABC News reports that a joint statement from the Australian and Vanuatu governments described Australia as Vanuatu's main policing partner over many years. Both governments put their names to that description. It reflects Vanuatu's own view as well as Australia's. Vanuatu treats its Nakamal Agreement with Australia as binding in law and open-ended, according to the statement. Vanuatu describes its police arrangement with China as limited in duration and expects it to shrink. The wording shows which relationship Vanuatu expects to endure. Across the two reports, China moves from holdout to short-term partner.

The policing memorandums deal with civilian officers, and China's role so far centers on training and equipping them. The Nakamal Agreement reaches further. The ABC's 2025 report describes it as a defense pact between Australia and Vanuatu, a tie that extends Vanuatu's security bond with Australia past police work. The Marshall Islands and Palau handed their defense to the U.S. Vanuatu instead spreads its policing ties across several governments and pairs them with a defense pact with one. Nor does the reporting place Vanuatu in an alliance such as NATO, which counts Iceland as a member. On the available evidence, Vanuatu's answer to who protects it runs through Australia first, as both its main policing partner and the other party to its defense pact.
