The countries with the most islands — and why Sweden beats Indonesia

📅 Nov 03, 2026  |  Category: Business  |  Read Source →

Free daily briefing on global business news.

Sweden, Norway, and Finland report 704,773 islands between them. See how 10 countries compare, from Canada to the Philippines.

A scrap of land ringed by water can become a world of its own. Cut off from the mainland, species follow separate evolutionary paths, and many island plants and animals exist nowhere else. Seabirds raise their young on island nesting grounds, and migrating flocks stop on them to rest during long journeys. Humans depend on these places as well. More than 600 million people live on islands, where distinct cultures and ways of earning a living stay tied to the surrounding ecosystems. Each island, however small, can matter to wildlife and communities out of proportion to its size.

Most readers who picture a country of islands imagine Indonesia or the Philippines, with their warm seas and sprawling tropical archipelagos. The image holds some truth, yet it skips a harder question about what qualifies as an island at all. A bare rock in a lake, a skerry off a jagged shore, and a landmass large enough for towns can all carry the same label in everyday speech. Governments do not agree on where the label starts. Some set a minimum size, some measure the shoreline, and some ignore freshwater. Each choice can swell or shrink an official tally by a wide margin. These standards open a gap between a country's reputation as an island realm and what it reports on paper. Making sense of any national number means knowing the rules behind it.

Visual Capitalist compiled island counts for 94 countries in an analysis by Bruno Venditti, published Oct. 31, 2026, that covers 945,247 islands in all. The publication warns that this sum does not amount to a uniform global census, since every country writes its own definition. Sweden admits any island of 9 square meters or more, and Norway draws its line at about 10 square meters. Both include inland islands. Japan requires a circumference of at least 100 meters and excludes artificial islands as well as those in lakes and rivers. Canada's figure leaves out everything but its coastal islands. The analysis adds that these differences keep the national counts from lining up for direct comparison. This article examines 10 of the 94 countries, moving from the largest reported count down, and treats each count as a record that local definitions shape as much as geography does.

Sweden reports 267,570 islands, which gives it 28.31% of the dataset's total and a larger tally than any other country on the list. More than one of every four islands on record lies within Swedish borders. Norway, the nearest challenger, falls 28,513 short. Sweden and Norway together report 506,627 islands, more than half of all islands counted. The margin between the two Nordic neighbors alone outnumbers Indonesia's islands.

Sweden's total dwarfs Indonesia's 17,508 by a factor of about 15.3, a difference of 250,062 islands. The gulf between those two countries is wider than Finland's whole tally, the third-highest in the table. Sweden surpasses Canada, Chile, the U.S., Indonesia, Japan, Australia, and the Philippines put together. The seven report 171,871 islands between them, nearly 100,000 fewer than Sweden. Indonesia's reported figure equals less than 7% of Sweden's.

Some 30,000 islands, islets, and skerries fan out from Stockholm into the Baltic Sea, forming the Stockholm Archipelago. This single cluster exceeds every island the U.S. reports and tops Indonesia's total as well. Still, the archipelago supplies about one-ninth of Sweden's islands, leaving more than 230,000 scattered beyond Stockholm's waters.

Sweden's other islands span rocky shores, protected seas, and freshwater lakes. Smooth rock formations and fishing villages with red boathouses define the Bohuslän coastline. Kosterhavet National Park holds the distinction of being Sweden's only marine national park. Inland, Lake Vänern, Sweden's largest lake, contains the Lurö archipelago, the biggest freshwater island group in Europe.

Rising land reshapes the High Coast, a Unesco World Heritage site, where existing islands grow larger and new islets break the surface. Sweden's island map there remains unfinished. Sheep and cattle graze several islands in the Östgöta archipelago, and sea eagles are a common sight there. Sweden's lead draws on islands across sea and lake alike, and the High Coast shows the land itself can keep adding to them.

Norway's 239,057 islands make up 25.29% of every island in the dataset. About one in four counted islands falls within Norwegian borders.

The 91 countries outside Sweden, Finland, and Norway report 240,474 islands among them. Norway trails that sum by 1,417 islands, a gap smaller than Thailand's whole tally. One country comes within a whisker of matching the combined holdings of 91 others. The group on the other side of the scale stretches from Cuba and the Bahamas to Fiji, Tonga, and the Solomon Islands.

Norway outnumbers the seven countries next in the table by 67,186 islands. Canada, Chile, the U.S., Indonesia, Japan, Australia, and the Philippines hold 171,871 between them. Canada, the strongest of the seven at 52,455 islands, reaches about 22% of the Norwegian figure. Norway's surplus over the group exceeds Canada's entire count. Indonesia and the Philippines, the two countries the source credits with reputations for vast tropical archipelagos, sit inside that outnumbered group.

Rocky islands divided by narrow channels define the Øygarden Islands west of Bergen. Thin strips of water run between the stone and split the shoreline into many separate pieces of land. A coast fractured this way can pack a large number of distinct islands into a short stretch of sea.

Remove Norway from the list and 706,190 islands would remain, with Sweden and Finland still holding about 66% of them.

Finland reports 198,146 islands, or 20.96% of the 945,247 in the dataset. About one island in every five on the list falls within Finnish borders. The Finnish tally trails Norway by 40,911 and Sweden by 69,424, gaps that shrink to footnotes beside the plunge that follows.

A steep cliff separates Finland from the rest of the table. Canada, the next country and the first outside the Nordic region, follows with 52,455, a decline of 145,691 islands. Finland's figure runs about 3.8 times the Canadian one. Chile, the second non-Nordic entry at 43,471, musters less than a quarter of Finland's count. The seven countries after Finland in this selection, from Canada through the Philippines, post 171,871 islands together, and Finland exceeds that joint sum by 26,275.

The Archipelago Sea packs tens of thousands of islands into a single body of water. Even the low end of that range, 20,000, would top the full national totals of Indonesia, at 17,508, and Japan, at 14,125. On that reading, one Finnish sea outnumbers four of the 10 countries covered here, Australia and the Philippines among them. Indonesia and the Philippines, the archipelagos most readers picture first, log 25,149 islands between them, less than one-seventh of Finland's haul. Finland's total by itself comes to about 11.3 times Indonesia's.

Islands crowd Finland's inland lakes as well as its sea. In the heart of Lakeland, Linnansaari National Park spreads across a maze of lush islands and rocky islets that shelters the endangered Saimaa ringed seal and the osprey. Southern Konnevesi National Park adds lakes threaded with sheltered island labyrinths amid herb-rich forests.

Finland completes a Nordic group of three whose combined total reaches 704,773, a 74.6% share of all counted islands. Spread the remainder across the other 91 countries in the table and they hold 240,474 islands among them. Finland's 198,146 alone equals more than four-fifths of that whole pool and falls 42,328 islands short of matching it.

Canada posts 52,455 islands, a 5.55% share of the dataset that earns it fourth place behind the Nordic trio. Its count beats Chile's 43,471 by 8,984 and runs about 1.8 times the 28,449 islands the U.S. lists. Canada is the only country beyond Sweden, Norway, and Finland to top 50,000, and no other country in the Americas approaches Canada's figure. The Canadian sum exceeds the 39,274 islands that Indonesia, Japan, and the Philippines report together. Even so, Sweden reports about five times as many islands.

More than 36,000 islands crowd the Canadian Arctic Archipelago, one of the country's largest concentrations of island terrain. Baffin Island, one of the biggest islands on Earth, belongs to the group. The Arctic chain on its own surpasses the full U.S. tally and more than doubles Indonesia's 17,508. It exceeds Japan's and the Philippines' combined 21,766 too. One northern region of Canada outstrips a country whose tropical archipelagos shape the popular picture of island geography.

Georgian Bay, an arm of Lake Huron in Ontario, contains more than 30,000 freshwater islands, and none of them appear in Canada's official record. The bay forms one piece of the country's extensive freshwater island geography, and the rest of those inland islands goes uncounted as well. Georgian Bay alone shows that Canada's total could climb by a wide margin if inland islands entered the reckoning.

Adding Georgian Bay's islands would lift Canada past 82,000, a jump of more than 57% over the published number. The bay outnumbers the 25,730 islands of Indonesia and Australia put together, and it has more than twice Japan's 14,125. The added islands would push Canada above the 71,920 that Chile and the U.S. claim between them. A single Ontario bay left out of the books holds more islands than all but five countries in the table report.

Chile reports 43,471 islands, or 4.60% of the 945,247 in the dataset, more than any other South American country in the table. The total puts Chile fifth, 8,984 islands short of Canada and 15,022 clear of the U.S. Its count sits closer to Canada's than to the American tally, which aligns Chile with the high-count group more than with the long tail below it.

The rest of South America reports a sliver of Chile's total. Brazil posts the continent's next-highest number at 1,200, followed by Venezuela at 650, Guyana at 365, Peru at 233, Argentina at 152, and Colombia at 74. Those six countries log 2,674 islands between them, and Chile outnumbers that whole group about 16 times over. Chile's islands exceed Brazil's by about 36 to 1 and Venezuela's by about 67 to 1.

Chile also surpasses the countries most readers picture as island nations. Its tally runs about 2.5 times Indonesia's and more than five times the Philippines'.

The table's seven South American entries hold 46,145 islands combined, and Chile accounts for about 94% of them.

The U.S. claims 28,449 islands, a 3.01% share of the dataset that leaves it 15,022 short of Chile's 43,471.

The American count outstrips the two countries best known for tropical archipelagos. It exceeds Indonesia's 17,508 by 10,941 and runs about 3.7 times the Philippines' 7,641. Indonesia and the Philippines together reach 25,149, which is 3,300 fewer than the U.S. reports alone. A country seldom pictured as an island realm thus outnumbers the two places most readers would name first.

Sweden's 267,570 islands come to about 9.4 times the U.S. tally, a gap that dwarfs any margin between the U.S. and the countries below it. A total larger than two famous island countries combined translates to one island in every 33 in the dataset. Outpacing the best-known archipelagos buys the U.S. little ground against the Nordic counts that dominate the record.

Indonesia records 17,508 islands, a 1.85% share of the dataset and enough to earn seventh place among the 94 countries on the list. Its fame for sprawling tropical archipelagos, a reputation it shares with the Philippines, does not lift it to the top spot.

Indonesia's total amounts to about one island in every 54 the dataset includes. The country reports more than twice the 7,641 islands of the Philippines, the other country famed for tropical islands. Japan, the next country down, has 3,383 fewer islands. China, at 7,226, holds less than half of Indonesia's number. Australia and the Philippines, the two countries after Japan, report 15,863 islands combined, a sum below the Indonesian figure.

Indonesia's tally outweighs the rest of Southeast Asia in the table. Its islands top the 17,117 that the region's eight other listed countries post between them, a group that runs from the Philippines, Laos, and Vietnam down to Singapore's 64 and Brunei's 33. Indonesia accounts for more than half of the 34,625 islands across the region's nine entries.

Karst formations, clear water, and coral reefs define Raja Ampat, where visitors dive, snorkel, and hop from one island to the next. Waigeo is the biggest of the group's four main islands. Boats from the port at Sorong reach Waisai on Waigeo in roughly two to three hours, according to Indonesia Travel.

Dense forest and mangrove swamps blanket Misool Island, and limestone pinnacles climb out of the surrounding sea to its east and west. Coconut palms fringe its white sand beaches.

As a pair, the two famous island countries trail the U.S. Indonesia by itself sits 10,941 islands behind the American count. Adding the Philippines lifts the pair to 25,149, a 2.66% share of the dataset, but they remain 3,300 short of the 28,449 the U.S. claims on its own.

Japan counts 14,125 islands, a 1.49% share of the dataset that outstrips every East Asian neighbor in the table. China comes closest with 7,226, leaving Japan with almost twice China's total. North Korea lists 3,579 islands, South Korea follows with 3,358, and Taiwan records 168. Japan holds more than 84 islands for every one in Taiwan.

Japan's tally falls 38 islands short of the 14,163 that China, North Korea, and South Korea reach between them. Adding Taiwan's 168 lifts the four to 14,331, 206 more than Japan. A single country rivals the combined holdings of four countries in its corner of Asia.

Japan's figure trails Indonesia's 17,508 by 3,383 and exceeds Australia's 8,222 by 5,903. It also approaches double the 7,641 islands of the Philippines. The United Kingdom's 6,346 and Greece's 6,000 add up to 12,346, still 1,779 below Japan. Chile's 43,471 runs more than three times Japan's. Sweden's 267,570 outnumbers Japan almost 19 times over. Japan's chain dwarfs its region yet looks slight beside the Nordic trio.

The Seto Inland Sea lies between three of Japan's main islands, Honshu, Shikoku, and Kyushu. Setonaikai National Park stands as the country's earliest and biggest national park, according to Japan.travel. Its terrain runs from pale sandy shores to thick pine woods. Setonaikai spans separate, unconnected zones that take in many of the inland sea's most familiar spots. Naoshima appeals to art lovers, Miyajima to visitors curious about Japanese religion, the Shimanami Kaido to cyclists, and Shodoshima to anyone wanting a relaxed island stay.

Omishima Island sits along the Shimanami Kaido cycling route, where shrines, citrus groves, and small fishing villages share the ground with the Toyo Ito Museum of Architecture. The Setouchi Islands offer mild weather, clean beaches, and bright blue water a short trip from the nearby cities of Okayama, Takamatsu, Hiroshima, and Kobe.

Australia registers 8,222 islands, a 0.87% share of the dataset and enough for ninth place. Fewer than one in every 100 islands in the tally belongs to Australia. The share sounds slight, yet it represents thousands of separate pieces of land.

Australia sits nearer to the countries trailing it than to the one ahead. It edges past the Philippines by 581 islands and clears China, the next entry in the table, by 996. Japan, one place higher, lists 5,903 more islands than Australia. The cushion below Australia is roughly one-tenth the size of the deficit above it. A modest revision to the Australian or Philippine figure could reverse their order, but catching Japan would take a far larger shift.

Australia's total looks modest beside the leaders. Indonesia posts a little more than twice as many islands, and Sweden more than 32 times as many. Still, Australia finishes ahead of the Philippines, one of the two countries most readers picture first when they imagine a land of warm seas and tropical islands. A reputation for islands, in other words, offers a poor guide to where a country lands in the reported counts.

Most of the Whitsunday Islands lie within national parks and have no residents, according to Australia.com. Business events groups can book stays on four of them: Hayman Island, Hamilton Island, Daydream Island, and Long Island.

Australia.com describes Western Australia's Kimberley as home to thousands of islands under tropical forest, along with tall ochre cliffs and galleries of rock art. Settlement there ranks among the sparsest anywhere on Earth. The rock art records a human presence in a region where people now live far apart.

Both the Whitsundays and the Kimberley point to the same pattern. Many of Australia's islands lie in protected or remote places that hold scant permanent population, or none.

The Philippines holds 7,641 islands, a 0.81% share of the total and the smallest count among the 10 countries in this selection. By that measure, the dataset assigns fewer than one island in every 100 to the Philippines. The archipelago lies between the South China Sea to the west and the Philippine Sea to the east. Open water on opposite sides frames the islands, a setting that fits the common image of an archipelago even though the Philippine count trails every other entry in this group.

The analysis pairs the Philippines with Indonesia as countries known for vast tropical archipelagos, and it notes that neither one claims first place. Indonesia's 17,508 islands exceed the Philippine figure by 9,867. Combined, the two countries hold 25,149 islands, or 2.66% of the dataset. Chile by itself outnumbers the pair. The two archipelagos that dominate the popular imagination account for a sliver of the whole, and one South American country surpasses them both.

Australia's total runs 581 islands above the Philippine mark, and China's falls 415 short. The United Kingdom lists 6,346 islands, or 1,295 fewer, and Greece reports 6,000, a gap of 1,641. Neighbors this close place the Philippines in a crowded stretch of the table, where modest gaps separate one country from the next. Beyond that cluster, the Philippines outnumbers most of the 94 countries the analysis covers, despite closing this selection. Sweden, at the other end of the list, claims about 35 times as many islands as the Philippines. The contrast shows how far the reported counts drop from the Nordic leaders to the bottom of the group.

Slim margins on both sides mean the Philippines holds its position by hundreds of islands, while the countries at the top of the selection stand apart from one another by tens of thousands of islands.

Tesla wins trade zone status for Texas Megapacks, but China tariffs stay

📅 Nov 03, 2026  |  Category: Business  |  Read Source →

Tesla’s Megapack factory in Brookshire, Texas, is now officially a foreign-trade zone, a US customs program that lets manufacturers defer duties on imported parts. The FTZ Board’s approval, published in the Federal Register on Monday, runs for 14 months.

It’s easy to read this as Tesla getting a pass on tariffs for the Chinese LFP cells inside its Megapacks. Tesla’s own application says otherwise: parts hit by Section 301, 232, or 122 tariffs still pay them.

Tesla filed the request on May 20 for Subzone 84AM, part of FTZ 84 in the Houston area. That’s the factory where Tesla started Megapack 3 production in August, with a planned capacity of 50 GWh per year.

The application lists roughly 80 imported components Tesla wants to bring in under zone procedures, from coolant pumps and transformers to busbars, wiring harnesses, circuit boards and battery cells. Duty rates on those parts go up to 6.5%, while finished Megapacks and Bento inverters range from duty-free to 3.4%.

That gap is the usual reason companies set up an FTZ. A manufacturer in a zone can normally elect to pay the finished product’s lower rate instead of the rate on each imported part.

But the application spells out the limit. Components subject to duties under Section 122 of the Trade Act, Section 232 of the Trade Expansion Act, or Section 301 “require subject merchandise to be admitted to FTZs in privileged foreign status,” according to the notice.

Privileged foreign status locks in a part’s own classification and tariff treatment when it enters the zone. So a Chinese LFP cell that ends up in a Megapack sold in the US still pays the Section 301 tariff on Chinese non-EV lithium-ion batteries, which jumped from 7.5% to 25% on January 1, 2026. Tesla just pays it later, when the finished Megapack leaves the zone.

Here’s what Tesla actually gets out of it:

The approval came with the same wording the FTZ Board has used all year. Production was “authorized on a limited basis,” and “further subject to a fourteen-month time period.”

Tesla got an identical decision for Megapack and inverter production at Fremont, Livermore and Lathrop in California this summer, with a one-year limit instead. Eastman Chemical, battery pack maker Rose Batteries, and chipmaker Qorvo all received similar time-limited, “limited basis” approvals in 2026.

The 14-month clock puts the expiry around the end of 2027. That’s roughly when LG Energy Solution is due to start supplying Tesla with LFP cells from Michigan under a $4.3 billion contract for Megapack 3, with mass production expected in the second half of 2027.

Tesla hasn’t hidden the issue. When tariffs escalated in April 2025, CFO Vaibhav Taneja said the impact on energy storage would be “outsized since we source lithium iron phosphate (LFP) battery cells from China.” We wrote at the time that Tesla’s only growing business was kneecapped by Trump’s tariffs.

Tesla has since brought up its own LFP cell factory in Nevada, but Taneja said it would only cover a fraction of Tesla’s installed capacity. And Brookshire alone is designed for 50 GWh a year, on top of the 40 GWh Lathrop factory in California.

Tesla’s Megapacks are bringing big batteries to the grid, but you can get the same benefits at home by pairing solar with storage. With electricity rates up almost 10% last year and expected to keep climbing, going solar is one of the best ways to protect yourself against rising costs. And with lease and PPA options, you can do it with zero upfront cost and start saving immediately. If you want to find the best deal, check out EnergySage. It’s a free service with hundreds of pre-vetted installers competing for your business, so you save 20 to 30% compared to going it alone. No sales calls until you pick an installer. Get your free quotes here.

Tesla is a transportation and energy company. It…

The largest U.S. states by area, ranked

📅 Nov 03, 2026  |  Category: Business  |  Read Source →

Free daily briefing on global business news.

Britannica ranks all 50 U.S. states by total area. Here are 10 of them, from Alaska at No. 1 to Wyoming at No. 10, with square-mile figures.

Spread a map of the U.S. across a table and the states look like pieces of a puzzle, some broad enough to fill a palm and others small enough to hide under a fingertip. Most readers carry a version of this picture in their heads. It guides how they imagine distance, open land, and the reach of a single state line. A mental map can mislead. An outline on paper reveals little about the territory inside it, and two states that look alike at a glance can enclose different amounts of ground. Real numbers turn a vague impression into something a reader can measure, compare, and remember.

Scale compounds the problem. The U.S. spans more than 3,800,000 square miles, or 9,840,000 square kilometers, which places it third among the world's countries by area. Its 50 states carve that expanse into unequal portions, and the gap between the biggest and the smallest runs wide. An average says little about pieces of such uneven size. One midpoint value for the typical state would blur the contrast between giants and slivers, and it would hide where the acreage concentrates. Ordering every state by area exposes those differences one entry at a time, and it supplies fixed points for judging the rest.

Britannica tackles the question with one table that ranks all 50 states by total area, listing each state's extent in square miles and square kilometers. The table credits the U.S. Census Bureau website as the origin of its data. Amy Tikkanen, the publication's managing editor, wrote the explainer, and its editors checked the facts. The page names no data year and offers no further detail on how anyone calculated the totals. The figures below carry its label of total area and nothing more. This article covers 10 of those 50 states, the ones in the first through 10th positions, and sets each state's total area against the neighbors above and below it on the list.

Alaska covers 665,384 square miles, or 1,723,337 square kilometers, a footprint no other state approaches. Texas, the second-largest state, would fit inside Alaska more than twice, at a ratio of about 2.48 to one. California, the third entry on the list, could sit inside Alaska four times with 10,604 square miles left over. The Texas-California pair adds up to 432,291 square miles and trails Alaska by 233,093.

The 10 states in this group cover 1,891,153 square miles between them, and Alaska accounts for about 35% of that sum. The other nine split the remaining 1,225,769 square miles, less than twice Alaska's total. Their average works out to about 136,197 square miles apiece, close to one-fifth of Alaska's expanse.

Alaska's bulk lifts the 10-state mean to about 189,115 square miles. Alaska and Texas are the lone pair above that mark, and the other eight fall below it. A single outsized entry pushes the group's mean past the size of eight of its 10 members, which makes the figure a poor stand-in for most of the states it summarizes.

Illinois and Iowa, the 25th and 26th entries at 57,914 and 56,273 square miles, mark the middle of the 50-state table. Alaska spans about 11.5 times the area of Illinois. By that midpoint, a typical state claims less than a tenth of Alaska's ground.

Rhode Island, the smallest state, takes up 1,545 square miles, and Alaska could absorb it about 431 times. A gap of 663,839 square miles separates the first and last of the 50 entries. Stacking up the smallest states shows how far that reach extends. The 22 at the bottom of the table, from Arkansas to Rhode Island, total 621,823 square miles and still trail Alaska. The bloc takes in Pennsylvania, Ohio, Virginia, Tennessee, Kentucky, and Hawaii, among others. Adding North Carolina, the 28th entry, lifts the sum to 675,642 square miles and past Alaska. Outweighing Alaska with the smallest states demands 23 of them, close to half of the country's 50.

Texas spans 268,596 square miles, enough ground for second place yet 396,788 square miles behind Alaska. The gap by itself exceeds the total area of any state except Alaska. In metric units, Texas covers 695,662 square kilometers and sits 1,027,675 square kilometers below Alaska.

Texas carries a 104,901-square-mile lead over California, the next state down, large enough on its own to outsize Colorado. The margin equals about 26% of the distance Texas trails Alaska, which leaves Texas closer in size to California than to Alaska. No step between back-to-back states from third through 10th place hits a quarter of that lead. Combine New Mexico's and Arizona's territory and the pair reaches 235,580 square miles, 33,016 short of Texas. Pair Montana with New Mexico and the sum, 268,630 square miles, edges past Texas by 34 square miles.

Texas could absorb the nine smallest states more than three times over. Maryland, Hawaii, Massachusetts, Vermont, New Hampshire, New Jersey, Connecticut, Delaware, and Rhode Island add up to 71,157 square miles. Maryland, the biggest of the nine, takes in 12,406 square miles, under 5% of the Texas total. Rhode Island, at the bottom of the list, would fit inside Texas about 174 times.

Texas packs a broad spread of terrain inside its borders, from coastal lowlands and barrier islands to forested floodplains beside big rivers, plateaus, rolling plains, wooded hills, and desert tracts. Water-based habitats of many kinds round out the mix.

Two sections of Texas owe their smooth surfaces to deposited material. The Southern Gulf Prairies and Marshes section forms a flat alluvial plain with few stream cuts. Continental sediment built the plain on a shallow shelf that sat underwater until a drop in sea level exposed it. The Texas High Plains section is another wide, level plain. Rivers shaped it by dropping debris worn from nearby mountain ranges.

California takes up 163,695 square miles, or 423,967 square kilometers, and trails Texas by 104,901 square miles. Colorado's entire footprint of 104,094 square miles could slip inside that gap with 807 square miles to spare. The distance between the second- and third-largest states exceeds the whole territory of the eighth. In metric units, the shortfall to Texas reaches 271,695 square kilometers.

California's 16,655-square-mile edge over Montana looks slim beside its deficit to Texas. Texas outsizes California by a factor of about 1.64. The lead over Montana equals about 10% of California's own expanse and about 16% of the margin separating it from Texas. The same cushion works out to 43,136 square kilometers. By sheer size, California has more in common with Montana than with Texas.

California joins Alaska and Texas as one of three states above 150,000 square miles. Montana, the next state down, misses that line by 2,960 square miles. The trio's combined 1,097,675 square miles makes up about 58% of the ground covered by the 10 states in this article. The other seven states in this group share the remaining 793,478. Alaska outweighs California by about four to one.

California's landscape swings between mountain and desert extremes. The Sierra Nevada Mountains hold sequoias and many alpine lakes. The desert terrain of Joshua Tree, Mojave, and Death Valley features salt flats, creosote plants, and sand dunes.

California could absorb three copies of New York, at 54,555 square miles apiece, with 30 square miles left over. Florida's 65,758 square miles would fit inside California about 2.5 times. Pennsylvania's 46,054 square miles go into California more than three and a half times. Maine, New Hampshire, Vermont, Massachusetts, Connecticut, and Rhode Island add up to 71,987 square miles, less than half the California figure. Rhode Island would fit inside California about 106 times. Among the smaller states on the list, matching California's reach takes a group of several.

Montana holds 147,040 square miles, or 380,831 square kilometers, and outstrips New Mexico, the next state on the list, by 25,450 square miles. A gap of that size exceeds the whole of West Virginia, which covers 24,230 square miles. Montana could give up a West Virginia's worth of territory and keep a 1,220-square-mile edge over New Mexico.

Texas spans about 1.8 times Montana's area and exceeds it by 121,556 square miles. The shortfall to Texas comes within 34 square miles of New Mexico's entire area. Alaska reaches about 4.5 times Montana's size. At the opposite end of the scale, Montana dwarfs Rhode Island, the smallest state, which would fit inside it about 95 times.

Montana outsizes the 11 smallest states combined. The group runs from South Carolina, at 32,020 square miles, to Rhode Island, at 1,545, and its members sum to 127,407 square miles. Montana clears that total by 19,633 square miles. Adding Maine, at 35,380 square miles, lifts the tally to 162,787, enough to overtake Montana.

Montana shares borders with Wyoming, Idaho, North Dakota, and South Dakota. Montana surpasses each of them by a wide margin. Its lead over Wyoming reaches 49,227 square miles. Idaho, at 83,569 square miles, falls 63,471 short. South Dakota trails by 69,924 and North Dakota by 76,342.

A merged North Dakota and South Dakota would edge past Montana. The first measures 70,698 square miles and the second 77,116, for 147,814 in all. The duo beats Montana by 774 square miles, about half of Rhode Island's total. Idaho and Wyoming, the two bigger neighbors, add up to 181,382 square miles and clear Montana by 34,342. The Dakotas make up the smallest pair among Montana's four neighbors, which means all six possible pairings of bordering states outweigh Montana. No single neighbor comes close to Montana, yet no two of them fall short of it.

New Mexico encompasses 121,590 square miles, or 314,917 square kilometers, enough to tip a four-state tally past Alaska. Texas, California, and Montana combine for 579,331 square miles, which leaves the trio 86,053 short of the largest state. Adding New Mexico raises the sum to 700,921 square miles and puts the group 35,537 ahead. Metric units confirm the result. The trio falls 222,877 square kilometers short of Alaska, and the four states amount to 1,815,377 square kilometers, which clears Alaska by 92,040. Alaska's lead survives the three biggest states behind it and gives way once New Mexico joins them.

Arizona trails New Mexico by 7,600 square miles, a margin less than a third the size of Montana's edge over New Mexico. In square kilometers, the Arizona gap comes to 19,683. Colorado, three spots down the list, falls 17,496 square miles short of New Mexico. Wyoming, in 10th place, lands 23,777 square miles back, a smaller distance than the one separating New Mexico from Montana. New Mexico sits nearer the cluster below it than the giants above. Texas holds about 2.2 times New Mexico's area, and Alaska holds about 5.5 times. New Mexico covers about 79 times as much ground as Rhode Island, the smallest state. New Mexico opens a run of comparable states more than it brings up the rear of the heavyweights.

Two major landform regions reach into New Mexico's territory. The Colorado Plateau spreads across the state's northern part and continues into northern Arizona, western Colorado, and most of Utah. The Pecos Valley Section, part of the Great Plains physiographic province, lies in west-central New Mexico, and the Pecos Plains and the Canadian Valley count among its main landforms. Arizona and Colorado, both among the 10 here, share the Colorado Plateau with New Mexico. New Mexico's expanse pieces together landscapes that run past its borders.

Arizona measures 113,990 square miles, or 295,234 square kilometers, and holds a 3,418-square-mile edge over Nevada, the state one step below it on the 50-state list.

Arizona's lead over Nevada equals about 3% of Arizona's own territory, a thin buffer for a state of this size. In metric units, the difference comes to 8,854 square kilometers. New Mexico, one place higher, holds a 7,600-square-mile advantage over Arizona. The gap up to New Mexico runs more than twice the size of the cushion over Nevada. Both margins fall below 8,000 square miles, which leaves Arizona hemmed in by close rivals above and below.

Arizona opens a crowded stretch of the table. Its total exceeds Colorado's by 9,896 square miles, a span that takes in three states. Wyoming, the final state in this group of 10, trails Arizona by 16,177 square miles. Michigan, the first state outside this group, trails Arizona by 17,276 square miles. The Wyoming gap amounts to less than a third of the 49,705 square miles separating Arizona from California.

Arizona outsizes the 10 smallest states put together. West Virginia, Maryland, Hawaii, Massachusetts, Vermont, New Hampshire, New Jersey, Connecticut, Delaware, and Rhode Island combine for 95,387 square miles, which leaves Arizona with 18,603 square miles to spare. Arizona's surplus over the group outstrips all of Maryland, the second-largest state among the 10. Rhode Island by itself would fit inside Arizona about 74 times. Adding South Carolina to the group lifts the tally to 127,407 square miles, beyond Arizona's mark.

Alaska holds about 5.8 times as much ground as Arizona, and Texas covers about 2.4 times Arizona's area. Pairing Arizona with Nevada yields 224,562 square miles, which falls 44,034 short of Texas. Montana, two spots higher, carries 33,050 more square miles. Arizona sits inside a middle tier where gaps between neighbors shrink to a few thousand square miles or less, a world apart from the six-figure gulfs that open between Alaska, Texas, and California.

Nevada claims 110,572 square miles, or 286,380 square kilometers, which wedges it into seventh place inside a tight pack. Under 10,000 square miles separate Arizona, one spot above, from Colorado, one spot below. Nevada's 6,478-square-mile lead over Colorado outstrips its deficit to Arizona by more than 3,000 square miles. In size, Nevada sits closer to Arizona than to Colorado.

Alaska could hold Nevada six times with 1,952 square miles to spare. Texas could absorb two Nevadas with 47,452 square miles left over. Nevada falls 53,123 square miles short of California but outsizes Michigan, ranked 11th, by 13,858. Arizona, Nevada, and Colorado add up to 328,656 square miles, less than half of Alaska's total. The arithmetic exposes a sharp split. Beside the two largest states, Nevada serves as a unit of measure, yet next to its neighbors it blends into the crowd.

Rhode Island, the smallest state, could fit inside Nevada 71 times. Nevada's distance from Rhode Island, 109,027 square miles, amounts to less than a fifth of its 554,812-square-mile gap to Alaska. The 10 smallest states, from West Virginia down to Rhode Island, total 95,387 square miles, 15,185 short of Nevada alone. Adding South Carolina, the 40th state on the list, pushes that tally to 127,407 and past Nevada. Territory piles up at the head of the ranking and dwindles toward its tail.

Most of Nevada lies inside the Great Basin Desert, a wide, arid expanse of sagebrush-covered parallel ridges and valleys with steep walls. Much of the southwestern U.S. falls within the Basin and Range Province, a broad region of thin mountain chains and level basins. The bulk of Nevada's ground alternates between ridgeline and valley floor.

Walker Lake, near Hawthorne, has no outlet. Water the Walker River carries into the lake can leave through evaporation or absorption and no other way. The Amargosa River in southern Nevada keeps moving, with the greater part of its flow underground, and surfaces after heavy precipitation. Water reaching parts of Nevada can stay put until the sun or the soil takes it.

Colorado occupies 104,094 square miles, or 269,601 square kilometers, which makes it the eighth and final state on the 50-state list to clear 100,000 square miles. Its margin above that six-figure threshold comes to 4,094 square miles. All 42 states beneath it fall short of the mark. The threshold splits the list into a group of eight and a tail more than five times as long.

The eight states above 100,000 square miles total 1,694,961 square miles, and Colorado brings the least ground of any of them. Alaska could hold Colorado about 6.4 times over. Texas could fit it inside about 2.6 times. Colorado could double itself and still trail Texas by 60,408 square miles. Montana, in fourth, carries 42,946 more square miles than Colorado.

Oregon, the next state down, comes in 5,715 square miles smaller than Colorado. Colorado's edge over its neighbor on the list equals about 5.5% of its own territory. In metric terms, the distance to Oregon measures 14,802 square kilometers. Wyoming sits 6,281 square miles behind Colorado, and Michigan, the 11th state, lags by 7,380.

Colorado outsizes New York and Pennsylvania put together. The two states combine for 100,609 square miles, leaving Colorado 3,485 square miles ahead. Colorado's lead grows against the nine smallest states on the list, from Maryland to Rhode Island. Their area adds up to 71,157 square miles, which leaves them 32,937 square miles under Colorado. Rhode Island could slot into Colorado about 67 times.

Colorado's terrain ranges from plains and tall mountains to red rock formations, sand dunes, and steep, deep canyons. Red rock fills Garden of the Gods and Red Rocks Park along the Front Range. Great Sand Dunes sits in the San Luis Valley. Colorado National Monument and the Black Canyon of the Gunnison lie on the Western Slope.

Vocabulary flag: "square miles" and "state" recur across every paragraph of slide 8 because no precise synonym exists.

Oregon contains 98,379 square miles, or 254,799 square kilometers, which leaves it 1,621 square miles under the 100,000 mark. Each of the eight states above it clears that threshold. Oregon begins the part of the list where figures shrink to five digits.

Wyoming trails Oregon by 566 square miles, a difference of 1,464 square kilometers. No other adjacent pair in this selection shows a narrower gap. The margin amounts to less than 1% of Oregon's total, a sliver that makes the two states near twins on paper.

Texas outsizes the Oregon-Wyoming duo. The two states sum to 196,192 square miles, and Texas surpasses that sum by 72,404 square miles. The same duo beats California by 32,497 square miles. By itself, Texas carries about 2.7 times Oregon's territory. California, next in line after Texas, has 65,316 square miles more than Oregon. Oregon matches about 60% of California's footprint.

Oregon holds more ground than the 10 smallest states combined. West Virginia, Maryland, Hawaii, Massachusetts, Vermont, New Hampshire, New Jersey, Connecticut, Delaware, and Rhode Island reach 95,387 square miles between them, leaving Oregon 2,992 square miles ahead. Rhode Island, the final state among all 50, would fit inside Oregon more than 63 times. Delaware, one place up, would fit inside Oregon more than 39 times.

Michigan, 11th overall, falls 1,665 square miles short of Oregon. The three-state band from Oregon down to Michigan spans under half the distance separating Arizona from Nevada. Minnesota, 12th, sits 9,778 square miles below Michigan, a drop that marks where the crowding ends.

Alaska dwarfs Oregon by 567,005 square miles, a surplus with room for five copies of Oregon and space to spare. Alaska's bulk lifts the average for these 10 states to 189,115 square miles, close to double Oregon's area. In raw square miles, Oregon stands nearer to Rhode Island, 96,834 away, than to Alaska.

Wyoming totals 97,813 square miles, or 253,335 square kilometers, and keeps 10th place by a slim cushion. Michigan, the 11th state on the list, falls 1,099 square miles short of Wyoming, a gap of 2,848 square kilometers. Oregon, one spot higher, edges past both, and the three states sit within 1,665 square miles of one another.

Minnesota, in 12th, opens a wider gulf at 86,936 square miles, which leaves it 9,778 behind Michigan and 10,877 behind Wyoming. The drop from 11th to 12th runs about six times the spread from ninth to 11th. Colorado, the eighth state, clears Wyoming by 6,281 square miles. Margins of several thousand square miles on either flank set the trio apart from the states around it.

Wyoming outsizes the 10 smallest states combined. West Virginia, Maryland, Hawaii, Massachusetts, Vermont, New Hampshire, New Jersey, Connecticut, Delaware, and Rhode Island add up to 95,387 square miles, which puts Wyoming 2,426 square miles ahead of the whole bloc. Adding South Carolina, the 40th state, lifts the group to 127,407 square miles and past Wyoming. Rhode Island would fit inside Wyoming about 63 times.

Wyoming holds more than twice the area of Pennsylvania, which covers 46,054 square miles, and of Ohio, which covers 44,826. New York, at 54,555 square miles, reaches about 56% of Wyoming's total. Each of the three trails Wyoming by more than 43,000 square miles.

Alaska covers about 6.8 times Wyoming's ground, Texas about 2.7 times, and California about 1.7 times. The multiples shrink by large steps across the first three states and by small ones after that. Montana carries about 1.5 times Wyoming's area, and Colorado, two spots above Wyoming, about 1.06 times. Alaska's edge over Texas runs to hundreds of thousands of square miles, while Wyoming's grip on 10th rests on less territory than half of Delaware.

South Of Midnight Director Says He Would Not Have Funded The Game

📅 Nov 03, 2026  |  Category: Business  |  Read Source →

Xbox funded and published 2025's South of Midnight, a highly acclaimed action-adventure game set in the American Deep South. The game's creative director, David Sears, said he believes South of Midnight is a "generational" game that he hopes people discover and play for a long time and one that he thought would help elevate the "prestige" of Xbox.

At the same time, Sears said he wasn't sure Microsoft's spending spree under the previous leaders Phil Spencer and Sarah Bond--which is how Sears' studio Compulsion got the funding to make South of Midnight--was going to work out long term.

"I literally told people, I was like, 'Yeah, wow, this could be terrible.' Because somebody is going to say, someday, 'The money tap ... we're closing those floodgates.' And that's going to have really traumatic results downstream," he told Ben Hanson on MinnMax.

Microsoft spent billions and billions of dollars to buy studios and franchises to help get Xbox Game Pass off the ground. The bet did not work out, and now Spencer and Bond are out. The new Xbox CEO is Asha Sharma, and one of her first moves was to cut costs dramatically with layoffs, studio divestures--including Compulsion--and game cancellations. Sharma also talked about how Game Pass needs to evolve into something that's "more enticing to a broader range of customers.”

Asked directly if he had been in control of the Xbox funding at the time, would Sears have stopped writing checks for games like South of Midnight? "Yeah, probably," he answered (via Kotaku).

"I wanted to make it. No doubt. Obviously. Because we made it. Would I have turned off someone else’s money flow before South of Midnight? Yeah. Because I’m not completely selfless. It just seems like, in hindsight at least, it was unsustainable," Sears said.

A lot of people said Microsoft's spending on Xbox Game Pass was unsustainable and ultimately going to lead to pain and turmoil. Raphael Colantonio, the co-founder of Arkane, said Game Pass is unsustainable and "increasingly damaging" to the industry. Game Pass has been propped up by Microsoft's "infinite money," Colantonio said.

Raccoon Logic studio head Alex Hutchinson spoke out about Game Pass devaluing games earlier this year after his game Revenge of the Savage Planet was a day-one title on Xbox Game Pass. Conversely, Rebellion touted the launch of Atomfall on Game Pass as a huge success for the company.

With Microsoft's big bets not panning out in the way the company envisioned, the Xbox business struggled, and the ensuing Xbox "reset" has been painful for the people, teams, and fans of Xbox. Sears said hindsight is 20/20, but he always had a feeling that things just weren't right and that the bottom could fall out one day in terms of Xbox's spending spree.

"Of course it was going to have a 'shocking' outcome. It’s easy to dissect it now. But at the time ... I would talk to other directors and [they’d] be like, 'How is this happening? How did we get so lucky? Is this a golden era?' And I would always be like ... 'For now,'" he said. "And there’s always a night when Rome burns. I didn’t know exactly when it would be, but I was always worried about it."

Sears went on to say the Microsoft of today is "much more humane" than the company was in the '90s and 2000s, something he said is "remarkable and commendable."

Sears said it's a good thing that Microsoft is allowing studios it wants to let go of get spun out instead of shutting them down outright. Compulsion, for its part, was among the studios that got spun out, and is now operating independently and seeking new projects and funding sources.

South of Midnight originally launched for Xbox and PC in April 2025, later coming to PS5 and Switch 2 in March 2026. Now that Compulsion is independent, buying the game supports the studio directly, and not Xbox.

Throughout all of the downturns at Xbox, Microsoft continues to make gigantic sums of money, in part because Xbox represents just a tiny sliver of the company's overall business. For the latest quarter, Microsoft made $92.3 billion in revenue (up 19%) and a profit of $36.6 billion (up 32%). Microsoft CEO Satya Nadella has praised Sharma for gutting the Xbox business so heavily.

Disney World will host the John Cena Classic next month, with an 'Avengers: Doomsday'-themed edition of 'Worlds Collide' to follow it.

The Walt Disney Co. and the WWE are expanding their partnership, bringing a pair of major events to Walt Disney World next month while also using the WWE to bolster interest in the upcoming Marvel Studios film Avengers: Doomsday.

Disney and the WWE are already in business via ESPN, which has the U.S. rights to WWE premium live events via its ESPN app, but the two companies will be bringing their next projects to ESPN’s Wide World of Sports at Disney World.

The sports complex will host The John Cena Classic on Monday, Dec. 14, with stars from Raw, Smackdown and NXT competing for the special championship belt as voted on by Club WWE members. The partnership expands the relationship between the companies to experiences and retail, beyond the ESPN events. Those also happen to be two areas that Disney knows well, with the Wide World of Sports complex having hosted the Special Olympics USA, the 2020 NBA bubble season, the Invictus Games, Pro Bowl skills competitions and other major sporting events.

As A Therapist, I Thought Caregiver Burnout Wouldn’t Come For Me. I Was Wrong.

📅 Nov 03, 2026  |  Category: Business  |  Read Source →

The sandwich generation is sorely lacking in support.

Tuesday, 8:10 p.m. I’m helping my 87-year-old mother brush her teeth and change for bed. My 10-year-old calls from the room next door that he and his dad are done reading his latest Big Nate book. He asks (again) when I’m coming to his room to say goodnight. I try not to rush my mother, but I’m exhausted after a long day, and keenly aware of missing those precious minutes right before bedtime when you get the real deal from your child about everything that happened that day.

After months of juggling my work as a therapist with caring for my aging mother (who has been diagnosed with dementia), and being a present, engaged mom and wife, I had to admit that I was dropping some balls. Having all of this on my plate was clearly not working.

I knew I was not alone — I’ve been having this conversation with many friends and clients, also in their 40s or early 50s. In fact, 47% of caregivers under the age of 50 are part of the sandwich generation, those of us who are raising children and caring for aging parents at the same time. If we aren't talking about perimenopause with all its patches and pills, we’re talking about the health concerns of our parents and, potentially, our grandparents as well.

The burnout I was feeling falls particularly heavily on the shoulders of women. Even if you have siblings, most of the caregiving responsibility consistently falls on one person, and that person is typically a woman. Women shoulder the majority of caregiving responsibilities, often to the detriment of their careers. To make matters worse, some caregivers may have a complicated relationship with the parent who is now dependent on them.

Caring for your parent can be immensely rewarding and depressing. Sometimes I feel deeply honored to care for the person who dedicated her life to caring for me. Other times, my mom can’t remember why her beloved husband hasn’t come home yet — my father passed away several years ago. She really doesn’t enjoy having to be bathed by her child, for that matter.

As a mental health professional who has sat with clients going through this very thing, I expected to have more of the answers, to be able to cope and balance more than someone else due to my experience. But the truth is, nothing can truly prepare you for your induction into the sandwich generation.

No matter how we try to overcome the difficulty of caregiving as individuals, we must acknowledge that there’s minimal financial or systemic help for consistent care for the elderly living at home. Some Medicare programs can provide all your healthcare in one place, which is obviously better than driving all over town talking to doctors who aren’t talking to each other. But they often only offer last-minute daily help and, because of the high need in our country, you typically see a new caregiver each day. While better than nothing, it’s clearly not a great situation for a parent who is already confused in their day-to-day life. Some programs actually pay you, the caregiver, for your work, but that doesn’t give you back the missed moments with your own children or restore those career opportunities.

Eventually my husband and I realized that we needed help. Fortunately, we could afford to pay a nurse out of pocket, but the costs have been steep: tens of thousands of dollars annually to have carers for my mother seven days a week, and that’s with me still doing bedtime duty on the weekends. It’s simply not realistic or sustainable for most people.

And one of the challenges that we don’t often talk about is the guilt. Even if you are able to defy the odds and pay someone to come in to help, you still feel guilty about it and get caught up in the “shoulds.” I should be able to care for my parents and my children at the same time; people have been doing this forever with a lot less help. I should be grateful to be able to spend whatever time I have left with my parent. The important part missing from that is the fact that many of us don’t have villages in the way we used to — that nearby family network that would support each other during all the stages of life.

Our lack of a village came into focus for me recently when we adopted a baby into our family. She has brought us more joy than we could have imagined, and I see how much a community would change this experience for us. In my Mommy and Me classes, so many of the young mothers have discussed having their parents come and stay with them during the early weeks of having a newborn. What a stark contrast for me as a mother, in a different phase in my life, where I am taking care of my parent, rather than her taking care of me and my children.

This is a problem that is not going anywhere. Around the country and the world, people are making tough decisions every day — draining bank accounts, missing out on kids’ milestones, declining promotions and date nights with their partner — to take care of a loved one. The physical, financial, and emotional toll can cause extensive damage to the caregiver’s well-being in so many ways.

We also know what to do about this problem. Studies show that while caregiver burnout is a worldwide issue, it is less so in the countries where (surprise, surprise) there is a strong public healthcare system, flexible work environments for caregivers, and affordable private health insurance. These kinds of safeguards can be a key part of rebuilding a new kind of village and community support. The answer is there. The question is if we will take heed before we all burn out.

Dr. Patrice Le Goy is a licensed marriage and family therapist and international psychologist. She is also the research chair of Maternal Mental Health Now and the board chair of Open Paths Counseling Center, as well as the host of Like-Minded with Dr. Patrice, an award-winning mental health podcast.

Patrice lives in Los Angeles with her husband, two children, and their massive dog who is definitely part wolf. You can follow her on social media @drpatricelegoy.

Trump Wants More Attention For His New Algae Biofuel Showcase

📅 Nov 03, 2026  |  Category: Business  |  Read Source →

US President Donald Trump has famously transformed the magnificent Lincoln Memorial Reflecting Pool into a high profile showcase for the algae biofuel industry, and he stands firm in his faith that the tiny green microalgae can produce renewable fuels at scale. In fact, on Monday, November 2, the President held his all-important closing message for the 2026 midterm election cycle right there at the Reflecting Pool. Hey, maybe Trump knows something about algae biofuel…

The Reflecting Pool is a massive body of shallow water that extends from the foot of the Lincoln Memorial in Washington, DC. Situated on filled ground in former marshlands, the Reflecting Pool provides a golden opportunity to showcase the ability of the domestic biofuel industry to take advantage of the built infrastructure, rather than taking up valuable farmland with conventional energy crops like corn or soybeans.

Although algae cultivation was probably not the original intention of the Reflecting Pool, it has been a perennial host to algae ever since its construction in the 1920’s, with the humid environment and temperate climate of DC lending a hand.

In earlier years, the Pool was drained and refilled twice a year to control the growth. By the 1990’s, the structure was clearly sinking into the marshes, sparking calls for a major structural overhaul and the addition of a water circulating system.

The soup-to-nuts rehab was completed in 2012, and yet the algae persisted. Finally, when Trump took office for the second time in January of 2025, he lit upon a brilliant idea of turning lemons into lemonade. The Reflecting Pool would be re-made as a monument to the domestic algae biofuel industry.

By the summer of 2026, the transformation was complete and the algae were thriving, as confirmed by satellite imagery. Others mocked, but the President stood by the plan. After all, biomass is a key element in his “American Energy Dominance” policy, and where better to showcase biofuel than in the nation’s capitol, to be seen and admired by millions of tourists.

And so, that explains why Trump chose the Reflecting Pool as the seemingly incongruous scene of his Election Day eve closing message, as reported somewhat incredulously by The Guardian among many others.

“On the eve of a consequential election day, with polls indicating that most voters feel the US president has grown too focused on building projects in the nation’s capital, he stood in front of the newly opened reflecting pool to discuss – in detail – changes to the pool’s pipe, the grass surrounding the area and granite,” observed The Guardian.

Algae biofuel surfaced regularly on the pages of CleanTechnica in the early 2000’s, with the high oil content and rapid growth cycle of algae being the attractions along with the potential for developing next-generation farming and extraction systems leading to a competitive renewable energy resource (see more algae background here).

Not much progress has been made since then, and stakeholders in the field have weathered much skepticism if not downright mockery. A seal of approval from the President of the United States of America is just what the industry needs to get to the next level.

Indeed, a fresh round of activity has surfaced in recent years, with the UK firm HutanBio being one example. The startup aims to generate profitability by leveraging the carbon-capturing angle of algae farming.

Earlier this year Hutan signed an MOU (Memorandum of Understanding) with the Irish firm Utopia World Investment and the biodiesel refiner Wakud International to scale up its algae farming system, with Oman being the location. “Our unique microalgae strain, Sphaerica, is grown in seawater, on non-arable coastal land, using CO₂ and sunlight creating a genuinely low-carbon biocrude stream that the industry currently lacks and urgently needs,” HutanBio says of itself.

As described by HutanBio, Sphaerica yields up to ten times more oil than typical algae biofuel strains. Hutan expects the high level of productivity to dovetail with Oman’s high level of solar radiation, enabling it to leapfrog over its competitors.

The Oman operation is set for operation at a demonstration scale before the end of 2027, which is just around the corner. Meanwhile, Japanese automaker Mazda is also beginning to crack the algae code.

On October 7, Mazda announced that its microalgae biofuel research program has nailed down support from NEDO, the New Energy and Industrial Technology Development Organization of Japan. The Mazda program is in early stages, with scale-up expected by the 2030’s. If all goes according to plan, Mazda will use its homemade algae biofuel to factory-fill the tanks of every diesel vehicle that rolls off its assembly lines.

Evidently Mazda does not anticipate that drivers can pump out algae biofuel at any corner gas station for their next fill-up. Still, it’s another sign that that years-long research programs are finally leading to commercial application.

For a glimpse into the future, check out newly published research from the Saitama University in Japan. Where conventional systems involve cultivating algae, drying it, and processing it to remove oils, the Saitama team has developed a strain of cyanobacteria (commonly known as blue-green algae) that can express free fatty acids outside of the cell and deposit them directly into a culture medium. Among other benefits, the Saitama system does not require destroying the algae with each harvesting cycle.

As for the US, ExxonMobil was the standard-bearer for early stage algae biofuel research throughout the early 2000’s. By 2017, the company was confident enough to put up millions for an elaborate advertising blitz that showcased its efforts to the world.

Or not, as the case may be. In 2019, Massachusetts State Attorney General Maura Healey filed suit against Exxon, alleging that the biofuel ads were deceptive. “Specifically, the complaint alleges that Exxon systematically and intentionally has misled Massachusetts investors about material climate-driven risks to its business and has deceived consumers about the central role its fossil fuel products play in causing climate change,” AG Healy’s office explained.

Exxon subsequently dropped the program altogether, but the lawsuit persists. Among the details coming to light is a deposition by an Exxon scientist who worked on the algae program. Under questioning by attorneys with the AG’s office, the scientist asserted that the claims in Exxon’s ads were “patently false,” and that he said as much to the company.

It’s too bad Exxon dropped the ball before Trump had a chance to pick up the algae biofuel torch. Oh, well. We’ll always have the Reflecting Pool…

Photo: The Lincoln Memorial Reflecting Pool has been host to thriving populations of algae ever since its construction in the 1920’s (cropped, courtesy of National Parks Service).

'Marshals' Brings One of Taylor Sheridan's Core Characters Back to the 'Yellowstone' Spin-Off

📅 Nov 03, 2026  |  Category: Business  |  Read Source →

Editor's Note: The following contains spoilers for Marshals Season 2, Episode 5.

As a Yellowstone spin-off, it never exactly comes as a surprise whenever Marshals nods to the events of the original Taylor Sheridan-made series. After all, Kayce Dutton (Luke Grimes) has a long Montana history that goes beyond his time with federal law enforcement. But sometimes, the CBS procedural goes a step further by using Kayce's past as a springboard for a larger idea. It may not always be in the form of an overarching plot, but in the case of this week's "Secret Weapon," he name-dropped a character who we thought the show forgot about after the first few episodes of last season: Wes Bentley's Jamie Dutton.

When trying to pull information about a bomb's location out of a runaway, Will McCoy (Keean Johnson, who previously appeared on Joe Pickett), Kayce compares him to his brother Jamie, noting that both were considered the "black sheep" of the family. In fact, Kayce takes the comparison a step further by adding that his brother was tormented by their older sister, Beth (Kelly Reilly), in a similar vein to how Will's older brother does him. Of course, all this is to figure out where Will's kin took serial bomber Rudy Carptener's (Braxton Hale) explosive device, which Derrek McCoy planned to use to take down the bankers who "stole" their family land. What’s especially interesting about Kayce's callback to his brother is that he uses Jamie's tragedy to encourage Will to do the right thing, deceptively noting that his own brother went and made his own life.

The show that claimed the most of your answers is the world you were built for. If two tied, both are shown — you're complicated enough to straddle two Sheridan universes.

You are a Dutton — or you might as well be. You understand that some things are worth protecting at any cost, and that the modern world's indifference to history, to land, to legacy, is not something you're willing to accept quietly. You lead from the front, you carry your family's weight without complaint, and when someone threatens what's yours, you don't escalate — you finish it. You're not cruel. But you are absolute. In Yellowstone's world, that combination of ferocity and loyalty doesn't make you a villain. It makes you the only thing standing between everything that matters and everyone who wants to take it.

You thrive in the chaos of high-stakes negotiation, where the money is enormous, the margins are thin, and the wrong word in the wrong room can cost everyone everything. You're a fixer — the person called when a situation is already on fire and needs someone with the nerve to walk into it. West Texas oil country rewards exactly what you are: sharp, adaptable, unsentimental, and absolutely clear-eyed about what people want and what they'll do to get it. You're not naive enough to think this world is fair. You're smart enough to be the one deciding who it's fair to.

You are a Dwight Manfredi — someone who has served their time, paid their dues, and arrived somewhere unexpected with nothing but their reputation and their wits. You adapt without losing yourself. You build loyalty through respect rather than fear, though you're not above reminding people that the two aren't mutually exclusive. Tulsa King is for people who are still standing when everyone assumed they'd be finished — who find, in an unfamiliar place, that they're more capable than the world gave them credit for. You don't need a throne. You build one, wherever you happen to land.

You carry the weight of a system that is broken by design, and you do it anyway — because someone has to, and because you're the only one positioned to do it without the whole thing collapsing. Mike McLusky's world is for people who are comfortable operating where there are no good options, only less catastrophic ones. You speak every language: law enforcement, criminal, political, human. That fluency makes you invaluable and it makes you a target. You've made your peace with both. Mayor of Kingstown belongs to people who understand that keeping the peace is not the same as being at peace — and who do the job regardless.

The whole thing was a clever but odd instance where Jamie, notoriously one of the worst on Yellowstone, actually did some good after death. Kayce seems to lament the fact that his brother died apart from the family, which is a new development, since we never got to see him grieve Jamie like he has others. Marshals killed off Monica (Kelsey Asbille) just before the events of the series, with Kayce's first season arc predominantly about dealing with her death. Likewise, the fifth and final season of Yellowstone spent time with Kayce (and Monica) as he grieved both his father, John Dutton (Kevin Costner), and his unborn son. But just as was the case with the premature demise of Kayce and Jamie's other brother, Lee (Dave Annable), all those years ago, Jamie's death has largely been swept under the rug by post-Yellowstone installments like Marshals and Dutton Ranch — except for one notable scene in the latter. While Kayce seems to know what happened to Jamie (as we can tell by the way he turns the tables on the young McCoy runaway later on), Marshals doesn’t press his feelings on the matter. Instead, he just buries it and presses forward.

Considering that Kayce now works in Montana-based law enforcement, and Harry Gifford (Brett Cullen) has previously expressed a professional (if not also personal) interest in the death of not just John Dutton, but also the disappearance of the state's attorney general, it's a bit odd that Marshals hasn't leaned more into Jamie's death. It seems like this would be a great way to have Kayce struggle to balance the sins of his family's past with the new path he's forging in the present, and it feels like too big a loose thread for someone in law enforcement not to be unraveling. Kayce and Jamie didn't end things between them on the best of terms, but throughout Yellowstone, it was always clear that Kayce cared for his older adopted brother. One has to wonder how Kayce really feels about Beth's homicide.

Between the death of the state's governor and the absence of its A.G., Montana — as explored in the Yellowstone Universe — has had a pretty tough break. It also didn't help that the government contracts with operators like Market Equities, which Jamie put together, fell through when Kayce and Thomas Rainwater (Gil Birmingham) struck a deal to preserve the Dutton land in Paradise Valley through the Broken Rock Reservation. With all of this in mind, Marshals could do something interesting here by leaning into what Jamie's disappearance (and John's death) has really meant for the state, and perhaps even pursue avenues of discovering what happened to him. Perhaps this ought to be the plot of a Dutton Ranch crossover! We can only hope...

Marshals airs Sundays on CBS and is available for streaming the next day on Paramount+.

Delta Air Lines Launches 4 New Nonstop Airbus A350 Routes: See Full List Now

📅 Nov 03, 2026  |  Category: Business  |  Read Source →

Delta Air Lines is preparing to expand its Airbus A350-900 network with four new route deployments between December 2026 and May 2027. The additions include new nonstop services to Manila and Riyadh, alongside the resumption of flights to Tel Aviv and an aircraft upgrade on its Athens route. The changes underline the growing importance of the A350 to Delta's international strategy.

The expansion comes as Delta continues investing heavily in long-haul aircraft. Its fleet of 42 A350s already serves destinations across Asia, Europe, Africa, South America, and Australia, while the larger Airbus A350-1000 is scheduled to arrive in 2027. The latest schedule changes offer a glimpse of how Delta intends to deploy this growing widebody fleet.

According to Cirium, an aviation analytics company, Delta has planned four additional A350-900 deployments, three of which originate at its Hartsfield-Jackson Atlanta International Airport (ATL) hub. However, these are not all entirely new routes: Athens' Eleftherios Venizelos International Airport (ATH) represents an equipment change, while Tel Aviv's Ben Gurion Airport (TLV) is a resumption of previously operated service.

The two genuinely new nonstop markets are Riyadh and Manila. Riyadh Airport (RUH) is particularly noteworthy, although its launch remains uncertain. Delta originally intended to begin the service in October but postponed it until March 2027, citing regional security developments. In its October 12 announcement, the airline emphasized its commitment to the Saudi Arabian market while prioritizing passenger and employee safety:

"Saudi Arabia remains an important market for Delta, and we are committed to serving the Kingdom for the long-term. We look forward to connecting Saudi Arabia to Delta's global network in March 2027."

Meanwhile, Los Angeles–Manila will launch on March 28, initially operating three times weekly before increasing to daily on June 7. The service marks Delta's return to the Philippines market after several years and will make it the only US airline operating nonstop between the two cities. Philippine Airlines already serves the market, providing established competition.

Atlanta and Los Angeles International Airport (LAX) will dominate Delta's A350 operations in June 2027, with 54 and 50 weekly departures, respectively. Together, they account for approximately 60% of the 175 weekly departures from Delta's six US hubs. Atlanta has the most geographically diverse network, while Los Angeles is especially important for flights across the Pacific.

The Pacific is particularly important to Delta's A350 strategy. Los Angeles will have nonstop A350 services to seven international destinations, including Hong Kong, Shanghai, Manila, Sydney, and Melbourne. Seattle provides another significant Asian gateway, while Detroit, Minneapolis, and Salt Lake City all contribute additional transpacific connectivity.

Athens, Cape Town, Johannesburg, Riyadh, Santiago, Seoul, Tel Aviv, Tokyo Haneda

Hong Kong, Manila, Melbourne, Paris, Shanghai, Sydney, Tokyo Haneda

Delta's recent international expansion has increasingly relied on the aircraft's range and premium capacity. However, the A350's capabilities extend well beyond the Pacific. Atlanta to Johannesburg Airport (JNB) is Delta's longest scheduled service, covering approximately 8,450 miles (13,600 km).

Meanwhile, Cape Town International Airport (CPT) provides another important South African connection. Delta also deploys the aircraft to Santiago and selected European destinations, demonstrating its flexibility across different long-haul markets and its importance to the airline's global network.

Transpacific corridors dominate this list, although the very longest routes serve destinations in Africa.

According to its latest quarterly filing, Delta currently operates 42 Airbus A350-900s, with another 17 on firm order. The carrier also has purchase options for 20 additional widebodies, which can be converted to either A350-900s or Airbus A330-900s. Delta currently operates 39 of the latter, with firm orders for 16 more.

Delta is currently standardizing its A350-900 fleet around a premium-heavy 275-seat configuration, featuring 40 Delta One Suites, 40 Premium Select seats, 36 Comfort seats, and 159 Main Cabin seats. Fleetwide conversions are targeted for completion by the end of 2027. The next major development will be the arrival of Delta's first A350-1000s in early 2027.

The airline has ordered 20 of the larger variant, with eight scheduled for delivery during 2027 and 12 during 2028. These aircraft will introduce next-generation Delta One Suites, larger entertainment screens, and additional premium capacity, supporting the carrier's international growth ambitions.

Delta's January 2026 order for 15 additional A350-900s and 16 A330-900neos further reinforces this strategy. Once its current Airbus widebody commitments are fulfilled, Delta expects its A350 fleet to reach at least 79 aircraft, including the 20 A350-1000s. Alongside its future Boeing 787-10 fleet, these aircraft will provide greater flexibility as Delta expands internationally and gradually replaces older widebodies.

Just one problem - it's retailing for a whopping $569.99.

Its existence on the Microsoft Store also means we have the full list of included games, which is as follows:

Now, as much as there are some excellent games in the list here, it's hard to recommend paying anywhere near $569.99 for them. We've already seen some people getting the bundle for free after buying a ROG Xbox Ally X20 in recent days, so that handheld seems to be part of the promotion, but it'd be nice if we had a proper breakdown of all the eligible devices.

The Who box sets are intended to be complete documents of individual records. So thereâs the disc of the remastered album (which sounds fantastic), then the initial Townshend demos. Whereas the Tom Petty box set is intended to show you how they got to the first two albums, this release is trying to show you why this was the record between âQuadropheniaâ and âWho Are You.â

Thatâs part of the cri de coeur you hear on âBy Numbers.â Itâs by no means the main thread, but it absolutely underpins Townshendâs personal anxieties. The Swansea show from 1976 here isnât complete, but it is Keith Moonâs last proper show before a U.K. audience. But itâs the last live artifact here thatâs a truly delightful surprise. It is the complete show from the Pontiac Silverdome in December of 1975, cleaned up and remastered. This show set the record for the largest ever indoor rock concert attendance of 76,896, which would be broken by Led Zeppelin in the same place two years later. Because of the size of both the venue and the crowd, a large screen was set up to project closed-circuit video, a thing weâd think absolutely nothing of now, but at the time was a revolutionary development.Â

There were four cameras, and they do a more than decent job of capturing the action but donât expect any fancy camerawork or closeups of guitar solos. (Wait for âMagic Bus,â which swings so much harder and better than it did later.) The band seems perplexed and concerned about the size of the crowd, with both Roger and Pete each taking turns to ask the crowd to take a few steps back.

This Silverdome footage is absolutely a text, and even this rudimentary capture that was meant to make sure the 76,896th guy in the back could see something is still full of electricity and life. If you were lucky enough to see the band with Keith Moon in those days, youâll remember, and if you werenât, this at least brings some of that fairy dust into your life and your house. Put it on, turn it all the way up, and enjoy it without 76,896 people stepping on your feet.
