Netflix's Adults-Only Animated Series Officially Sets Release Date

📅 Nov 03, 2026  |  Category: Business  |  Read Source →

Hannah covers horror, sci-fi, and games at Collider, where she writes news and features, reviews new releases, and interviews the people who make them. She’s helping build out Collider’s gaming coverage, with Pokémon as one of her specialties. If she isn’t writing about a monster, she’s probably watching one on a tape from her VHS collection.

After everything Mizu survived in Blue Eye Samurai’s first season, the prospect of an ocean crossing almost sounds restful. Maya Erskine’s swordswoman fought her way through an extraordinary number of people, repeatedly treated devastating injuries as minor inconveniences, and remained spectacularly resistant to accepting help. Anyone hoping the trip might give her time to reconsider her priorities should probably alter their expectations.

The second season takes her beyond Japan, following a finale that left several characters facing lives they hadn’t necessarily planned for. Mizu has another destination and more names to pursue. The people she leaves behind have their own problems, including the consequences of deciding what they want and discovering what getting it might cost.

Netflix has announced that Blue Eye Samurai Season 2 will premiere January 19, 2027, with six new episodes. Created by Amber Noizumi and Michael Green, the Emmy-winning animated series picks up with Mizu traveling to London to find the two remaining men who might be her father. The new season adds Jonathan Pryce, Joel Kim Booster, and Sherry Cola to its voice cast. Animation studio Blue Spirit returns to bring the story to life, with Mizu facing new allies, familiar enemies, and the personal demons that won’t conveniently stay behind when she reaches another country.

Four of the largest communities on the planet, and four completely different reasons to belong to one. Here is yours, what it would give you, and the argument you would be having within a week.

You belong to the fandom that is really about comfort. Hogwarts works on people because it is a place rather than a plot, and the pull is not the wizard war so much as the corridors, the food, the weather and the sense that somebody is expecting you.

You belong to the deepest fandom on this list, the one where somebody has always read further than you and is quietly delighted to say so. Middle-earth rewards patience more than any other world in popular culture.

You belong to the loudest and largest tent of the four, the one where three generations are all watching the same thing and none of them agree about any of it. That disagreement is not a flaw in this fandom, it is the main activity.

You belong to the thinking fandom, the one that turns up for the argument rather than the explosion. Trek has always been a series of ethical problems in space, and the people who love it love being asked what they would have done.

London puts Mizu in an unfamiliar position beyond having to find her way around. Her revenge is inseparable from the hatred she has endured for being mixed-race, and she is now entering a society that could challenge her assumptions about how others see her. That makes the move more interesting than supplying her with different streets to fight through. Her skill with a sword can get her out of plenty of situations. It cannot settle every question she has about herself. Back in Japan, Akemi and Taigen must navigate life at Edo Castle under a dangerous new shogun. Ringo, meanwhile, searches for a new purpose. Brenda Song, Darren Barnet, and Masi Oka return to voice the characters, whose stories continue alongside Mizu’s overseas pursuit.

Akemi’s next steps are especially worth watching after her determination to gain control over her life became one of the first season’s strongest stories. Reaching a position of influence brings its own dangers, particularly within a court where power offers little guarantee of safety. Ringo’s search carries a different emotional weight: helping Mizu gave him direction, but his future deserves to amount to more than waiting for her return. With six episodes, the new season has two very different worlds to explore and plenty of unfinished business in both. Mizu may be focused on reaching the next person on her list, but the people around her have ambitions that won’t stop moving while she’s away.

Blue Eye Samurai Season 2 premieres January 19, 2027, on Netflix.

How Her Love of Vintage Changed Writer Keli Goff’s Life

📅 Nov 03, 2026  |  Category: Business  |  Read Source →

Her collection, which has given new life to some of the great Black designers, is now a museum show.

When Keli Goff began purchasing vintage, it was a way to put together stylish looks for her new job within her budget. But over the years, as her interest grew, collecting became a way of connecting to the stories of Black designers unrecognized during their lifetimes. Goff, a writer who has been on the staff of shows including Being Mary Jane and And Just Like That, now owns work from major fashion figures like Patrick Kelly, Isaia Rankin, Stephen Burrows, and Ann Lowe, together making up one of the biggest private collections of work by Black designers. This month, the Houston Museum of African American Culture presents “Collecting in Color: Highlights from the Keli Goff Collection,” which was curated by John Guess and Danielle Finnerman. It’s the first major exhibition focused on her collection, and some of the pieces will be donated to the Bard Graduate Center’s Study Collection.

Goff’s interest in fashion design has intersected with her writing career in interesting ways. The estate of legendary costume designer Edith Head chose Goff to adapt her story for a biopic. “It’s all so surreal. I obviously have admired Edith’s work as long as I can remember, so my collection bringing this project into my life is a dream come true,” she says. Last year, Goff completed studying to become an art appraiser. She now consults in the art and auction sphere in addition to writing.

“People have been fascinated by my collection,” Goff says. “It’s a bit rare to see, frankly, collectors in this space like me. I mean like me in all sorts of ways, not just as an African American woman, but an African American woman who’s not from a privileged background. It’s important to me that I’m not the last one like me in these spaces.”

Goff spoke to ELLE about the joy of working with a museum in her Texas hometown, the old movies that inspired her love of vintage, and the designers she wishes everyone knew.

It really started by watching old movies with my mom and watching her dress up [when I was] a little girl. When I show friends old photos of her, people are always thinking, “Oh, she looks like a movie star or very Diana Ross-esque.” We would watch old movies, especially Imitation of Life and things from that era, the real glamour, the golden age of Hollywood. She would always say, “Look at the dresses, look at the dresses,” and I would. I really fell in love with old movies, which I do believe is part of how I ended up becoming a writer.

But also, I really fell in love with vintage clothes. I moved to New York and didn’t have a lot of money, and vintage was not trendy and didn’t cost thousands of dollars like it can be today. For one of my earliest jobs, I had to go to fancy events, but I had no money. I bought a couple of pieces from the ’60s at a flea market. I remember being at this event, and all these women who obviously had a lot more money to spend on clothes, asking, “Who’s this by? What boutique did you buy this at?” And I was like, “Oh, I got it at a flea market on Avenue B.” I think they thought I was joking.

I wrote a piece for The Daily Beast several years ago about the most influential Black designers in history. It was one of the most memorable pieces I’ve written. I don’t think at the time I was aware of how profoundly it impacted me, but I learned how many seminal Black American designers died of AIDS in a really short window of time. Willi Smith, one of the first Black designers to achieve major crossover commercial success in department stores, died. And so did Isaia Rankin and then Patrick Kelly. What I took away is that so much history was lost, but I also think about how different the future of American fashion might be today because these were really trailblazing and hugely influential.

They weren’t just influential Black American designers, but hugely influential American designers. Isaia Rankin basically had invented athleisure wear. It was his idea that you should be able to go from a coffee shop to a gym to the club. [He made] one of the first pieces by a Black designer I acquired online. I’ll never forget that the person selling it said, “This belonged to my aunt. Apparently, this guy used to be somebody,” which I just think is the saddest thing. He had this profound impact and then had been largely forgotten.

During the pandemic, when everyone worked from home with too much time on their hands and easy access to shop online, I ended up acquiring quite a bit, in part because a lot of the brick-and-mortar stores, particularly in Europe, were really struggling. They were so reliant on tourist foot traffic, and so a lot of them started selling online [for the first time]. And so there were a number of French boutiques I started acquiring pieces from, including some Patrick Kelly pieces that still have the original store tags from the 1980s, which I won’t remove. Those I won’t wear. The rest of them I wear when they fit. As life goes, they don’t always fit forever.

I’ve become friendly with Patrick Kelly’s widower, Bjorn Amelan, who’s a lovely, wonderful man and the keeper of Patrick’s legacy. It is important to me, when possible, to try to maintain relationships with the people I view as keepers of the legacies because I see my collection as serving the legacy of the designers. The pieces don’t belong to me. They belong to our wider community because they’re a part of our lived history.

The really cool thing is how many people I’ve met along this journey who have not only become cheerleaders but actually reach out to me about pieces. Writer Jill Kargman has become a friend and she gifted me a vintage Stephen Burrows dress. She said, “It belongs in your collection,” which is just the coolest thing ever.

I’m from Houston, and we have the Houston Museum of African American Culture here. We’re living in a really interesting time where people are having conversations about the politics of diversity, but one of the things that’s cool about fashion is that people of different political persuasions were like, “I think this collection’s really cool. I read an article about your collection, I ended up ordering a book about Elizabeth Keckley, or I ended up going down a rabbit hole about Ann Lowe.” At this moment, to be able to do an exhibition like this in my hometown felt particularly poignant.

Melanie Lawson has been a legendary anchor in Houston for decades. She was asked what she’s going to do in retirement, and she said that her husband, John Guess Jr., was the CEO of the Houston Museum of African American Culture. I sent him a note about how much Melanie inspired me and then mentioned my collection.

It was just kismet. They’ve actually never done a fashion exhibition. It was a real gamble. But what I so appreciated is that they had the same vision that, in a time in which our world is so divided, it’s really beautiful when you can find pieces of art that people can come together around.

This interview has been edited and condensed.

Inside Sophie Bille Brahe’s Danish Seaside Home, Where Every Object Tells a Story

📅 Nov 03, 2026  |  Category: Business  |  Read Source →

It’s almost dusk in Copenhagen when jewelry designer Sophie Bille Brahe tells me over the phone that she’s about to take a quick dip in the ocean before dinner. It’s a small ritual tied to the reason she chose her five-bedroom, three-bathroom seaside home, 20 minutes north of the city center, in the first place. Designed by Danish architect Ib Lunding in 1929, the house sits on one of three small streets of similar homes in what Danes call the “flower neighborhood,” each one a slight variation on the next. For Bille Brahe, it’s this setting—and the sensory elements that come with it—that were non-negotiable. “I open up my window and I can see trees. I can smell the sea,” she says. “For me, that’s very important to have in my life every day.”

The space is a respite for the designer, who views the home as a collection of memories and a backdrop for her bustling family life, kids, and border collie included. It’s also where her namesake fine jewelry line begins. In fact, Bille Brahe often develops her designs within the home’s walls before she ever takes them to the workshop. “I sometimes catch these sensations, these small ideas, and make them a little bit more stable by doing drawings of them here at home. And then, when I have an idea of what I want to do, I’m ready to go to the workshop and start,” she explains. In those beginning stages, she works alone. “I need to be able to make really ugly things or really great things,” she says. “I need a bubble around me.”

That bubble is her workspace, an all-white room anchored by an 1870s Swedish fireplace Bille Brahe had installed when she moved in. “It has gold detailing, and it’s so beautiful,” she says. “A fireplace has a way of gathering family and speaks to a house built with love.” Nearby sits her desk, which belonged to her father’s best friend. “It’s basically where everything I make starts,” she says. “I’d loved it since I was a kid.”

Pulled up to the desk is a chair that visitors to her New York showroom may recognize: a curved stainless-steel design by Mats Theselius reupholstered in Sahco zebra fabric. The same seating appears in the showroom, a throughline between her personal and professional worlds. Marc Chagall’s Soleil Couchant, a Le Klint lamp, a neon work by Gun Gordillo, and a collection of seashells share the desk with her sketches and notes. The room opens onto the dining room, centered on a Børge Mogensen Øresund table surrounded by Gio Ponti chairs and a bouquet of sunflowers resting on it.

Of course, Bille Brahe furnished her boutiques with intention. In Copenhagen, she sought out one-of-a-kind Danish furniture and even began making her own vases in Murano. In New York, she tracked down a rare Poul Kjærholm table, square with a gray leather top instead of the round marble version many Danes grew up eating breakfast at. At home, she followed a similar rule and took her time, layering pieces slowly and making sure each one felt collected and had a story connected. “It took me many years to find a sofa, because every time I bought one I’d almost have a panic attack about the idea of buying something new, something everybody could have,” she says. “Everything I have in my home I’ve inherited, borrowed, or a friend made for me. If something doesn’t have a story, it doesn’t belong.”

Her jewel-toned Svenskt Tenn sofa is piled with pillows, each one a gift from a Swedish friend who came to visit. Beside it sits a slim side table, part drink trolley, stacked with books, a seashell, and a lamp she inherited from her grandfather. Most of the lighting in the house is inherited, except the Noguchi lanterns, which she brings back one at a time from trips to Japan. Another Japan find, the Nextmaruni Armless Chair by the architect duo SANAA (known as the Rabbit Chair for its ear-like back), sits beneath a pink painting by Danish artist Tal R.

Above all, though, it is a family house at its core. “It never feels like it’s an empty house,” she says. “I absolutely love my life with kids.” That’s particularly evident in the kitchen, where the day begins with coffee, breakfast for the kids, and, when the weather allows, the garden door thrown open. “It’s not a big house, so the kitchen needs to be efficient,” she says. “I cook a lot, so it ends up being a kitchen that can manage a lot of food without a lot of space.” Despite its small footprint, the room is warm, with kids’ art hung above the seating, embroidered tablecloths dressing the table, and an airy pendant overhead.

The same energy carries into the hallway, with its rounded window (a signature of the neighborhood’s houses) and walls in a blush pink by Farrow & Ball, the same color as the hallway in her previous home. When she moved, she changed everything else but kept the pink. “I normally don’t like colors on walls,” she says. “But I felt this was bringing something I used to love into the new house.”

Outside on the terrace, that pull toward nature continues. Every plant on the deck is one she’s had for 15 years or more, from a pomegranate tree she’s carried from home to home to the pink nerines called the “autumn flower,” and she has a story for each of them. According to Bille Brahe, there’s no need for more than this. If the trees are close enough to smell and the sea near enough to hear, she’s set and then some. “I’m not a person who needs a lot. I just need something close and clear, and if I have that, I can do what I need to do.”

Billboard Launching ‘Up & Coming’ Music Podcast With SickBird Productions

📅 Nov 03, 2026  |  Category: Business  |  Read Source →

The new video podcast, hosted by Billboard staff writer Kyle Denis, will treat listeners to conversations with music's hottest breakthrough acts across genres.

Billboard has announced the launch of Up & Coming With Kyle Denis, a new weekly video podcast that brings listeners behind the key career moments of some music‘s fastest-rising new stars.

Built on the foundation of its hit “Up & Comer of the Month” franchise, Billboard created the new show in partnership with SickBird Productions. The first episode of Up & Coming will go live Tuesday, Nov. 10, on all podcast platforms.

Breakthrough alt-pop singer/songwriter Bella Kay graces the first episode, which was filmed in the heart of Midtown in New York City and chronicles Kay’s rapid ascent to fame following the release of “The Sick” and “iloveitiloveitiloveit.” The 20-year-old, Texas-born hitmaker also discusses navigating the prickly world of social media virality, how she went from college coffeehouse performances to opening for Noah Kahan in stadiums, and the making of her impressively incisive debut studio album, My Reckless Abandon.

Kay made her Billboard Hot 100 debut earlier this year with “iloveitiloveitiloveit,” which soared to No. 17, while also reaching No. 2 on Hot Rock & Alternative Songs and No. 20 on Pop Airplay. The gut-punching midtempo single previewed her debut studio album, My Reckless Abandon, which she co-wrote entirely with close collaborator Alexis “Idarose” Kesselman, who helped her nail a unique blend of indie pop, soul, country, electronic and rock. My Reckless Abandon hit No. 14 on Top Alternative Albums, with more than 385 million official on-demand streams through Oct. 31, according to Luminate. Nominated for best new artist at the 2026 MTV Video Music Awards, Kay has also spent three weeks atop the Emerging Artists chart.

Up & Coming will highlight rising stars across pop, country, dancehall, R&B and more, allowing fans to get a closer look at the artists behind the songs dominating their streaming and social media algorithms.

SickBird Productions is onboard as Up & Coming’s co-producer. Founded by Jade Watson, SickBird is an award-winning global production company that has launched more than 20 shows to date. Notably, Billboard and SickBird began their partnership with the launch of the hit music business podcast On The Record With Kristin Robinson in 2025.

Since joining Billboard in 2023, Up & Coming host Kyle Denis has become a leading music and culture journalist, covering all genres and how they intersect with politics and business. This year alone, Denis launched a monthly franchise highlighting burgeoning Caribbean artists and hosted a red-carpet livestream for the Trinidad-hosted Caribbean Music Awards, drawing tens of thousands of viewers. He has also received honors for his work, including a Certificate of Recognition from New York State Senator Kevin S. Parker and a nomination for excellence in Caribbean storytelling at the 2026 Island Icons Honors. When he’s not hosting red carpets or penning critical reviews and essays, he also writes cover stories for Billboard, including profiles of Leon Thomas, Shaboozey, Latto, Vybz Kartel, Druski and Sexyy Red.

“From entering music journalism through my own blog almost 10 years ago to my current tenure at Billboard, I’ve become intimately aware of how easy it is for an artist — and the human being at the core — to get lost in the throes of a runaway hit or viral moment,” says Denis. “My goal with Up & Coming is to properly contextualize music’s hottest new names and get to better understand their influences, origins and long-term goals in a game that moves faster than the speed of light.”

“Music and artist discovery is at the heart of Billboard, and Kyle has his finger on the pulse of everything that is new and noteworthy. We can’t wait to showcase his findings to the world, ” said Billboard co-chief content officer Leila Cobo. “As someone covering emerging artists on a daily basis, Kyle Denis is the perfect host to highlight their stories for this series — let him power your new music discovery!” adds Billboard co-chief content officer Jason Lipshutz.

Diona DaCosta, head of content at SickBird and executive producer of Up & Coming, says: “We’re excited to expand our partnership with Billboard and bring Up & Coming to life. There’s nothing more exciting than discovering an artist before they become a household name, and this show gives listeners an inside look at the talent and stories shaping the next wave of music. With Billboard’s unparalleled access and SickBird’s approach to creating premium content, we’re excited to deliver a show that keeps listeners one step ahead of what’s next in music.”

Listen to Up & Coming With Kyle Denis on Apple Podcasts, Spotify and YouTube, and follow along with the show on Instagram and TikTok.

Eric Dane Initially Received Different Diagnosis Before ALS Battle

📅 Nov 03, 2026  |  Category: Business  |  Read Source →

Grey’s Anatomy alum Eric Dane shared in his new autobiography My Book of Days: A Memoir of Moments, that he initially received another diagnosis prior to amyotrophic lateral sclerosis (ALS.)

Eric Dane was searching for answers before receiving his ALS diagnosis.

In the Grey’s Anatomy alum’s new autobiography My Bood of Days: A Memoir of Moments, he shared he initially received a separate diagnosis before amyotrophic lateral sclerosis (ALS).

As Dane recalled, his health battle began when he realized he was “gradually losing any use” of his right hand.

“First, I saw a hand specialist, who passed me off to another hand specialist,” Dane recalled in an excerpt from his book, per US Weekly, “who sent me to a neurologist.”

Once he saw the neurologist, his doctor posited that the Euphoria actor had “contracted some form of neuropathy” but could not determine what he was suffering from, admitting the ailment was “above his pay grade.”

“I shuttled from doctor to doctor and underwent a battery of tests, including one called an EMG, an electromyography test,” Dane recalled, noting his medical team finally confirmed a “diagnostic consensus” of multifocal motor neuropathy.

The initial diagnosis was hopeful, Dane noted, sharing that while it was a “rare autoimmune disorder causing extreme muscle weakness,” the known treatments were “90 percent effective.”

After beginning treatment for multifocal motor neuropathy, Dane’s medical team grew “scared” after he did not respond to treatment.

“‘We’ve been discussing ALS,’” Dane recalled his doctor telling him. “‘But we have to wait and see.’”

Feeling “frustrated and fed up,” The Last Ship star went on to get a lumbar puncture, spinal fluid extraction and brain scans. In 2024, he received his ALS diagnosis, calling it a “death sentence.”

But when Dane—who shared daughters Billie, 16, and Georgia, 14, with ex Rebecca Gayheart—told his family, the heartbreaking news hit him even worse than before.

“I don’t remember a single word I said after that,” Dane wrote. “I remember only feeling a violent impact, like getting the wind knocked out of me, and Rebecca wailing on the other end of the phone, and then I really lose it, and my sobs make my body shudder, and I hear her words pouring out amid her tears, ‘I’m here, we’ll fight this together, I’m not going anywhere.’”

Dane fought his ALS battle for two years before he died Feb. 19 at the age of 53.

For a deeper look into Dane’s fight, keep reading…

Opening Up About His Private Health Battle

Eric Dane shared that he was diagnosed with amyotrophic lateral sclerosis (ALS), also known as Lou Gehrig's disease, in April 2025.

"I have been diagnosed with ALS,” Dane told People at the time. “I am grateful to have my loving family by my side as we navigate this next chapter.”

The Euphoria star made it clear that he wasn’t stepping away from the screen amid his diagnosis, sharing in April 2025 that he was continuing to film the final season of the HBO series.

And Dane, whose Amazon MGM Studios series Countdown debuted over the summer, admitted that acting has kept him “sharp” has he navigates his health battle.

"I feel great when I'm at work," he explained to E! News in June 2025. "Of course, there have been some sort of setbacks, but I feel pretty good. My spirit is always pretty buoyant, so at the end of the day, that's all that matters."

Two months after sharing his diagnosis, the Grey’s Anatomy alum spoke candidly about his journey, including the symptoms that led to his diagnosis.

"I started experiencing some weakness in my right hand," Dane told Diane Sawyer in a sit-down on Good Morning America in June 2025. "I didn’t really think anything of it at the time. I thought maybe I’d been texting too much or my hand was fatigued, but a few weeks later I noticed it got a little worse."

After nine months of appointments, which involved visiting two hand specialists and two neurologists, Dane was told he had ALS.

"I will never forget those three letters," the father of two explained. "It's on me the second I wake up. It's not a dream."

While sharing how the rare degenerative disease has affected him physically so far, he also detailed where he sees the progression headed.

“My left side is functioning, my right side has completely stopped working," Dane shared during his interview with Sawyer. "I feel like maybe a couple more months, and I won’t have my left hand either. It’s sobering."

As Dane goes through this health battle, his wife of over 20 years, Rebecca Gayheart, who filed to dismiss their 2018 divorce in March, has been by his side.

“I call Rebecca. I talk to her every day,” Dane shared during his interview with Sawyer. “We have managed to become better friends and better parents, and she is probably my biggest champion and my most stalwart supporter, and I lean on her."

According to Gayheart, she and Dane are the “best of friends.”

“We are really close. We are great co-parents,” the Beverly Hills, 90210 star explained to E! News April 9, 2025. “We really figured out the formula to staying a family and I think our kids are benefiting greatly from it and we are as well.”

While Dane has been marking milestones with his and Gayheart’s daughters Billie and Georgia, he’s admitted he resents how his ALS is affecting the lives of his family members.

"I'm angry, because my father was taken from me when I was young," the actor, whose father died by suicide when he was a young boy, said during his GMA interview. "And now there's a very good chance I'm going to be taken from my girls while they're very young. At the end of the day, all I want to do is spend time with my family and work a little bit, if I can."

Dane was set to present at the 2025 Emmys alongside his Grey's Anatomy costar Jesse Williams on Sept. 14 but ended up missing the award show. A few weeks after his absence, Dane shared that he had to skip the show because of a hospital visit.

“ALS is a nasty disease," he said during an interview with The Washington Post published on Oct. 2. "So I was in the hospital during the Emmys getting stitches put in my head."

"I was a really upset about it,” Dane added. “But you know, there was nothing I could do about it."

Dane met with doctors and representatives from the I Am ALS organization in September, 2025, and spoke with California congressman Eric Swalwell about federal funding towards research for the currently incurable neurological disease.

“I wanna ring every bell,” Dane told Swalwell in a TikTok video posted by the politician on Sept. 29. “I have two daughters at home. I wanna see them graduate college, get married, maybe have grandkids.”

Dane died on Feb. 19 at age 53 following his yearlong battle with ALS. His family confirmed in a statement to E! that he "spent his final days surrounded by dear friends, his devoted wife, and his two beautiful daughters."

“Throughout his journey with ALS, Eric became a passionate advocate for awareness and research, determined to make a difference for others facing the same fight,” his family's statement continued. “He will be deeply missed, and lovingly remembered always. Eric adored his fans and is forever grateful for the outpouring of love and support he’s received. The family has asked for privacy as they navigate this impossible time.”

Their Names Sound Like Legacy Medical Groups. Their Agendas Tell a Different Story.

📅 Nov 03, 2026  |  Category: Business  |  Read Source →

— Newcomer organizations have names that resemble established groups

Several organizations have sprung up in recent years with names that sound very similar to long-standing established nonprofit public health and medical groups, whose science-based policies have withstood the test of time.

Here's a list of some of these organizations whose names, at first glance, may confuse prospective members or donors.

The American Public Health Association, founded in 1872, has some 25,000 individual members and more than 150,000 allied health professionals and advocates for public health science and spending. It publishes the American Journal of Public Health. Its CEO, Georges Benjamin, MD, has been a vociferous critic of the Trump administration's policies. The APHA has filed a lawsuit against its vaccination schedule policies and several others over grant funding.

The Academy of Public Health was founded in February 2025 by Jay Bhattacharya, MD, PhD, the Trump administration's NIH director, and others who questioned some of the ways the U.S. responded to the COVID pandemic, and who advocated the controversial policy of herd immunity. Its founders also criticized publication policies of many mainstream medical journals. It publishes the Journal of the Academy of Public Health.

The American Academy of Pediatrics was launched in 1930. It now has some 67,000 pediatricians as members. It has opposed Trump administration immunization policies as well as statements by HHS Secretary Robert F. Kennedy Jr. questioning vaccine schedules that have led to lower immunization rates among children, and more illness. It supports gender-affirming care for transgender youth.

The American College of Pediatricians was launched in 2002 by several pediatricians who left the AAP to protest the support of allowing same-sex couples to adopt children. It holds that life begins at fertilization. Its website says it is the "leading U.S. medical organization protecting the biological integrity of children against transgender ideology."

The American College of Obstetricians & Gynecologists began in 1951 to focus on issues specific to women's health, including family planning. It now focuses on advocating for maternal health as a national priority. It has about 60,000 members and publishes the journal Obstetrics & Gynecology. It has opposed several Trump administration policies, including warnings about use of antidepressants during pregnancy, suggesting these are linked with autism, miscarriage, and birth defects.

The American Association of Pro-Life Obstetricians and Gynecologists was an ACOG special interest group until 2013 when ACOG ended that designation. It opposes abortion, suggesting it is associated with health risks such as depression and substance abuse, and supports "the physician's right of conscience in medical decision making" regarding abortion.

The American Academy of Family Physicians began in 1947 and now says it is the voice of 124,500 family doctors. It publishes a journal, American Family Physician. It has opposed several Trump administration actions, such as its removal of an AAFP member and other members from the CDC's long-standing Advisory Committee on Immunization Practices.

The American College of Family Medicine began in the spring of 2023. It opposes mandatory abortion training in residencies and maintains that "abortion is never medically necessary and should not be offered or performed by any health professional."

The American College of Surgeons was founded in 1913 to improve surgical outcomes. It has launched numerous surgical quality improvement programs for surgical specialties to learn strategies and compare results, and its website has a surgical risk calculator. It claims 95,000 members globally, and publishes the Journal of the American College of Surgeons.

The Association of American Physicians and Surgeons was launched in 1943 by a conservative group of physicians. It has long opposed the Affordable Care Act, believes physicians are often unfairly targeted with federal fraud accusations, and are too frequently subjected to "illegitimate" hospital peer review. It opposed COVID mask and vaccination mandates and generally believes that healthcare providers are over-regulated. It opposes gun control.

It publishes the Journal of American Physicians and Surgeons.

The Center for Science in the Public Interest began in 1971 as a consumer advocacy group and now advocates for policies that keep dangerous chemicals out of the food supply. It wants to "fix our broken food additive regulations" and improve food labeling.

The Center for Medicine in the Public Interest says it is a non-partisan research and educational organization with a goal to advocate for patient-centered healthcare. It has come under fire for its free-market policies and has been said to be overly influenced by the pharmaceutical industry, including lawsuit immunity for drug manufacturers.

The Children's Defense Fund began in 1973, rooted in the civil rights and women's movements and the fight for equity and racial justice. It works to reduce child hunger and gun violence and improve nutrition, education, and school safety.

Children's Health Defense began as the World Mercury Project, but under Robert F. Kennedy Jr.'s chairmanship -- his picture is at the top of the organization's homepage -- the name was changed in 2018. Through litigation and public messaging campaigns, the group has challenged public health mandates and school vaccine requirements, and has suggested vaccines and some environmental contaminants are the underlying causes for autism, despite evidence to the contrary.

As the Trump administration's Health Secretary since 2025, Kennedy has taken many of CHD's causes and statements and incorporated them into federal recommendations and policies. His actions have prompted numerous lawsuits from states, medical groups, and others.

On its website soliciting donations, CHD acknowledges the potential for name confusion with other organizations. It says, "Please note, we are often confused with Children's Health Fund or Children's Defense Fund, so be careful not to inadvertently select the wrong recipient."

Several of these organizations did not respond to a request for comment, or said they declined comment on whether name similarity had affected their philanthropic donations or membership.

Former CAKE team is back in the kitchen with a new electric motorcycle

📅 Nov 03, 2026  |  Category: Business  |  Read Source →

If the new ReBake Abler electric motorcycle looks like it might share a bit of DNA with Sweden’s dearly departed CAKE, there’s a good reason for that.

ReBake Motors has officially unveiled its first electric two-wheeler at EICMA 2026 in Milan, introducing the Abler as a highly modular, utility-focused electric moped and light motorcycle.

Behind the new Swedish company is founder and CEO Karl Ytterborn, who spent eight years at CAKE from its founding in 2016 through 2024. CAKE famously built some of the most interesting minimalist electric motorcycles and utility bikes on the market before ultimately collapsing after failing to secure additional funding. Ytterborn says ReBake is intended to apply many of the lessons learned during that experience, while eliminating unnecessary cost and complexity.

Its chunky 42mm aluminum tube frame isn’t just a styling exercise. ReBake designed it as a mounting system, allowing cargo accessories to attach directly to the frame at multiple locations. That means riders can configure the bike for everything from carrying groceries and passengers to more serious utility duties.

There are two versions. The Abler45 is an EU L1e-B moped with a 45 km/h (28 mph) top speed, 4 kW nominal motor and 5.12 kW peak output. It starts at €3,990 including VAT.

The €4,990 Abler80 steps up to the A1 light motorcycle category, increasing nominal power to 6.14 kW and peak power to 7.68 kW while pushing the top speed beyond 80 km/h (50 mph). Both versions use a direct-drive rear hub motor.

Energy comes from removable 2 kWh lithium iron phosphate (LFP) batteries weighing 17 kg (37.5 lb) apiece. The Abler45 comes standard with one battery but can carry two, while the Abler80 includes two for 4 kWh of total capacity. With both batteries, ReBake claims up to 160 km (99 miles) of low-speed urban range on the Abler45 or 114 km (71 miles) on the Abler80. Each battery can be charged either on or off the bike from a normal 230V outlet, reaching a full charge in four hours.

The rest of the hardware includes 14-inch cast wheels, 140mm of front suspension travel, dual rear shocks, front and rear disc brakes with combined braking, regenerative braking, and full LED lighting.

Perhaps more interesting than any individual specification, though, is simply seeing another Swedish electric motorcycle startup emerge from CAKE’s ashes.

CAKE demonstrated that there was plenty of appetite for unconventional, lightweight electric motorcycles. Unfortunately, it also demonstrated just how difficult it is to turn that enthusiasm into a sustainable business.

ReBake appears to be getting another crack at that equation – this time with eight years’ worth of lessons already baked in.

Tarik Skubal contract predictions: Ace's new deal could break multiple MLB records for a pitcher

📅 Nov 03, 2026  |  Category: Business  |  Read Source →

If not listed, please contact your TV provider.

Tarik Skubal is about to become a very, very rich man. The two-time Cy Young winner and arguably the best pitcher in baseball hits free agency this winter, and it stands to reason that he'll break the record for highest-paid pitcher in MLB history, both by total value and average annual value.

By total value, Dodgers ace Yoshinobu Yamamoto currently holds that record thanks to the 12-year, $325 million contract he signed ahead of the 2024 season (we're eliminating Shohei Ohtani from the running for obvious reasons, though his 10-year, $700 million deal would clearly lead the list).

As for AAV, Ohtani again tops the class at $70 million a year, but for those who only pitch, Max Scherzer and Justin Verlander share the record at $43.33 million annually.

The expectation is that Skubal will break both records, even as the owners try to tamp down on spending in the ongoing CBA negotiations. Just how high could those figures go? CBS Sports experts pulled out their calculators.

Mike Axisa: Seven years and $325.5 million. That's $46.5 million per season and it would break the records for average annual value (Max Scherzer's and Justin Verlander's $43.3 million) and total guarantee (Yoshinobu Yamamoto's $325 million) for a pitcher. I would expect there to be an opt out because those are just the cost of doing business these days. Maybe even multiple opt outs. Scott Boras loves to break records, though, and this contract allows him to break two.

Dayn Perry: I'll say eight years, $350 million. Likewise, that sets the record for total contract value and AAV for a pitcher. Normally, I'd ding him a bit for those elbow problems in his walk year, but he was mostly dominant after returning ahead of schedule. I also think Skubal will benefit from an aggressive pre-lockout market and from a pretty thin free agent class overall.

Matt Snyder: Eight years, $400 million. This gets him the record deal for pitchers both in terms of the guarantee and the average annual value. I'm probably going too low on the years, but I feel like teams have been burned so many times before on the length of pitcher deals due to injury that this holds down the market a bit. Ever so slightly. I do think there will be opt outs after years three and five.

Trump DOJ sends target letters to Democrats in Alaska Senate criminal probe: Reports

📅 2026-11-03  |  Category: Business  |  Read Source →

The Trump administration's months-long campaign to challenge how states conduct elections and pushback against those efforts came to a head on Election Day on Tuesday.

Those efforts have included lawsuits, an attempt to implement a new U.S. Postal Service rule for mail-in ballots this year, threatening letters to state election officials and a plan to increase the number of poll monitors.

Follow CNBC's live coverage of the 2026 midterm elections

On Tuesday afternoon, The New York Times reported that the Department of Justice had sent letters to about 20 Democrats, among them prominent election lawyer Marc Elias, informing them that they might be targets in a criminal investigation of how a candidate with the same name as incumbent Republican Sen. Dan Sullivan ended up on the ballot of the Alaska Senate race.

NBC News reported that the tally of letters includes both target letters "and preservation requests to third parties to retain records."

Elias has represented Mary Peltola, the Democratic nominee in the Alaska election. In addition to him, at least one official at the Democratic Senatorial Campaign Committee was sent a target letter, The Times reported.

"The department is investigating possible donor fraud and conspiracy charges," according to The Times, citing a person familiar with the investigation.

Receiving a target letter does not mean a person has been charged with a crime, and does not necessarily mean that they will be charged.

In a statement, Elias said, "No one should be surprised that President Trump's Department of Justice is targeting me and my law firm on Election Day."

President Donald Trump, during an interview earlier Tuesday on Fox News' "Fox and Friends," blasted Elias.

"You had the Democrat Party headed up by Elias, their lawyer, who was a bad guy, very bad guy — should go to jail, in my opinion, " Trump said. "But you had the Democrat Party look all over Alaska for somebody named Sullivan."

A federal judge on Sunday dismissed the DOJ lawsuit that sought to delay certification of Alaska's Senate election if incumbent Sen. Sullivan loses by a margin that could be attributable to voter confusion from having another Dan Sullivan on the ballot. The DOJ is appealing that ruling.

That case, and the reported letters to Elias and other Democrats, underscore the likelihood that the Trump administration's election-targeting efforts will continue beyond Election Day.

Democratic lawmakers, attorneys general and advocacy groups are poised for battle if the administration tries to interfere with voting sites or challenge ballot counts, particularly in the states that could determine whether Trump's fellow Republicans lose their narrow Senate majority.

Senate Minority Leader Chuck Schumer, D-N.Y., on Sunday night convened the entire Senate Democratic Caucus for a briefing by him and Elias about what a Democratic aide said was the "current landscape of election threats from Trump and his allies." The aide spoke on condition of anonymity to discuss the private briefing.

"We've been, you know, blocking and tackling and protecting and defending our elections for months and through the last few weeks of the voting window, and it'll all culminate tomorrow," California Attorney General Rob Bonta told CNBC on Monday evening. "And each of Trump's attempts has failed."

"I'm not sure what he has up his sleeves," Bonta said of Trump.

Bonta said he and other California officials, along with the 23 other Democratic state attorneys general for months have been preparing for Election Day and potential actions by the Trump administration "to disenfranchise and suppress votes and voters."

"We game-planned, and ... war-gamed and tabletop- and scenario planned all the possibilities," Bonta said.

Bonta said that if extreme events happened, California officials would quickly seek relief from the court system.

He noted, "We have laws in place right now where it is a criminal offense" to remove ballots from the custody and control of an election official.

On Monday, multiple federal courts dealt with urgent motions in election-related lawsuits and appeals by and against the Trump administration.

The DOJ on Monday asked the Supreme Court to block California from counting about 20,000 mail-in ballots from voters whom the DOJ suggests might not be U.S. citizens. Hours later, the Supreme Court rejected that request without any noted dissents.

In U.S. District Court in Washington, a DOJ lawyer told Judge Reggie Walton on Monday that the department expected to deploy 900 people to monitor polling places at sites in all 50 states.

"In 2024, there were about 100 monitors," the lawyer, Kathryn Barragan, said.

The DOJ on Tuesday said it was deploying approximately 1,000 election monitors and observers across the country. The areas it is focusing on are primarily in urban areas that vote Democratic.

Barragan's statement came at a hearing where Walton was considering an emergency request by the NAACP to bar the DOJ from using election monitors who are federal law enforcement personnel.

John Cusick, assistant counsel at the Legal Defense Fund, a civil rights group, said the administration has used the DOJ, Department of Homeland Security and other federal agencies to target election procedures this election cycle for three main reasons.

"First and foremost, it's an attempt to intimidate voters and election officials," Cusick said in an interview.

"The second component is confusion" about election rules, he said. "And the third, the unfortunate part, is trying to lay the groundwork for challenging the post-election counting and certification of ballots."

He said there has been a "constant barrage" by the administration to target election workers and election officials with unlawful actions.

"That has created such a chilling effect," Cusick said. "There's no doubt that the threats are threats to our democracy."

The DOJ, which did not respond to a request for comment by CNBC for this article, has previously defended its election-related efforts.

"Voting by noncitizens and other ineligible voters is a federal crime, and the department will enforce the law whether a case involves one illegal ballot or hundreds," the DOJ's rapid response account on X said on Oct. 12.

Attorney General Todd Blanche, when asked on Oct. 22 if there was any scenario in which the DOJ would try to block states from certifying their election results, conceded that was possible.

"I mean, is there any scenario?" Blanche told reporters in Virginia. "Yes, of course. There's scenarios that we could all come up with, hypothetically, you know, some doomsday scenario, for example."

"It is not DOJ's intention to, and it's never our intention to improperly interfere with elections and the state confirmation process or certification process," the attorney general said.

"But, you know, to say that there's no circumstances that we would ever do that? Of course I'm not going to say that."

Cusick and other experts note the president and executive branch of the federal government have no official role in administering elections, which are handled by the states as designated under the Constitution.

In her ruling Sunday dismissing the DOJ's Alaska Senate lawsuit, District Court Judge Sharon Gleason said the Trump administration was making requests that involve "the time, place, and manner of Alaska's election, which are the responsibilities of the State, not the executive branch of the federal government."

On Monday, a bipartisan group of former election officials in a filing told the 9th Circuit U.S. Court of Appeals that "increased federal pressure and threats on election officials ... are part of a pattern by the administration of attempting to assert authority over elections and questioning the integrity of state and local officials."

The filing came in a case where the DOJ is appealing an order issued Friday by a federal judge in California barring the Trump administration "from carrying out a series of efforts to actually or attempting to intimidate, threaten, or coerce election workers who aid others in exercising their right to vote."

The judge cited threats by the DOJ to prosecute state and local election officials if they process and count ballots using procedures that are legally required.

Bonta, California's attorney general, said he expected Trump and the DOJ To continue targeting states over their election procedures through the next cycle in 2028, when voters will choose a new president.

"We can't be naive about it. We have to expect it to get worse, prepared for it to get worse. I don't want to be alarmist," Bonta said. "I am a realist though, and I see the way he thinks and acts and what he's done, and maybe we'll be prepared for the worst, and it'll never happen."

"But we can't not be prepared for it. And I expect him to escalate as he gets more desperate."

Ferrari raises 2026 guidance after Q3 earnings beat

📅 2026-11-03  |  Category: Business  |  Read Source →

Ferrari on Tuesday raised its 2026 guidance after the sports car maker slightly beat Wall Street's third-quarter expectations.

Here's how Ferrari performed in the third quarter compared with average estimates compiled by LSEG:

Ferrari raised its guidance to revenue of 7.65 billion euros, up from 7.6 billion euros; adjusted earnings before interest, taxes, depreciation and amortization to 3 billion euros, up from 2.97 billion euros; and adjusted EPS to at least 9.85 euros, up from at least 9.68 euros.

The automaker also upgraded its expectations for industrial free cash flow to up to 1.65 billion euros, up from previous expectations for at least 1.55 billion euros.

This is the second consecutive quarter Ferrari has raised its 2026 guidance, despite vehicle shipments being lower by 318 units through the first nine months of the year, at 10,170 vehicles, compared with last year. The decline is led by fewer shipments to the Americas and China, Hong Kong and Taiwan.

Ferrari said Tuesday that the guidance improvements were a result of stronger-than-expected customer personalizations, lower than anticipated currency headwinds and a second place finish in the Formula 1 championship.

"Our order book now reaches into 2028, and we still have two more launches to come this year. In addition, we continue to create engaging events around the world to further tighten the bonds with our clients and partners across Racing, Sports Cars and Lifestyle," Ferrari CEO Benedetto Vigna said in a release.

RBC Capital Markets analyst Tom Narayan said Tuesday that Ferrari's results were largely in line with Wall Street expectations. However, he said shares may be under "under slight pressure given some were hoping for a bigger guidance raise, but expect it to get better once folks realize these results are healthy."

Ferrari's U.S. shares initially declined on the results before turning positive and were up roughly 2% during premarket trading.

Yum Brands earnings beat estimates, but cyclospora outbreak weighs on Taco Bell

📅 2026-11-03  |  Category: Business  |  Read Source →

Yum Brands on Tuesday reported earnings that beat expectations, but a cyclospora outbreak linked to iceberg lettuce at Taco Bell weighed on the company's same-store sales growth.

However, Yum said Taco Bell, which the company considers a "growth engine" for its business, has since rebounded.

"Since the end of September, Taco Bell U.S. has been outpacing the [quick-service restaurant] industry on same-store sales growth," Yum CEO Chris Turner said in a statement.

Taco Bell's same-store sales are up about 1% so far in the fourth quarter, CFO Ranjith Roy said on the company's earnings conference call Tuesday. In the last four weeks ended Friday, same-store sales have risen 3% compared with the year-ago period.

Shares of Yum rose more than 2% in premarket trading.

Here's what the company reported compared with what Wall Street was expecting, based on a survey of analysts by LSEG:

Yum reported fiscal third-quarter net income of $1.62 billion, or $5.92 per share, up from $397 million, or $1.41 per share, a year earlier.

Excluding gains from its divestiture of Pizza Hut and other items, the restaurant company earned $1.56 per share.

During the quarter, Yum completed its divestiture of Pizza Hut. It closed the sale of the pizza chain's business in mainland China to Yum China on Aug. 7 and sold the rest of Pizza Hut to LongRange Capital on Sept. 1. The company's third-quarter results include Pizza Hut up until those dates.

Excluding Pizza Hut, Yum's same-store sales were flat for the quarter as Taco Bell weighed on the company's overall results. Taco Bell's same-store sales declined by 4% as diners temporarily pulled back on visits due to the foodborne illness outbreak.

Yum executives previously said the weekend of July 18 saw the most severe impact to sales, but trends have improved since then.

Taco Bell's most loyal customers returned faster, Yum said, thanks in part to weekly Tuesday deals for loyalty program members after the cyclospora outbreak took over headlines.

Traffic from "medium" and "light" customers has bounced back to about 95% of prior levels, while visits from "heavy" users is back to normal, Roy said. To attract more casual diners, Taco Bell has been relying on star power, like a collaboration with pop star Olivia Rodrigo on a pink-colored Mountain Dew Baja Blast.

Outside of the U.S., Taco Bell's much smaller international business reported same-store sales growth of 5%.

KFC's same-store sales ticked up 1%, helped by system sales growth in China and Europe. The U.S., now the chicken chain's third-largest market, saw system sales shrink 6% in the quarter.

Like Restaurant Brands International's Popeyes, KFC has struggled in the U.S. amid a wave of new competitors and more budget-conscious consumers. Its home market represents only about 5% of KFC's operating profit, but it remains a symbolically important part of the business for Yum investors.

During the quarter, KFC U.S. launched popcorn chicken, which sold out in many restaurants. However, Yum said the limited-time menu item was not enough to offset the successful Hot & Spicy Wings and Potato Wedges promotion during the year-ago period.

Globally, KFC is trying to become "the most modern chicken QSR," Turner said on the company's earnings conference call. To achieve that lofty goal, he said the chain will roll out buzzy promotions, a new brand identity and a wider menu that includes upgraded sauces and more beverages.

📅 2026-11-03  |  Category: Business  |  Read Source →

Pfizer on Tuesday reported fiscal third-quarter results that topped estimates and hiked its full-year outlook, as demand for its newly launched and acquired products grows.

Pfizer also Tuesday announced that Cecile Guegan, who has held various finance leadership roles at the company over the last two decades, stepped in as chief financial officer this month. She had served as interim CFO since August, when Pfizer's former CFO, Dave Denton, stepped down to take the same position at Nike.

The company is now expecting full-year revenue to total $62 billion to $64 billion, which compares with a previous outlook of $60.5 billion to $62.5 billion. The low end of that sales range would be roughly flat or down slightly compared with 2025 revenue of $62.6 billion. Still, Pfizer cited its "strong year-to-date performance" as a driver for the guidance hike.

The pharmaceutical giant now expects full-year adjusted profit of between $2.95 and $3.05 per share. That compares with previous guidance of between $2.80 and $3 per share.

That includes a one-time $650 million charge, or a negative impact of 10 cents per share, recorded in the third quarter tied to the company's licensing and collaboration agreement with Chinese cancer drugmaker Innovent Biologics.

Pfizer is working on a comeback story after its massive pandemic-era success waned. As demand for Covid products drops and generic rivals threaten its biggest moneymakers, CEO Albert Bourla is looking to jump-start growth through strategic acquisitions, fresh cancer drugs and a big bet on weight loss treatments through its acquisition of Metsera.

Pfizer in August expanded its multiyear cost-cutting efforts to help offset sharp declines in Covid product sales, and is now targeting roughly $9.7 billion in net savings through 2029.

Here's what the company reported for the third quarter compared with what Wall Street was expecting, based on a survey of analysts by LSEG:

Tune in at 1:30 p.m. ET as Pfizer CEO Albert Bourla joins CNBC TV to discuss the company's earnings. Watch in real time on CNBC+ or the CNBC Pro stream.

Pfizer reported third-quarter revenue of $16.58 billion, relatively flat from the same period a year ago. The company cited an operational decrease of $57 million, primarily due to lower Covid revenues, and a $15 million negative impact from foreign exchange.

That was offset by strong sales of key products like its blood thinner Eliquis and targeted cancer drug Padcev, which jumped and beat expectations.

Eliquis in particular raked in $2.52 billion in sales, up 25%. Analysts were expecting revenue of $2.40 billion, according to StreetAccount. Padcev posted $720 million in revenue for the period, which compares with the $658 million that analysts were expecting.

Sales of Pfizer's newly launched and acquired products grew 32% operationally. That includes Padcev, its respiratory syncytial virus vaccine Abrysvo, its pneumococcal shot Prevnar 20 and oral lung cancer drug Lorbrena, among several other products.

Pfizer's Covid products also topped estimates for the quarter despite seeing steep sales declines. The company's Covid vaccine generated $808 million in sales, which compares with the $770.4 million that analysts were expecting, StreetAccount estimates show. Meanwhile, Pfizer's antiviral pill, Paxlovid, booked $449 million in sales, coming in above the $394.1 million that analysts were expecting.

The company booked net income of $3.24 billion, or 56 cents per share, for the period. That compares with net income of $3.54 billion, or 62 cents per share, during the third quarter of 2025.

Excluding certain items, including restructuring charges and costs associated with intangible assets, Pfizer posted earnings per share of 87 cents for the quarter.

<a href="https://www.fiercehealthcare.com/providers/measles-update-us-passes-4000-cases-pennsylvania-reports-sixth-death" hreflang="en">Measles update: New York reports first measles-associated death; US nears 5,000 confirmed cases</a>

📅 2026-11-03  |  Category: Business  |  Read Source →
Measles update: New York reports first measles-associated death; US nears 5,000 confirmed cases" style="max-width:100%; height:auto; border-radius:8px;" loading="lazy">

The New York State Department of Health on Monday afternoon reported its first measles-associated death in 30 years.

The state has counted 210 total cases this year as of Nov. 3. The reported death was a child in the northern Lewis County, according to the department. No information was given about the child's specific illness or the role measles infection may have played in their death.

Sixteen confirmed cases of measles have been reported in Lewis County as of Tuesday, the department said. Many of New York's cases are located among upstate counties, and just eight are in New York City, according to the department.

In a statement, New York Governor Kathy Hochul—who on Oct. 5 declared a Disaster Emergency in response to rising measles case rates—expressed her sympathies to the child's family and told residents that "vaccination remains our best protection against this dangerous disease."

"This is New York’s first measles-related death in 30 years, a devastating reminder of what’s at stake when Washington deliberately erodes decades of progress in protecting Americans from preventable illnesses," the statement reads, echoing criticisms of federal health leadership voiced by Pennsylvania Governor Josh Shapiro regarding measles-associated deaths in his own state.

As of Oct. 29, the Centers for Disease Control reports 4,852 confirmed cases of measles this year across the country, far outpacing 2025's total of 2,289. Of these, 511 have been hospitalized (versus 244 in 2025) and three deaths have been recognized—though health officials in Pennsylvania alone said they have confirmed five measles-associated deaths. The CDC has said its death count may change after it completes work to develop a new standardized case definition for "deaths due to measles."

A spokesperson for the Department of Health and Human Services, in a statement to Fierce Healthcare, said it had deployed its epidemiologists and other experts to New York after receiving a formal request for support. The support is currently scheduled to run through Nov. 13, and includes on-the-ground "surveillance, data analysis, strategic planning and other outbreak response support," they said.

Of the roughly $100 million of additional measles and vaccine-preventable diseases funding the department said it has provided via the CDC since August, nearly $2.4 million has gone directly to New York State and $2.1 million to New York City, they said.

"Secretary Kennedy and CDC Director Dr. Erica Schwartz continue to make clear that the MMR vaccine is the most effective way to prevent measles," they added.

The U.S. is in the midst of its worst measles outbreak in decades, with more than 4,000 cases reported to the Centers for Disease Control and Prevention as of early October and a sixth measles-associated death disclosed by local Pennsylvania officials on Monday.

The vaccine-preventable disease was declared eliminated in the U.S. back in 2000—meaning that there was no endemic spread of the disease—but has resurfaced in force during recent years as declining childhood vaccination rates have pushed most states below the 95% coverage bar for school-age herd immunity.

The CDC had counted 2,289 cases across the entirety of 2025, which had been the country’s highest total since 1990, and several public health officials have warned for months that number would likely be surpassed based on the scale and frequency of early-year cases.

Measles-associated deaths have also accompanied the higher case counts, though just how many is a subject of dispute. While three deaths were confirmed in all of 2025, this year logged its first in late August with Pennsylvania, which hosts more than a quarter of the total cases, now reporting six measles-associated deaths as of Oct. 13. The CDC, however, lists two deaths alongside a note that its count is subject to change, and has promised more broadly to develop a new, standardized case definition to be used across all reporting states.

That discrepancy is an example of friction between federal health leadership and the broader public health community that experts say has contributed to this year’s outbreaks.

Pennsylvania’s initial death announcements in August were contested by Health and Human Services Secretary Robert F. Kennedy Jr., who accused Pennsylvania Governor Josh Shapiro of withholding sufficient information for the CDC to confirm the deaths and using his press conference announcing them as “gaslighting the public” for political purposes.

During an ensuing social media feud, the governor and the secretary described a heated phone call in which Shapiro (at the time) rejected federal assistance and accused Kennedy of fueling the outbreaks with only lukewarm endorsement of the MMR vaccine and a broader concurrent campaign to scale back other routine childhood vaccinations.

Numerous state public health officials and experts have lobbed similar criticisms of Kennedy, who is affiliated with anti-vaccine groups, and federal health agencies under his purview.

The CDC, for its part, has reportedly infused at least $100 million of extra public health funding to states for immunization programs and surveillance, and leading officials including Kennedy and new CDC Director Erica Schwartz, M.D., have described the MMR vaccine as safe and effective when asked directly. Still, the agency has not launched the type of broader vaccine messaging and awareness campaigns that it would have in years past, critics are quick to note.

Pennsylvania isn’t alone in raising alarms over rising measles cases. New York Governor Kathy Hochul declared a State Disaster Emergency in early October, the first such declaration in recent years.

New Jersey Drops Coinbase Staking Order After Three Years

📅 Nov 03, 2026  |  Category: Business  |  Read Source →

New Jersey’s securities regulator has rescinded its 2023 cease-and-desist order and $5 million penalty against Coinbase’s staking service.

New Jersey’s securities regulator has withdrawn the 2023 cease-and-desist order that barred Coinbase from offering staking to new customers in the state, ending a three-year standoff that cost residents an estimated $15 million in forgone rewards.

The Bureau of Securities notice, dated Oct. 30 and signed by Acting Bureau Chief Keith A. Alt, rescinds the order and the $5 million penalty that came with it. The withdrawal is without prejudice and without a ruling on the merits.

Coinbase disclosed the development in its third-quarter report filed with the Securities and Exchange Commission, which states that the regulator informed the company of its intent to rescind on Oct. 29.

Coinbase CEO Brian Armstrong said New Jersey users would regain access to staking “very soon.”

As of Monday, the exchange’s eligibility page continued to list New Jersey among the states where customers cannot stake new principal, and no reopening date has been announced.

The order traces to June 6, 2023, when the New Jersey bureau joined securities regulators in nine other states — Alabama, California, Illinois, Kentucky, Maryland, South Carolina, Vermont, Washington and Wisconsin — in a coordinated action against Coinbase’s staking program.

The states alleged the service amounted to an unregistered securities offering under state law.

California led the task force; its action covered more than 600,000 accounts. In July 2023, Coinbase agreed with California, New Jersey, South Carolina and Wisconsin to block customers in those states from staking new funds pending final adjudication. Maryland reached a similar agreement in October 2023.

Customers who had staked before the orders kept earning rewards, but could not add principal or compound the rewards they received.

The state cases ran parallel to the SEC’s June 2023 lawsuit against Coinbase, which named staking among its claims. The SEC dismissed that case in February 2025. Alabama, Kentucky, Illinois, South Carolina and Vermont dismissed, vacated or withdrew their own actions in March and April 2025. New Jersey is the sixth of the original ten states to step back.

Rescinding the order ends the restriction on new staking in New Jersey. It does not resolve the underlying legal question. The bureau made no finding on whether Coinbase’s staking program is a security, and the “without prejudice” language preserves its ability to file a new action. Coinbase’s own filing calls the state matters “preliminary” and says an adverse resolution “could have a material impact” on the business.

The filing describes a planned rescission rather than a completed one, which explains the gap between Armstrong’s statement and the unchanged eligibility page. The change for New Jersey residents arrives when Coinbase updates its systems, not when the notice was signed.

Coinbase has framed the state restrictions as a competitive disadvantage. In an April 2025 post, the company’s Ryan VanGrack argued the orders singled out one provider while residents could stake through other platforms. Coinbase put forgone rewards across the four restricted states at more than $130 million as of October 2025.

Four states remain. California, Maryland and Wisconsin continue to bar new staking on Coinbase while their cases proceed; Washington permits staking but is pursuing a $4 million fine. Coinbase estimates California residents have missed more than $89 million in rewards since 2023.

The decision lands as federal policy has shifted but not settled. The SEC has withdrawn from the registration-based cases it brought in 2023, and the CLARITY market structure bill passed the House in 2025 but stalled in the Senate. State securities regulators are not bound by the SEC’s enforcement priorities, which is why the staking cases outlived the federal one by more than a year and a half.

A future SEC, or a future bureau chief in Trenton, could revisit the question.

Watch live, catch up on episodes, and get exclusive content on the go.

Bitcoin Magazine is published by BTC Inc., a subsidiary of Nakamoto Inc. (NASDAQ: NAKA).

<a href="https://www.fiercehealthcare.com/providers/samaritan-health-services-join-multicare-health-system" hreflang="en">Samaritan Health Services, MultiCare Health System close merger</a>

📅 2026-11-03  |  Category: Business  |  Read Source →
Samaritan Health Services, MultiCare Health System close merger" style="max-width:100%; height:auto; border-radius:8px;" loading="lazy">

After months of securing the necessary regulatory approvals, Samaritan Health Services officially joined up with MultiCare Health System at the top of November to create an 18-hospital, $7.5 billion nonprofit health system.

The Northwestern organizations' deal, announced last year, directs $700 million from MultiCare into care services, workforce development, facility improvements and technology investments across Samaritan facilities over the coming decade. Those investments and other "longer-term" decisions will be informed by local community needs and perspectives, with decisions headed by "a locally led regional strategic planning process," they said in this week's announcement.

“MultiCare brings expertise and resources that will help us meet the changing healthcare needs of our region and support our clinicians and employees in their work every day," Marty Cahill, Samaritan's president and CEO, who has also taken on the roles of senior vice president and chief executive of MultiCare's Oregon Coast to Cascades Region, said in the announcement.

Samaritan comprises five hospitals in western Oregon, and will also remain locally led under its new parent organization, officials have said throughout the merger process. Its addition represented a geographic market expansion for the 13-hospital MultiCare.

Samaritan Health Services and MultiCare Health System have cleared all the regulatory bars necessary to complete their merger, which will cross the finish line on Nov. 1.

The nonprofits' affiliation would create an 18-hospital system with more than $7.5 billion in revenue—and give MultiCare an acute-care foothold in Oregon. Announced in 2025, it also includes a $700 million, 10-year commitment from MultiCare to invest in Samaritan's facilities, workforce and care access points.

On Monday, the pair said that all public agencies that were reviewing their deal "have either approved the transaction or announced their intent to do so." Some of these, such as the approval from the Oregon Health Authority (see below), would bring additional conditions on areas such as the reinvestment of operating margins into local care.

“We look forward to investing in the future of healthcare across Samaritan’s service area and advancing our vision to be the Pacific Northwest’s highest-value system of health,” Bill Robertson, CEO of MultiCare, said in a statement. “Together, we can build on the strengths of both organizations and invest in the future of healthcare for the people who depend on us.”

A phased integration process will begin on Nov. 1, with the organizations reassuring patients and health plan members that the process wouldn't interrupt services or coverage. Samaritan is also slated to retain its name, local leadership and community-based governance, they reaffirmed.

The Oregon Health Authority (OHA) approved a deal on Monday that would merge the nonprofits MultiCare Health System and Samaritan Health Services.

State regulators said the OHA's Health Care Market Oversight (HCMO) program reviewed how the proposed merger would affect Samaritan's facilities and its impact on Oregon citizens' access to affordable and equitable healthcare.

HCMO approved the deal provided it meets established conditions (PDF), including requiring MultiCare to follow its financial commitment of investing $700 million towards Samaritan and posting a public-facing version of the commitment on Samaritan's website. Other conditions involve reinvestment of Samaritan's operating margins into local communities and a requirement to maintain existing services.

The proposal to form a $7.5 billion, 18-hospital system still has to face review from other state programs prior to moving forward.

OHA's Coordinated Care Organization (CCO) Form A review is analyzing the deal's potential impact on InterCommunity Health Network's financial stability and ability to serve state Medicaid recipients. Moreover, the state Department of Consumer and Business Services (DCBS) is reviewing the financial and overall impact of changing control of Samaritan commercial health plans.

Samaritan President and CEO Marty Cahill said in a statement that the approval is "an important step forward for the communities we serve."

"Our affiliation with MultiCare will provide additional resources to invest in our workforce, facilities, technology and services—and help make it easier for people to receive high-quality care close to home," Cahill said.

The nonprofits will continue to operate as independent organizations until the transaction closes, and executives say they are working toward an Oct. 1 closing.

The boards of nonprofits MultiCare Health System and Samaritan Health Services have approved an affiliation they expect to consummate in mid-2026.

The arrangement would bring Samaritan, which runs five community hospitals as well as health plans and clinics in Oregon’s Benton, Lincoln and Linn counties, under the 13-hospital MultiCare—a new geographic foothold for the Tacoma, Washington-based organization.

“MultiCare and Samaritan are closely aligned in our mission, vision and values, making us ideal partners as we strengthen our networks across our service regions,” MultiCare CEO Bill Robertson said in the announcement. “It will be our privilege to be a part of the communities Samaritan serves, and we look forward to working together to support and advance health care in Oregon.”

The systems said there are no immediate changes for Samaritans’ patients, plan members or employees as the two sides move through the affiliation process.

Once the deal is finalized, Samaritan will remain “locally governed and community-based” but receive new resources from MultiCare that will expand services, update its operations and ensure sustainable care in its Oregon communities, according to the announcement.

More specifically, the deal includes a 10-year investment commitment focused on modernizing Samaritan’s facilities, expanding the flagship Good Samaritan Regional Medical Center’s inpatient capacity, adopting new technologies including telehealth capabilities and growing primary and specialty care. MultiCare’s behavioral health will also expand into Samaritan’s service area along the mid-Willamette Valley and central coast.

These changes will together increase care quality and access while helping to lower the cost of care, the organizations said.

“This affiliation represents an important step in securing the future of nonprofit health care in our region,” Marty Cahill, president and CEO of Samaritan, said in the announcement. “Our commitment has always been to remain a community-based, mission-driven organization, and this partnership strengthens that promise for decades to come. […] We are excited and honored to become MultiCare’s first health system in Oregon and to bring the benefits of MultiCare’s expertise home to the communities we serve.”

The parties said they will sign a formal affiliation agreement “in the coming weeks.”

MultiCare Health System employs more than 28,000 people across its 13 hospitals. It has 300-plus care locations in Washington, Idaho and Oregon, though no hospitals in the latter. The system reported $5.9 billion of total operating revenue but a $116.5 million operating loss (-2% operating margin) in 2024, a nearly $70 million improvement over the prior year.

Samaritan Health Services operates more than 80 physician clinics and multiple health insurance plans, serving 110,000 people, as well as its five community hospitals. In 2024, the nonprofit reported more than $1.7 billion of revenue but a $93.6 million operating loss (-0.5% operating loss).
